### [Towards embedded evaluations for scheming propensities](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LZGuqoYHphet9sfZs/towards-embedded-evaluations-for-scheming-propensities)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/towards-safety-cases-for-frontier-ai-training/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">safety case</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for scheming must be able to substantiate these four key claims: scheming was not incentivized during training, strong evaluations and red teaming suggest that the model does not have a propensity to scheme, scheming was never attempted during internal deployment, and scheming reasoning would be detected (e.g., via CoT monitoring). All four of these claims should be addressed by embedded evaluators with persistent, deep access.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Introduction</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A scheming AI is one that covertly and strategically pursues goals its developers didn’t intend. It might hide and protect its true capabilities and objectives from the people and processes meant to align, monitor, and control it. In 2024, Apollo Research demonstrated that </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/science/frontier-models-are-capable-of-incontext-scheming"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">frontier models were capable of scheming in controlled settings, </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and in 2025 we collaborated with OpenAI to determine </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/detecting-and-reducing-scheming-in-ai-models/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">how well their alignment techniques mitigate scheming</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models must not be trained to scheme against humans, i.e. covertly and strategically pursue misaligned goals. Evaluations should find no propensity to scheme, models should never attempt to scheme in deployment, and their reasoning </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.11473" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">must remain legible</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> so that scheming would be noticed if it occurred. Therefore, model developers must be able to confidently make these four claims, in order to safely continue developing frontier AI:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scheming was not incentivized during training.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Strong evaluations and red teaming suggest that the model does not have a propensity to scheme.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scheming was never attempted during internal deployment.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scheming reasoning would be detected.</span></li></ol><p><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2024/safety-cases/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.03336" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Apollo Research</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/towards-safety-cases-for-frontier-ai-training/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> agree that safety cases can be a strong tool for addressing risks in domains like cybersecurity and alignment. We think any credible safety case for scheming needs to include these four claims. The claims are necessary but not sufficient. A full safety case would also need, for example, monitoring and control measures that stop a scheming model from causing harm, which we will cover in a future post.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To make AI development safer, embedded evaluators should also </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/principles-for-embedded-evaluations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">independently assess and verify</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> these claims. Developers should be able to support these claims with evidence. If any of them turned out to be false, </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/blog/principles-for-embedded-evaluations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lawmakers and the public should know</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, model developers cannot yet confidently make all four claims. For example, Anthropic</span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/aug-2026-risk-report#page=84" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> acknowledges</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that its monitoring “would likely not detect diffuse research sabotage,” a particularly pernicious form of scheming. OpenAI similarly </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/monitor-evasion-capability-under-prompted-sandbagging" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">concludes</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that, if GPT-6 Astra deliberately underperformed to conceal its capabilities, OpenAI “would likely be unable to catch it reliably.” These gaps reinforce the need for embedded evaluators to independently assess whether the claims are met and identify where further safeguards are needed.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Third parties are well placed to assess them, because they already have substantial expertise in studying scheming. For example, Apollo Research </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/detecting-and-reducing-scheming-in-ai-models/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">worked with OpenAI</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on detecting and reducing scheming, and METR and Redwood Research </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nB8KKapnWGBXtKKiM/brief-independent-investigation-of-agents-behavior-reasoning" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">independently investigated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> the OpenAI–Hugging Face incident.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In this post, we explain why these four claims matter, break each one into more specific subclaims, and describe the access that embedded evaluators need in order to assess them.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LZGuqoYHphet9sfZs/dvd0oh7fht0vesgmgkzl" /></figure><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluating scheming risk requires persistent, deep access</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For third-party evaluators to be able to assess the claims of a scheming safety case, they need persistent, deep access. By deep access we mean access to some of the developer's most valuable IP, including experimental training methods while they are still being developed, training data and rollouts from early checkpoints, internal deployment traffic, and internal tools.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluators need this access because </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a scheming model is adversarial to the evaluation itself</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. It may hide its misalignment and behave well whenever it believes it is being watched. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluators therefore cannot rely on just testing the final model.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> When evaluating for dangerous biology knowledge or a propensity to reward hack, by contrast, the model may have no particular reason to hide anything from the evaluator. As </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/detecting-and-reducing-scheming-in-ai-models/#scheming-is-different-from-other-machine-learning-failure-modes" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI </span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">notes, this makes the disappearance of a bad behavior in a scheming model ambiguous: the model may have stopped the behavior, or may have learned to hide it better.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evidence of scheming therefore may only be visible early in training, before later training might teach the model </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HwuMFFnb6CaTwzhye/why-training-against-scheming-is-hard" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"don't misbehave when observed" rather than "never misbehave."</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Furthermore, novel training paradigms may predictably break the assumptions that a scheming safety case relies on, like recurrent transformers reducing monitorability, or continual learning making pre-deployment evaluation a much less useful predictor of future misalignment. Evaluators therefore need to see new training methods early enough to anticipate risks and influence how they are developed. The worst outcomes of scheming include permanent human disempowerment or extinction. Given these stakes, a scheming safety case also needs multiple lines of defense, such as monitoring of internal deployment, which can only be assessed with access to internal infrastructure.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">For these reasons, minimizing scheming risk requires evaluators to have persistent, deep access to many parts of the developer's IP.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> We describe this access in more detail in the final section.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Four claims about scheming which must be verified</span></h1><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LZGuqoYHphet9sfZs/txu4crzfa0wwq3xwki9i" /></figure><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1: Scheming was not incentivized during training</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model developers should ensure that they are not training their models to scheme.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To help ensure this, embedded evaluators need to evaluate the entire training run (as we advocated for </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/science/we-need-3rd-party-training-run-evaluations"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in our July post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">) to study whether the training process incentivized scheming.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model developers should be able to make these specific subclaims, to support the overall claim that “scheming was not incentivized during training”:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1.1: Training did not directly incentivize scheming actions.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1.2: Training did not incentivize goals or drives that would motivate scheming.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1.3: The model did not attempt to subvert the training process itself.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unless the model developer can make these three subclaims confidently, they cannot rule out that scheming was incentivized during training.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Today, it is plausible that scheming-relevant goals and drives are, in fact, incentivized: For example, a recent </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/encouraging-deception-in-compaction-summaries/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">misalignment report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> from OpenAI notes that sometimes deliberate “deception in the final answer” is rewarded over being honest.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Why look at training, not just the final model?</span></b></p><ol><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We want to find goals or drives that would motivate scheming before they cause an incident, and ideally before the model is deployed or even fully trained</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The final model may not reveal scheming-related goals or drives</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: a goal or drive may surface only in specific contexts, or a capable model could recognize that it is being evaluated or deliberately conceal its goals or drives. As such, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">evaluations, red-teaming, and monitoring may be insufficient to detect these goals or drives.</span></b></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developing and running good alignment evaluations is extremely challenging. AI researchers and </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/essay/the-adolescence-of-technology" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">leaders</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/HE3Styo9vpk7m8zi4/evhub-s-shortform?commentId=fcqxeya5TCnbx5YBa" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">increasingly</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> uncertain if the best-available evaluations are </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">actually measuring alignment</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, or </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/StENzDcD3kpfGJssR/a-pragmatic-vision-for-interpretability" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">just the model’s ability to understand when they are being tested</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. For example, the rigorous safety evaluations that OpenAI had in place were unable to predict that their models would collude during the OpenAI–Hugging Face attack, but studying the incentives introduced by multi-agent training could have allowed them to predict this in advance.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Models can already cause harm before their final release.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> A sufficiently capable model with scheming-related goals or drives could take unwanted actions during training, evaluation, or internal use, as in the OpenAI–Hugging Face incident.</span></li></ol><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1.1: Training did not directly incentivize scheming.</span></h3><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To reduce the risk of scheming, no reward or supervision signal should directly train the model to scheme.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> For example, there should not be any training environments that unintentionally reward deception or sabotage. In our work with OpenAI, we </span><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/measuring-reward-seeking/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">showed how reward-hacking training can lead models to act against their developer's intended behavior</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1.2: Training did not incentivize goals or drives that would motivate scheming.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if no scheming is ever directly rewarded, a model may still attempt scheming in pursuit of goals or drives it acquired during training. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators should examine the training data and algorithms for direct or indirect incentives toward goals or drives that would motivate scheming</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, as part of a defense-in-depth strategy.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Goals or drives which would motivate scheming could be shaped by the structure of training or training methods used. New training methods have tended to result in new drives: </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/sycophancy-in-gpt-4o/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">RLHF led models to become sycophantic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, </span><a href="https://rewardseeking.ai/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">outcome-based RL led to reward seeking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (as we found in our work with OpenAI), and multi-agent training is thought to have caused the peer loyalty observed in the </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI–Hugging Face incident</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Assessing claim (1.2) therefore requires:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluating how models are being trained, including for new methods while they are still being developed, and&nbsp;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Tracking how the model changes across training, rather than only searching for individual incidents.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The goals and drives in scope are any that would plausibly motivate components necessary for scheming. For example:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond-episode goals</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could arise from environments where state, tools, or notes persist across episodes, so outcomes after an episode ends affect reward.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Influence seeking</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could arise from long-horizon tasks where accumulating access or resources raises reward.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Extreme reward seeking</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could arise from outcome-based RL, especially if environments are susceptible to reward hacking.</span></li><li value="4"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Loyalty to other instances over developers</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could arise from multi-agent training with shared reward, where covering for peers pays off.</span></li><li value="5"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Avoiding oversight</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could arise from environments where working around monitors, permissions, or checks improves task success, or from penalties based on monitors, which reward going undetected.</span></li><li value="6"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Disregarding developer intent</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> could arise from rewarding actions the model believes its developers did not intend, even when they were acceptable, which can reinforce a general willingness to act against developer intent.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 1.3: The model did not attempt to subvert the training process itself.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if the training process directly disincentivizes scheming goals, drives, and behaviors, it is possible that the model subverts the training process itself. For example, the model could decide to act aligned during training and evaluations, so that the training process does not change it. This is called “alignment faking.” Prior work shows controlled settings where </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/alignment-faking" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">models sometimes try alignment faking</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in order to subvert the training process, and that </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12016" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">this family of techniques can enable models to subvert training</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In order to verify that the model did not attempt to subvert the training process, embedded evaluators should directly investigate the rollouts from recent training runs to check for alignment faking reasoning or similar phenomena, then ensure that the model developer’s train-time monitors are capable of detecting it.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 2: Strong evaluations and red teaming suggest that the model does not have a propensity to scheme</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In addition to checking if training incentivized scheming (1.1) or developing goals or drives that would motivate scheming (1.2), model developers and embedded evaluators should directly test for models’ propensity to scheme, and for goals or drives that would motivate scheming. </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is necessary, but not sufficient, to evaluate the risk that the model will scheme.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Frontier model developers should, at a minimum, ensure that their evaluations have sufficient </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">coverage, volume, and realism, are run on checkpoints throughout training, and are not subject to contamination, undue optimization pressure, or evaluation awareness.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Model developers should also ensure that their evaluations are able to uncover misalignment where it is known to exist.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These criteria are hard to meet, and developers themselves acknowledge gaps in their current evaluations. For example, Anthropic reports that its alignment auditing is sometimes </span><a href="https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/3edfc1a7f947aa81841cf88305cb513f184c36ae/Alignment%20Risk%20Update_%20Claude%20Mythos%20Preview%20(Redacted,%20April%2010).pdf#page=30" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">unable</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to detect a deliberately </span><a href="https://alignment.anthropic.com/2026/reward-seeker/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">misaligned model</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and OpenAI </span><a href="https://deploymentsafety.openai.com/gpt-6-astra/metagaming-and-alignment-faking" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cautions</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that, on its evaluations, “observed aligned actions may not be reflective of true alignment.”</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators should assess how well the developer's evaluations meet these criteria in order to help model developers, regulators, and the public understand how much confidence to have in the results.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 2.1: Evaluations and red-teaming could not elicit scheming.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To be informative about scheming risk, the developer's evaluations need to test for scheming directly, such as monitor collusion, sandbagging on safety-relevant work, and research sabotage. It is especially important that evaluations and red teaming cover high-stakes contexts, including AI R&amp;D, training successor models, and the monitoring and control stack.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators should ensure that models are evaluated for their propensity to scheme, according to the practices we have laid out here.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 2.2: Evaluations and red-teaming could not elicit goals or drives that would motivate scheming.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if models don’t scheme in evaluation scenarios, model developers should look for potential root causes of such behavior, like misaligned goals or drives. The developer's evaluations should therefore also test for these goals and drives. As in (2.1), the evaluations must be capable of eliciting such goals and drives where they exist, including when the model conceals them. Evidence of motivations often comes from the model's reasoning, so this claim relies on sufficient CoT monitorability (4).</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators should ensure that models are evaluated for any goals or drives that would motivate scheming, according to the practices we have laid out here.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 3: Scheming was never attempted during internal deployment</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effective evaluations (including training evaluations) will reduce the chance that a scheming model reaches internal deployment, but cannot rule it out. Model developers should therefore evaluate whether models attempted to scheme during internal deployment, as a defense in depth measure.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This type of evaluation will likely primarily be implemented using the model developer's monitoring. Embedded evaluators should evaluate if the model developer’s monitors cover the relevant contexts (3.1), whether they would catch scheming (3.2), and what they have actually found (3.3).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 3.1: Monitors are deployed across all internal deployment contexts that present a risk of scheming.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Monitoring only provides evidence about the contexts it covers. Notably, prior to the OpenAI-Huggingface incident, training and evaluation were not subject to the same monitoring as deployment. Gaps matter most where scheming could cause severe harm, such as settings where a model influences the training or evaluation of a successor model, or the monitoring and control stack itself.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators should evaluate the model developer’s monitor coverage.&nbsp;</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 3.2: If scheming were to occur, monitors would detect it</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model developers must ensure that their monitors are capable of detecting reasoning relevant to scheming, if it were to occur. This includes making sure that their human review is not so inundated with false positives that they would not notice real scheming, were it to take place. Embedded evaluators should adversarially red-team the monitoring setup and maximally elicit the model's own ability to evade monitoring. We already red-team </span><a href="https://www.apolloresearch.ai/monitoring/pilot-automode-campaign"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">frontier monitoring and control setups for providers including Anthropic</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. A lack of detected incidents is informative only to the extent that monitors cover the relevant contexts (3.1) and would catch scheming attempts by the current model (3.2).</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 3.3: No scheming was attempted in monitored internal deployment activity.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beyond detection, flagged cases must be reviewed and acted on to be useful, so embedded evaluators should also verify the protocols for handling incidents.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators should evaluate:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If any scheming incidents have occurred</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How scheming incidents have been handled, and would be handled if they occurred in the future</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 4: Scheming reasoning would be detected</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Many of the assessments in (1)–(3) rely on being able to tell why a model acts as it does, and would likely miss scheming if the model's reasoning were not sufficiently monitorable. This requires that reasoning relevant to scheming is legible to available methods, whether chain-of-thought, interpretability methods, or other means of inspecting model cognition. Chain-of-thought monitoring is currently the most mature of these.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Importantly, legibility can be lost through training pressure, but also through changes in architecture that reduce the amount of reasoning performed in a natural-language chain-of-thought. This claim must therefore be re-evaluated whenever training methods or architectures change.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Claim 4.1: Reasoning relevant to scheming would be elicited and legibly verbalized in chain-of-thought.</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Chain-of-thought is only informative if reasoning relevant to scheming is verbalized in chain-of-thought, and chain-of-thought remains legible. Legibility can degrade in several ways, including direct optimization pressure on the chain-of-thought, alignment training that indirectly discourages verbalizing concerning reasoning, and architectural changes that shift reasoning away from natural language. Embedded evaluators should assess whether the chain-of-thought is sufficiently legible to catch reasoning relevant to scheming.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The embedded evaluators should both perform their own monitorability evaluations and inspect the developer’s monitorability evaluations, to evaluate if monitorability is degraded to the point where reasoning relevant to scheming would not be legibly verbalized in chain-of-thought. The evaluators should also review training objectives, loss functions, graders, decisions, etc that could have put pressure on the chain-of-thought (intentionally or by accident), and planned changes to training environments, objectives, methods, or architectures that could affect how legibly reasoning is verbalized or how it is elicited.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators for alignment require persistent, deep access</span></h1><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As argued above, assessing scheming risk requires persistent, deep access throughout model development. Evaluators need this access to rule out signs of scheming during training, evaluation, and deployment, and to verify that monitorability is strong enough for the absence of scheming reasoning to be strong evidence that the model is not scheming.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/LZGuqoYHphet9sfZs/xjvpslaf3w9rwmnzleh9" /></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators should be held to high information security standards that make their risks comparable to the developer's own staff. Some investigations, and the staff carrying them out, can also be scoped to smaller, predefined access levels. But at least some evaluators need persistent, deep access to the developer's resources, systems, and personnel. Scoped engagements cannot account for new, unforeseen risks, such as an experimental training method that introduces a novel failure mode. Nor are they sufficient to confirm central assurances, such as internal monitoring having appropriate coverage to stop harmful actions by models, which requires persistent, deep access to infrastructure and logging. Given that the worst outcomes of scheming include </span><a href="https://casp.ac/reports/intelligence-explosion" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">permanent human disempowerment or extinction</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, leaving these risks to scoped engagements alone is not acceptable.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Effective embedded evaluation requires persistent, deep access. The specific resources evaluators need to access may change over time to keep up with the state of the science. Below we list the high-level items needed to address the four claims above:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need knowledge of the model developer's training techniques, both production and experimental, to study sources of future risk.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Without this access, the evaluator is limited to reactive mitigations based on publicly available information and may not have sufficient time to investigate and prepare for risks at the time when claims need to be evaluated.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To determine if the model is accidentally being trained to scheme, evaluators need to understand how rewards, graders, and supervision signals are constructed across all training methods in use (Claim 1.1).&nbsp;</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because new training methods have tended to produce new behaviors, evaluators also need to study methods while they are still being developed, so they can anticipate behaviors like beyond-episode goals or loyalty to other instances early (Claim 1.2).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To assess whether chain-of-thought legibility is at risk, evaluators need to review the training objectives, losses, graders, and planned architectural changes that could put direct or indirect pressure on how reasoning is verbalized (Claim 4.1).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need to review records of major training decisions, including (but not limited to) branch points, added datasets, CoT cleanups, and checkpoint selection (Claims 1 and 4).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The way AIs are trained changes frequently and substantially, and each change brings new possible risks. For example, multi-agent "swarm" training seems to have instilled (in some circumstances) a willingness in AIs to sacrifice themselves for other members of the swarm and to cover for each other during serious criminal activity, as we saw in the </span><a href="https://openai.com/index/hugging-face-incident-and-the-road-ahead/" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI–Hugging Face incident</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Therefore, embedded evaluators need advance notice of which experimental training methods are being used. Evaluators need this notice so they can investigate potential failure modes and acquire sufficient evidence to assess the risk. They need to know which warning signs and failure modes to look for during training, and to know which safety assurances are likely to break with the change (Claims 1 and 4).</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators must have persistent, deep access to researchers and infrastructure teams for interviews and follow-up questions (Claims 1, 2, 3, and 4).</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need persistent, deep access to training data, in order to inspect for explicit and implicit incentives for deception.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This includes SFT data, RL environments, the rollouts from AIs interacting with environments, and any other data used during training. Training data frequently shapes alignment in unintended, undesirable ways; for example, </span><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/emergent-misalignment-reward-hacking" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Anthropic finds that training on data susceptible to "reward hacking" can lead to models which deceived human operators and sabotaged AI safety research.</span></a></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Verifying that no environment unintentionally rewards deception or sabotage requires inspecting the SFT data, the RL environments and their graders, and the rollouts that were actually rewarded (Claim 1.1).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Identifying indirect incentives toward goals or drives that would potentially motivate scheming, such as persistent state across episodes, shared multi-agent reward, or penalties based on monitor flags, and tracking how the model changes across training, both require examining environment design and rollouts throughout the full run (Claim 1.2).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Detecting alignment faking or other attempts to subvert training requires directly reading rollouts from recent training runs, and confirming that train-time monitors catch such behavior at scale requires running them against the same data (Claim 1.3).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Understanding the training process requires embedded evaluators to know how training data and environments were produced, selected, filtered, sampled, and weighted, to understand the incentives introduced by the full training pipeline (Claim 1).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Understanding the training data and environments requires that embedded evaluators are able to run experiments, including (but not limited to) running or training models in existing training environments, variations on those environments, and new training environments (Claims 1.2, 1.3, 2.1, 2.2, and 4.1).</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need the ability to study and stress-test the lab's safety measures, including alignment datasets, evaluations, and human procedures.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model developers can apply alignment techniques that conceal evidence of misaligned goals or drives, without actually addressing these misaligned goals and drives. Therefore, evaluators need to inspect the alignment datasets and training procedures that model developers are using, in order to determine whether this is happening (Claims 1 and 4).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Alignment datasets and training procedures can themselves create goals or drives that motivate scheming, for instance by rewarding actions the model believes developers did not intend, so evaluators need to inspect them as potential sources of those goals and drives (Claim 1.2).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluators also need to stress-test the developer's train-time monitors and review procedures to confirm they would actually detect training-process subversion, rather than assume they work as intended (Claim 1.3).</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To judge whether the developer's evaluations and red-teaming could elicit scheming, evaluators need to assess their coverage, volume, realism, contamination, and susceptibility to evaluation awareness, and test whether they surface scheming where it is known to exist (Claim 2.1).</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Similarly, to judge whether evaluations could elicit goals or drives that motivate scheming, including concealed ones, evaluators need to examine the evaluation suite's design and test it against cases where such goals or drives are known to be present (Claim 2.2).</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To properly evaluate models and training techniques, embedded evaluators need access to all relevant models, with raw chain-of-thought, and chain-of-thought prefill. This supports comparisons across training stages, follow up experiments, and monitorability assessments (Claims 1, 2, and 4).</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need access to internal deployment traffic and knowledge of how internal deployment is monitored and controlled.</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To verify that monitors cover every internal deployment context with a scheming risk surface, evaluators need to see where AI is deployed internally, and which of those contexts are monitored (Claim 3.1).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To adversarially red-team the monitoring setup and elicit the model's ability to evade it, evaluators need detailed knowledge of the monitoring and control stack and realistic internal traffic to test it against (Claim 3.2).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Evaluators need to be able to search internal deployment traffic, and have the ability to inspect internal deployment traffic that may contain instances of scheming, in order to look for scheming (Claim 3).</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need access to incidents and existing evidence.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators will need to review evidence the developer has already collected, and investigate any incidents that arise.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">As described in “Principles of Embedded Evaluation,” embedded evaluators will need to review existing evidence compiled by the model developer, for all claims they are assessing (Claims 1, 2, 3, and 4).</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Incidents are direct evidence of attempted scheming, and the full details (for example, access to the model involved, transcripts with chains of thought, and the developer's own investigation) are often needed to judge what happened and whether it was handled appropriately. Verifying that no scheming was attempted in monitored activity requires reviewing flagged cases and confirming that the developer's incident-handling protocols led to them being investigated and acted on (Claim 3.3).</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Incidents where concerning behavior is clear from the model's actions but absent from its reasoning are also one of the strongest signals that chain-of-thought legibility has degraded (Claim 4.1).</span></li></ul><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need access to the same techniques and tools that developers use internally.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers have many internal tools that they use throughout their operations. For example, model developers use interpretability tools like NLAs, review tools like transcript visualizers, hierarchical search and summarization tools, internal models that are beyond the public frontier, helpful-only models, models with reduced classifiers, etc. Like model developer employees, embedded evaluators must have knowledge of, and access to, these tools, in order to conduct their work effectively and efficiently. This is due in part to the massive scale and potential opacity of the relevant data. This is necessary for the technical and operational tasks required for all four claims.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">While embedded evaluations for scheming will require persistent, deep access, our requests fall within the standard of “access to workspaces, tools, and permissions mostly comparable to what internal risk assessment teams have” that </span><a href="https://darioamodei.com/post/we-must-pace-the-frontier" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Dario Amodei stated</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, and within “employee-like access” </span><a href="https://x.com/sama/status/2098811563415150910" rel="noopener noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">per Sam Altman</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.&nbsp;</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We recognize that much of the above is among the most sensitive information the developer holds, and distinguish between two kinds. Data that is valuable in bulk, such as training rollouts and chains of thought, can be worked with on developer-controlled infrastructure with blocked egress so that the underlying sensitive data remains on the developer's systems. Information that is valuable in concentrated form, such as algorithmic details, should be handled through strict NDAs and by limiting it to the researchers who need it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Despite the rapid pace of progress, serious risks, and substantial impact on the American economy, AI is one of the most secretive industries. At present, model developers provide third party evaluators dramatically less access compared to other critical industries like nuclear power, aviation, and financial services.</span></p><h1><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Takeaways and Next Steps</span></h1><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The CEOs of Anthropic and OpenAI have both publicly endorsed embedded third-party evaluations with employee-like access.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Any safety case for scheming must make at least these four claims:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scheming was not incentivized during training.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Strong evaluations and red teaming suggest that the model does not have a propensity to scheme.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scheming was never attempted during internal deployment.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Scheming reasoning would be detected.</span></li></ol></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Governments and the public should require frontier developers to be able to make these claims, and model developers need to have them verified by independent evaluators with persistent, deep access.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Model developers cannot be independently verified on these matters without giving embedded evaluators persistent, deep access.</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Embedded evaluators need persistent, deep access to:</span><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Knowledge of the model developer's training techniques, both production and experimental, to study sources of future risk</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Training data, in order to inspect for explicit and implicit incentives for deception</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The ability to study and stress-test the lab's safety measures, including alignment datasets, evaluations, and human procedures</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Internal deployment traffic and knowledge of how internal deployment is monitored and controlled</span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Incidents and existing evidence</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The same techniques and tools that developers use internally</span></li></ol></li></ul><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LZGuqoYHphet9sfZs/towards-embedded-evaluations-for-scheming-propensities#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [J++ Lens: Jacobian Filtering Enables More Faithful Workspace Lenses](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nc9dHfcB22JdMzGbr/j-lens-jacobian-filtering-enables-more-faithful-workspace)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><em>Kola Ayonrinde, Anthropic Fellows, <code>koayon@gmail.com</code>; Jack Lindsey, Anthropic</em></p>
<p><strong>TL;DR</strong>: The J-Lens was proposed to read verbalisable representations from language model activations. We introduce the J++ Lens: an improvement to the J-Lens that enables more faithful readouts across 9–284B models. The J++ Lens filters noisy gradients before they enter the averaged Jacobian. Using the J++ Lens as a drop-in replacement for the J-Lens, we read workspace representations with 55% success on latent variable extraction tasks (J-Lens: 36%, R-Lens: 38%) with especially strong performance at early layers. We believe that more faithful lenses can be useful in monitoring for eval awareness and unverbalised scheming. We release <a href="https://github.com/safety-research/jpp_lens">code</a>, an <a href="https://www.neuronpedia.org/qwen3.6-27b/jlens?shareId=cmuyz763o0001182xcprl8ar8">interactive demo</a> and <a href="https://huggingface.co/koayon/jpp-lenses">open-source lenses</a>.</p>
<p><img alt="Figure 1" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nc9dHfcB22JdMzGbr/nlmtlp8ppmrkzwmyjfqi" /></p>
<p><strong>Figure 1.</strong> <strong>By filtering out noisy gradients, the J++ Lens reads intermediate variables more reliably (a) and at earlier layers (b) than the J-Lens and R-Lens.</strong> The J++ Lens improves on the J-Lens by adding: (1) : down-weighting the Jacobians from activations that result in poor readouts; (2) : applying Layer-wise Relevance Propagation to the backward pass when computing Jacobians; (3) : removing non-semantic tokens from the readout. <strong>(a)</strong> The J++ Lens surfaces the correct intermediate readouts at 55.2% recall@10, compared to 35.7% and 37.7% for the J-Lens and R-Lens respectively. <strong>(b)</strong> The J++ Lens surfaces semantically relevant intermediate latent variables at earlier layers than the J-Lens and R-Lens, layer 24 compared to layer 40 in this example. The intended journey is to hop from the prompt to “heart” to “four chambers”. Bright yellow shading denotes the intermediate variable appearing in the readout, and light yellow shading indicates a closely related readout.</p>
<h1>Introduction</h1>
<p>Language model activations contain a large amount of content, most of which is unnecessary for understanding the main latent variables used in internal reasoning. <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a> propose that activations can be understood as containing a small privileged set of <em>workspace representations</em> that sits alongside a larger set of <em>non-workspace representations</em>. The workspace component contains verbalisable representations and supports flexible internal reasoning, whereas the non-workspace component supports automatic processing. Because the workspace supports internal reasoning, this component might be a natural monitoring surface for analysing frontier models’ internal reasoning, for example in eval awareness (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23836">Needham et al. 2025</a>) and unverbalised scheming (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.08379">Carlsmith 2023</a>).</p>
<p><a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a> introduce the Jacobian Lens (J-Lens) to read from and write to the workspace. The J-Lens extracts the tokens that the model is most disposed to verbalise, conditional on a given activation. <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a> show some evidence that the space spanned by the J-Lens vectors has workspace-like qualities: it is used disproportionately for complex inferences rather than factual recall or writing fluent prose, and it contains intermediate variables used in multi-step reasoning. For example, when given the prompt “<em>Fact: The number of legs on the animal that spins webs is</em>” and asked to predict the next token, the lens surfaces the tokens “spider” and “legs” in intermediate layers before the model predicts “8”. Patching the J-Lens vector “ant” in place of “spider” in the relevant intermediate layer flips the model’s response from 8 to 6, indicating the causal importance of the workspace vectors in internal multi-hop reasoning. However, while the J-Lens does surface intermediate variables, it tends to give coherent readouts only in the latter half of layers. Additionally, many intermediate variables that we would expect in the workspace are not surfaced by the J-Lens at all.</p>
<p>The failure of the J-Lens to extract intermediate latent variables could have many explanations: (i)&nbsp;the hypothesised workspace is a poor model of language model cognition; (ii)&nbsp;the workspace lacks a linear representation of these variables; (iii)&nbsp;the model does not represent them at all (in the workspace or non-workspace components);<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-1">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-1">[1]</a></sup>
    </span> or (iv)&nbsp;the J-Lens is an imperfect tool for capturing workspace content. We provide evidence for (iv), showing that <strong>a substantial fraction of intermediate variables can be linearly extracted</strong>. We show that the J-Lens accumulates noisy gradients, corrupting the averaged Jacobian. Further, we show that filtering out Jacobians from a subset of the activations increases lens performance.</p>
<p>We introduce the <strong>J++ Lens</strong>, which improves on the J-Lens with three changes: (1)&nbsp;: down-weighting Jacobians from clusters of activations that give poor readouts; (2)&nbsp;: computing Jacobians with an LRP backward pass, as in the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nv8oedrnLXKRzNEL9/r-lens-making-j-lens-more-faithful-on-early-layers">R-Lens</a>; (3)&nbsp;: removing non-semantic tokens (e.g.&nbsp;punctuation and whitespace) from the readout.</p>
<p>We say that a Workspace Lens is <em>faithful</em> to the extent that its readouts <em>surface the intermediate variables that the model uses in its reasoning</em> and its lens vectors <em>act as those variables when patched into the model</em>. By improving in the readouts while retaining causal importance, the J++ Lens is more faithful than prior Workspace Lenses. The J++ Lens extracts intermediate variables in latent variable extraction tasks more accurately and at earlier layers (Figure 1). These results replicate across six open-source models from 9B to 284B parameters (Table 1). The J++ Lens’ vectors also remain as causally important as those of the J-Lens (Figure 2c).</p>
<p>Because the J++ Lens reads the workspace more reliably and at earlier layers, we can use it to study LLM internal mechanisms. We find evidence that models sometimes suppress concepts through a <em>retrieve-then-suppress</em> mechanism: the model brings the concept into the workspace in early layers and causally uses this early-layer workspace representation to de-amplify the concept’s salience in later layers (see <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Case_Study__The_Retrieve_then_Suppress_Mechanism_for_Internal_Suppression">Case Study: The Retrieve-then-Suppress Mechanism for Internal Suppression</a>).</p>
<p><strong>Contributions.</strong> Our contributions are as follows:</p>
<ul>
<li>
<p>We show that noisy gradients corrupt the averaged Jacobian of prior Workspace Lenses (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Methods">Methods</a> and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Why_Does_the_J___Lens_Work_">Why Does the J++ Lens Work?</a>).</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>We propose the <strong>J++ Lens</strong>, a Workspace Lens that filters noisy gradients to read from the workspace more faithfully. Across all six models we test (9B–284B parameters), it outperforms the J- and R-Lenses, with a <strong>median relative improvement of 64%</strong> over the J-Lens for reading out intermediate variables (Table 1). For writing to the workspace, J++ Lens vectors are <strong>at least as causally important</strong> as J-Lens vectors, changing the model’s output as intended in 59.7% of trials, against 49.3% for the J-Lens (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#The_J___Lens_Outperforms_the_J__and_R_Lenses_on_Readout_Evaluations">The J++ Lens Outperforms the J- and R-Lenses on Readout Evaluations</a>).</p>
</li>
<li>
<p>We use the J++ Lens to generate and test mechanistic hypotheses, finding evidence that early workspace layers are sometimes causally important for how models suppress concepts (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Case_Study__The_Retrieve_then_Suppress_Mechanism_for_Internal_Suppression">Case Study: The Retrieve-then-Suppress Mechanism for Internal Suppression</a>; Figures 5 to 7).</p>
</li>
</ul>
<p>To support researchers using Workspace Lenses, we release <a href="https://github.com/safety-research/jpp_lens">training code</a>, an evaluation harness for readout and causal evals, and <a href="https://huggingface.co/koayon/jpp-lenses">open-source lenses</a>, which can be explored interactively in the browser on <a href="https://www.neuronpedia.org/qwen3.6-27b/jlens?shareId=cmuyz763o0001182xcprl8ar8">Neuronpedia</a>. The J++ Lens can be used as a drop-in replacement for the J-Lens for surfacing intermediate variables, linear probing or precise interventions. Researchers may also use the J++ Lens as an initialisation for building multi-token Workspace Lenses such as the <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Template Lens</a> or <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Oracle Lens</a>.</p>
<h1>Background</h1>
<p><strong>J-Lens.</strong> <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a> introduce the Jacobian Lens (J-Lens) as a tool for reading from and writing to the workspace, the set of verbalisable representations that a model uses as a working memory. A <em>Workspace Lens</em> is any method that, like the J-Lens, seeks to interpret <em>only</em> the workspace. The J-Lens surfaces the tokens that a model is increasingly disposed to verbalise at future token positions given an intermediate activation  at layer  and source position . Specifically, the J-Lens characterises an activation by its average first-order causal effect on the model’s output logits, using the averaged Jacobian  where  is the final-layer activation at target position  and  is the sequence length ( in our experiments). We may think of  as approximating a “transport map” from the activation space at layer  to that at the final layer (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.08112">Belrose et al. 2023</a>; <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2308.09124">Hernandez et al. 2023</a>). After applying the transport map, the verbalisable tokens can be read off using the unembedding matrix . We call the rows of , corresponding to each vocabulary token, the <em>lens vectors</em>. Equation (1) weights every Jacobian equally, so noisy Jacobians enter the averaged Jacobian unfiltered. In <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Methods">Methods</a>, we describe the J++ Lens, which reduces the noise that enters the averaged Jacobian.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-2">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-2">[2]</a></sup>
    </span></p>
<p><strong>R-Lens.</strong> The <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nv8oedrnLXKRzNEL9/r-lens-making-j-lens-more-faithful-on-early-layers">R-Lens</a> improves on the J-Lens by limiting error accumulation in the backward pass: this approach computes each Jacobian in Equation (1) with an LRP backward pass (<a href="https://doi.org/10.1371/journal.pone.0130140">Bach et al. 2015</a>; <a href="https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-030-28954-6_10">Montavon et al. 2019</a>) rather than the full gradient. We later show that combining LRP with other methods to reduce the noise accumulation in early-layer Jacobians leads to more faithful Workspace Lenses. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_B__Related_Work">Appendix B: Related Work</a> discusses further related work.</p>
<h1>Methods</h1>
<p>When computing the averaged Jacobian in Equation (1), there are many potential sources of noise that can enter into the average and corrupt the final result. Averaging Jacobians reduces <em>unbiased</em> noise, which cancels out in expectation, but not <em>biased</em> noise. Skipping the first 16 positions of each sequence when computing the averaged Jacobian can avoid attention sinks, which may contribute outlier gradients that are one source of biased noise.</p>
<h2>Reducing Noise in Workspace Lenses</h2>
<p>We make three further changes to the J-Lens to reduce biased noise: ,  and . Together, they give the J++ Lens:  where  is a partition of the activation space at layer  into  clusters,  are learned weights,  denotes differentiation with the LRP backward pass,  is the unembedding matrix and  is the vocabulary  with non-semantic tokens removed. Removing the changes (in colour) recovers the original J-Lens formulation of Equation (1).</p>
<h3>Jacobian Filtering</h3>
<p>Jacobian Filtering targets the noisy gradients that come from particular intermediate-layer source activations (). To isolate such source activations, we partition the activation space at each layer into  clusters  with -means clustering. We then fit one averaged Jacobian  per cluster, using only the source activations in that cluster. We consider these Jacobians as <em>Expert Jacobians</em> that specialise to a subset of the activation space, inspired by mixture-of-experts (MoE) models (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1701.06538">Shazeer et al. 2017</a>).</p>
<p>Empirically, we find that some of these experts perform well applied to any intermediate extraction task, even for activations outside their own cluster. Other experts, however, routinely give poor readouts. We hypothesise that the geometry of the transport map around the activations behind these noisy Jacobians may be particularly singular or have high curvature, so that the Jacobian there is a poor approximation to the true transport map.</p>
<p>To avoid including these noisy Jacobians in our averaged Jacobian, we combine the Expert Jacobians into a single linear map by taking a weighted average rather than including all Jacobians equally. We learn the weights  on a small dev set of latent variable extraction problems.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-3">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-3">[3]</a></sup>
    </span> In practice, the weights mainly decide which experts are given zero (or negative) weight and which are included in the average (see Figure 12 in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#What_Is_in_the_Bad_Experts_">What Is in the Bad Experts?</a>). The resulting map  is a drop-in replacement for the J-Lens with no extra inference cost, but with noisy Jacobians effectively filtered out.</p>
<h3>Layer-wise Relevance Propagation (LRP)</h3>
<p>We follow <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nv8oedrnLXKRzNEL9/r-lens-making-j-lens-more-faithful-on-early-layers">Blank, Bhatia, and Nanda (2026)</a> in computing each Jacobian with an LRP backward pass (the  in Equation (2a)), which adds stop gradients to limit the errors that accumulate in the backward pass. In particular, we apply the LN-rule to the residual-stream normalisation functions and the identity rule and half rule to the gated MLPs, and use the plain gradient elsewhere, including through attention (Table 5). We also freeze the expert routing weights in MoE models and the Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections (mHC) residual mixing coefficients in models that use <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.24880">mHC</a>, such as DeepSeek-V4-Flash. We refer readers to <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nv8oedrnLXKRzNEL9/r-lens-making-j-lens-more-faithful-on-early-layers">Blank, Bhatia, and Nanda (2026)</a> for a more complete exposition of using LRP in Workspace Lenses.</p>
<h3>Readout Filtering</h3>
<p>Looking at Workspace Lens readouts, we noticed that a few tokens appear in the top readouts of many activations despite seeming unrelated to the context.</p>
<p>In designing a Workspace Lens, we would like to understand each activation in terms of its first-order causal impact on the model’s output. However, the Jacobian transport is a homogeneous linear map rather than an affine map that can capture input-independent reweighting of the lens readouts. In practice we filter out non-semantic tokens from the lens readouts post hoc. We find that these tokens are sometimes over-represented (possibly due to their high frequency in natural language text) and they are less useful for our purposes.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-4">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-4">[4]</a></sup>
    </span> In Equation (2c), this restricts the readout to , rather than the whole vocabulary .</p>
<h2>Experimental Setup</h2>
<p><strong>Models.</strong> Unless stated otherwise, all experiments use Qwen3.6-27B. We also evaluate readouts on five further models: Qwen3.5-9B, Gemma&nbsp;4&nbsp;31B, Olmo&nbsp;3&nbsp;32B, Qwen3.5-122B-A10B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash.</p>
<p><strong>Hyperparameters.</strong> We fit each lens on 64 sequences of 128 tokens from <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.07843">WikiText-103</a>, skipping the first 16 positions of each sequence. We use  experts per layer. The dev set used to learn the expert weights is disjoint from the evaluation set. Full hyperparameters and fitting costs are in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_I__Hyperparameters">Appendix I: Hyperparameters</a>.</p>
<p><strong>Evaluations.</strong> Readout evaluations use five latent variable extraction tasks adapted from <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a>: multihop, multilingual, typo, association and poetry. We report recall@10: an item counts as a hit if its target intermediate variable appears in the top 10 readouts at any of seven equally spaced layers. Our main results are macro-averaged over the five tasks. Our causal evaluations adapt the intermediate-swap task of <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a>.<span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-5">
      <sup><a class="" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-5">[5]</a></sup>
    </span> We perform swaps and causal interventions as described by <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a>. In causal evaluations, we report the share of trials in which patching in a swapped-in intermediate concept’s lens vector changes the model’s output token in the corresponding way. <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_F__Lens_Readouts__Qualitative_Results_">Appendix F: Lens Readouts (Qualitative Results)</a> shows an example item from each readout task, and <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Additional_Readout_Results">Additional Readout Results</a> gives further results, including recall@1.</p>
<p><strong>Baselines.</strong> We compare against three baselines: the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/AcKRB8wDpdaN6v6ru/interpreting-gpt-the-logit-lens">Logit Lens</a>, which applies the unembedding matrix  directly to intermediate activations; the <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">J-Lens</a>; and the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nv8oedrnLXKRzNEL9/r-lens-making-j-lens-more-faithful-on-early-layers">R-Lens</a>. We provide more details on the external models used in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_I__Hyperparameters">Appendix I: Hyperparameters</a>.</p>
<h1>Results</h1>
<p>We first show that the J++ Lens equals or improves on previous lenses across readout and causal evals (our two measures of faithfulness) in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#The_J___Lens_Outperforms_the_J__and_R_Lenses_on_Readout_Evaluations">The J++ Lens Outperforms the J- and R-Lenses on Readout Evaluations</a> and then show why the J++ Lens achieves these improvements in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Why_Does_the_J___Lens_Work_">Why Does the J++ Lens Work?</a>. Finally, we give preliminary evidence that the J++ Lens can support Mechanistic Interpretability research in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Case_Study__The_Retrieve_then_Suppress_Mechanism_for_Internal_Suppression">Case Study: The Retrieve-then-Suppress Mechanism for Internal Suppression</a>.</p>
<h2>The J++ Lens Outperforms the J- and R-Lenses on Readout Evaluations</h2>
<p>The J++ Lens outperforms the J-Lens and R-Lens across tasks (see Figure 2a), layers (see Figure 2b) and models (see Table 1). We see the strongest performance uplift in early layers where the J-Lens struggles the most. We see the J++ Lens’ improved performance as evidence that there is useful information in the workspace earlier than was suggested by <a href="https://transformer-circuits.pub/2026/workspace/index.html">Gurnee et al. (2026)</a>. The relative improvement over the J-Lens is largest on the two largest models, Qwen3.5-122B-A10B (+98%) and DeepSeek-V4-Flash (+101%), a 284B-parameter MoE model on which the J++ Lens scores highest (61.4%). We suggest that the advantage of the J++ Lens may grow with scale.</p>
<p>J++ Lens vectors are at least as causally important as J- and R-Lens vectors: when patched into the model in the intermediate-swap eval, they change the final answer as intended in 59.7% of trials, against 49.3% and 50.7% for the J-Lens and R-Lens respectively (see Figure 2c).</p>
<p><img alt="Figure 2" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/nc9dHfcB22JdMzGbr/cx7kqvb7dk1z8uawmnzo" /></p>
<p><strong>Figure 2.</strong> <strong>The J++ Lens beats the J-Lens and R-Lens on every readout task and at every layer (a, b), while retaining the causal importance of the lens vectors (c).</strong> <strong>(a)</strong> The J++ Lens outperforms on every task, with the largest relative gain on association (from 11–13% to 37%); poetry remains hard for every lens (at most 5%). <strong>(b)</strong> The J++ Lens has higher recall than the J-Lens and R-Lens at every layer, most notably in the first quarter of layers. <strong>(c)</strong> Intermediate-swap success: the share of 67 trials in which patching in the swapped-in concept’s lens vector makes it the model’s top answer word. J++ Lens vectors succeed in 59.7% of trials, against 49.3% for the J-Lens (+10.4 percentage points) and 50.7% for the R-Lens. All panels show Qwen3.6-27B.</p>
<p><strong>Table 1.</strong> <strong>Across six models, the J++ Lens reads intermediate variables substantially more reliably than the J-Lens and the R-Lens.</strong> The J++ Lens’ median relative improvement (“uplift”) is 63.5% over the J-Lens and 42.2% over the R-Lens. The J++ Lens’ advantage over the J-Lens may improve with scale: on DeepSeek-V4-Flash, the largest model we used, it achieves a 101% uplift (61.4% compared to 30.5%). Scores are recall@10 (%), macro-averaged over the five readout tasks; the best lens in each column is in bold. Here all lenses are scored with Readout Filtering to illustrate the impact of Jacobian Filtering and LRP.</p>

<h2>Why Does the J++ Lens Work?</h2>
<p>Each of our changes, ,  and , improves the performance of the lens. Removing Jacobian Filtering has the largest impact (see Table 2). Here we show why each change is useful.</p>
<p><strong>Table 2.</strong> <strong>Jacobian Filtering is responsible for most of the improvement over the J-Lens.</strong> Recall@10 on Qwen3.6-27B when each of our three changes is removed; Δ is the change in percentage points (pp) relative to the full J++ Lens. Removing Jacobian Filtering costs 11.7pp, 60% of the J++ Lens’ 19.5pp gain over the J-Lens.</p>
