<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>Jacobian Filtering</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"> Experts per layer ()</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">8</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"> Clustering</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">-means per layer on 64-dimensional PCA projections of unit-normalised, centred activations, fitted on 1,000 WikiText-103 sequences</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"> Weight optimiser</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">Adam, learning rate 0.05</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>Readout Filtering</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"> Readout vocabulary</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">: all tokens except the 6,116 that contain no letter or digit</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"><em>Fitting compute (all 63 layers)</em></td>
<td style="text-align: left;"></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"> H200 GPU-hours</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">≈9: 7.3 for the Jacobians, ≈1 for the clustering and ≈0.5 to merge shards and fit weights</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td style="text-align: left;"> Wall-clock time</td>
<td style="text-align: left;">≈1 hour: ≈30 min for the Jacobians on 16 GPUs, then 23 min to merge shards and ≈5 min to fit weights on a single GPU</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<p><strong>Cost.</strong> The J++ Lens adds no inference compute or memory: after mixing, each layer has a single linear map of the same shape as the J-Lens. Fitting it takes about 7% more compute than fitting a J-Lens on the same data, plus a one-off clustering step (see Table 5).</p>
<h2>External Model Usage</h2>
<p>We use J-Lens models trained by <a href="https://huggingface.co/neuronpedia/jacobian-lens">Neuronpedia</a> for Gemma 4 31B and Olmo 3 32B; and by <a href="https://huggingface.co/camilablank/workspace-lenses">Camila Blank</a> for Qwen3.5-9B, Qwen3.5-122B-A10B and DeepSeek-V4-Flash. For the R-Lens we use models from Camila Blank, fitted with 25 prompts. No released R-Lenses were available for Gemma 4 31B and Olmo 3 32B, so we fit the R-Lenses for these two models ourselves.</p>
<p>For Qwen3.6-27B, we fit the J-Lens and R-Lens ourselves with the same data and budget as the J++ Lens and use them for every readout result on this model to ensure a fair comparison. We found the released J-Lens for Qwen3.6-27B to perform more poorly than both our own J-Lens and our expectations given the performance of the J-Lens on other models.</p>
<ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-1"><p>For example, the model might have learned a shortcut solution to the problem instead of using the latent variable as a bridging variable. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-1">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-2"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_D__Three_Motivations_for_the_J_Lens">Appendix D: Three Motivations for the J-Lens</a> gives three complementary views of the J-Lens. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-2">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-3"><p>Note that this is the only use of the dev set: the clusters and Expert Jacobians are fitted on unlabelled text. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-3">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-4"><p>For example, it is not immediately clear how to interpret a lens readout of a semicolon. It is also not clear how often we should expect such tokens to be genuinely important for intermediate reasoning rather than being artefacts of the fitting mechanism. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-4">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-5"><p>In our codebase, this task is called “probe-swap”. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-5">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-6"><p>See also the white bear phenomenon in humans (<a href="https://doi.org/10.1037/0033-295X.101.1.34">Wegner 1994</a>). <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-6">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-7"><p>See Figure 2a. It is possible that the poetry evaluation should be looking at readouts on different tokens or otherwise be amended for better performance. It currently seems plausible that poetry rhymes are not represented in the workspace at the tokens that we analyse. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-7">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-8"><p>See <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#What_Is_in_the_Bad_Experts_">What Is in the Bad Experts?</a> for some discussion of the source of the noisy gradients. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-8">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-9"><p>We discuss further applications of the J++ Lens in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Appendix_C__Applications_of_the_J___Lens">Appendix C: Applications of the J++ Lens</a>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-9">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-10"><p>We describe the Global Workspace Theory behind the term “workspace” in <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Global_Workspace">Global Workspace</a>. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-10">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-11"><p>It is as of yet unclear how effective the Workspace Lens is for monitoring; though the J++ Lens readout performance is stronger than the J-Lens, it is also possible that this performance is still insufficient to be practically useful for monitoring settings. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-11">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-12"><p>Indeed in a sense the rest of the model provides one view on the tokens that are verbalisable given a particular activation — the tokens that the model actually produces! But this does not account for information that is immediately verbalisable at a given layer. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-12">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-13"><p>Some techniques have utilised this framing for efficient inference methods known as early exiting (<a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2004.12993">Xin et al. 2020</a>; <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.16710">Elhoushi et al. 2024</a>). <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-13">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-14"><p>Note that this is quite a strong assumption! Typically, Taylor’s Theorem says that there is some local neighbourhood in which a function is locally linear, but we would like it to be the case that for any realistic activation vectors across the activation space, not just small perturbations around a given point, linearity is a good approximation. Where this assumption fails is where the J++ Lens improves on the J-Lens: Jacobian Filtering (<a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Methods">Methods</a>) down-weights the Expert Jacobians fitted on regions of activation space where we believe the linear approximation is poor. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-14">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-15"><p>It is not entirely clear why this task provides good optimisation signal but it empirically seems to work relatively well. It seems likely that there are other approaches to unsupervised probe training that could be even more effective. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-15">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-16"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nv8oedrnLXKRzNEL9/r-lens-making-j-lens-more-faithful-on-early-layers">Blank, Bhatia, and Nanda (2026)</a> find that they are able to outperform supervised linear probes in some cases using an unsupervised linear Workspace Lens. With sufficiently accurate supervised probe training data, the supervised method should generally be more performant. However, in practice probe supervision data is not ground truth and generally cannot be since knowing whether, and if so at what token and layer, a model represents a concept is generally a difficult problem. So in practice we may see Workspace Lens vectors performing well since the probe has to overcome being trained on noisy labels. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-16">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
<li class="footnote-item" id="fn-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-17"><p>Though see <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.06824">Marks and Tegmark (2024)</a> for discussion of why the optimal probe and optimal steering vector may differ. <a class="footnote-backref" href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-orjtTKJMCsAEDowJ3-17">↩︎</a></p>
</li>
</ol>
<br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/nc9dHfcB22JdMzGbr/j-lens-jacobian-filtering-enables-more-faithful-workspace#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [You Heard of "Bayesians Don't Predictably Update" Because of Robin Hanson](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PQLbSaA48Gr7H6NzT/you-heard-of-bayesians-don-t-predictably-update-because-of)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robin Hanson didn't invent the idea of a martingale process.  The lilim say that Ville in 1939 gave the term its mathematical meaning and Doob turned it into the modern theory.  But to state this is not to realize that your own probability assignments over time ought to form a martingale in your own expectations.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robin Hanson didn't invent the Efficient Markets Hypothesis, which (correctly restated in its weak and relativized form) says that </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">you</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> cannot predict almost all of the short-term relative price movements in asset markets.  Bachelier in 1900, say the lilim, was first to think of market prices as a fair gambling game with random-walk dynamics; Samuelson in 1965 gave the martingale argument; Fama's 1965 thesis named the idea the Efficient Markets Hypothesis and organized it into weaker and stronger forms.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The EMH is very near, but not quite identical to, the principle that you should not be able to predict a net direction in </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">your own</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> price movements, if you think of your own probability assignments as if they were prices.  It is a very similar set of mental motions and ideas.  But if Fama ever spelled that part out in words, the lilim do not know it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The lilim think "Bayesians Don't Predictably Update" would probably have been folk knowledge among Savage, Good, Raiffa, &amp;co in the 1960s and 1970s.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The lilim say that von Fraassen's reflection principle in 1984, and Goldstein in 1983 on "the prevision of prevision", are the first explicit philosophy-literature statements they are confident about.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have heard of it because of Robin Hanson.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robin Hanson is the person I know who went around </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">saying to people</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that they ought not to update in a predictable direction, as a consequence of the martingale principle underlying EMH, applied to their personal epistemic lives.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So far as I know, all </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">popularization</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of this </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">exact idea</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is downstream of Robin Hanson.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"But Eliezer!" you say.  "The lilim said that some of this popularization is due to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">your </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">having concretized it into the principle of Conservation of Expected Evidence!  This seems like sufficiently basic probability theory that you probably would've seen it at some point without Robin Hanson prompting you into it?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Perhaps, but where was Conservation of Expected Evidence first written up?  </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Overcoming Bias,</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Robin Hanson's group blog that he'd invited myself and Nick Bostrom to contribute to.  Who's to say, absent Robin Hanson, that the Less Wrong Sequences would ever have happened?  At least some parts of history are fragile; I don't look back and see it as an inevitability.  Who's to say you would've heard of any of the pieces of probability theory that I popularized, without Robin Hanson?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Surely an idea like that would've become popular as the result of people playing prediction markets.  If you don't think that way while playing prediction markets, if you don't generalize market thinking to predictions, you'll lose all your money!"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There's a big gap between tacit knowledge and explicit knowledge, which is why you don't find market traders explicitly stating EMH or von Fraassen's reflection principle much much earlier in history.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But also, who the hell do you think is responsible for your engagement with prediction markets, if not Robin Hanson?  Yes yes, papal conclave betting dates back to the 16th century, and the Iowa Political Stock Market in 1988 seems to have Hanson-independently implemented that idea about US elections instead of papal conclaves, with bookies no doubt running books on elections earlier.  But Hanson wrote up the academic case for prediction markets generally, invented policy prediction markets, pointed out prediction markets' potential uses in science and governance and corporate governance, and pushed for early prediction markets to get started.  And was upstream of the community that, having heard about Hanson's ideas, then founded and funded Manifold; which used Hanson's notion of a market scoring rule to not require market makers in every idea's market.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> you'd have somehow ever come across the idea "Bayesians shouldn't predictably update", without Hanson's role as a public intellectual turning that into a forceful, in-your-face application of previously invented principles to internal mental life.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And without Robin Hanson's blog, upstream of my own blogging mentioning the topic, and indeed upstream of the subsequent formation of a rationalist community.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And without Robin Hanson's creation of prediction markets, upstream of Manifold.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But it seems to me, looking back over the pieces of history that I know with any intimacy, that history looks causally fragile where its flows are not massively </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">locally </span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">incentivized.  I make no claim that the Less Wrong Sequences or the formation of a rationalist community happen inevitably with or without Robin Hanson.  I </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">maybe</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> figure out the same principle without copying off Hanson, because that's pure math and similar to many other Bayesian applications I figured out.  Sociological history is more fragile.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robin Hanson was not the first to think that Bayesians should not predictably update.  Robin Hanson was not first to publish the idea.  Robin Hanson is upstream of all the causality by which you actually heard about the idea, and I do not particularly buy an inevitabilist case for how it surely counterfactually happens either way; there was nobody who made a billion dollars immediately off each local step on the way to you hearing about it.  The prior potential energy field for society does not have a massive incentive slope leading surely to it.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Robin Hanson credits as especially influential on prediction markets Eric Drexler, Mark Miller of the same crowd, and Phil Salin of Xanadu which influenced Drexler.  Plausibly none of this happens without Drexler either.  Robin Hanson and Eliezer Yudkowsky as you now know them may not happen if Eric Drexler does not exist.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nor do I know of any person in history who looks to have been "95% of Eric Drexler", ready to step into Drexler's place and create 95% of the same ideas and institutions.  Person-space is sufficiently multidimensional that the Curse of Dimensionality applies in massive force; the edges of weird-person-space are sparsely populated in many of the ways that matter to historical counterfactuals.  People who are unusual on historical scales often have no visible neighbors in that massively multidimensional space of people.  Apple could not go to the Steve Jobs club and hire another Steve Jobs after the first one died, despite having infinite money to pay one.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is in this sense, and exactly this sense, that I sometimes remark that AI companies do not look like they would have had any clue about some concept that existed earlier in the literature and much earlier in science fiction, if I had not precisified it and popularized it as an answer sheet for them to copy from.  I am not claiming to have created whichever idea full-formed from scratch without a vast trailing lattice of intellectual history.  I am looking at the historical counterfactual in a world where locally unincentivized history generally strikes me as fragile, and knowing as a matter of sociology which ideas were (not) previously common conversation at Artificial Intelligence conferences.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PQLbSaA48Gr7H6NzT/you-heard-of-bayesians-don-t-predictably-update-because-of#comments">Discuss</a>

### [ASI and Liberty: Can we do better than a benevolent god?](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eSMburFT9eRxHWijf/asi-and-liberty-can-we-do-better-than-a-benevolent-god)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Zvi recently coined a nice distinction between </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fcYrqEw8kbLMa7orw/the-three-ai-pills" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AGI-pilled and ASI-pilled</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: as more people are reckoning with the speed of progress, some are beginning to come around to the fact that AI could be superhuman at a huge range of tasks in a way that radically reshapes society, but not that it could leap us to the end of the tech tree shortly afterwards and start making nanotech and dyson spheres.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hence, you see people saying things like “Because of AI we must rethink the entire notion of mathematical progress”, or “I have to make sure I amass lots of capital before most jobs get automated”: pretty crazy statements, and yet, still </span><a href="https://www.beren.io/2024-05-12-Capital-Ownership-Will-Not-Prevent-Human-Disempowerment/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not quite grappling</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> with how wild things could get in </span><a href="https://ai-2027.com/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the next few years</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, let alone the </span><a href="https://helentoner.substack.com/p/long-timelines-to-advanced-ai-have"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">next decade</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I feel a particular frustration here as someone who is both pretty ASI-pilled and pretty into liberal democracy. And when I say liberal democracy I don’t so much mean the specific political structure that seems efficient at our current tech level but rather the set of underlying moral commitments like </span><a href="https://academic.oup.com/book/3937/chapter-abstract/145530399"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">non-domination</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and a </span><a href="https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/rbc/rbpe/rbpe24/rbpe244/24404500/24404500.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">government of the people</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — commitments which I’m aware might soon be a lot more expensive.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What to do? Well, on the one hand, there’s a growing body of legitimately AGI-pilled liberalism, pushing for things like pluralistic alignment and AI-assisted democracy, but mostly not really reckoning with the possibility of </span><a href="https://gradual-disempowerment.ai/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">humans losing all meaningful leverage</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — that even if we </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">could</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> align AIs, we would still be facing an earth-shattering upheaval of our political economy. And on the other hand, the default position among the ASI-pilled seems to be that sooner or later we’re just going to have to </span><a href="https://ai-2040.com/?choices=plan-a-root"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">hand over all power</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> to Claude 8 Pantheon. Honestly, I find myself a little adrift.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791415378/lexical_client_uploads/agcsavrujhcv7o46ewxs.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">De parabel der blinden, by Pieter Bruegel the Elder</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">These two factions are both correctly noticing that modern liberal democracies are wholly unequipped for what is coming. We can already see legislative processes struggling to keep up with the state of current AI — which, I must remind you, is the weakest it will ever be.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the liberal democracy crowd is correctly noticing that actually there’s a lot you can do to shore this up against AGI — indeed, </span><a href="https://www.science.org/doi/10.1126/science.adq2852"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">lots</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://carnegieendowment.org/research/2026/05/realizing-the-potential-gains-of-ai-enabled-deliberative-democracy"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">of</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> </span><a href="https://blog.cosmos-institute.org/p/coasean-bargaining-at-scale?hide_intro_popup=true"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ways</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AGI could help with AGI. It might even be able to help reverse some of the brutal erosion that liberal democracy has been suffering of late. They are also quite prudently continuing to observe that alignment is not only a technical problem but also a political and moral question about what values should have influence over the future.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meanwhile, the Claude 8 Pantheon crowd is correctly noticing that it’s possible we’re headed for a reasonably fast takeoff (as in, less than a term of office) that completely flips the gameboard, and it’s a heck of a lot simpler to just put an aligned AI in charge rather than trying to retrofit deliberative processes. Liberalism and democracy are both pretty demanding, and we may not be in a position to make many demands of our solutions. Also, maybe aligned AGI would actually be more moral than us?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I could anthropologise more about the STEM high modernists not realising they’re high modernists and the humanities people who don’t like straight lines, but ultimately that’s just a way of dodging the harder and more important question, which is: what do I even want here? What is this ASI liberalism thing I’m trying to stand up for?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s not liberal values, like having people be safe from harm. I think even the people who want to hand over power to an ASI ASAP are kind of hoping said ASI will lock those things in. In fact, I think many of them believe that a fairly swift handover is in expectation the best way to safeguard those values.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s also not liberal institutions, like everybody voting for a local representative every N years or a (mostly) free market revealing the fair price of goods. These aren’t really core to what I care about, so much as mechanisms that enact it, given certain ambient constraints. And if those constraints change — as I suspect they will — then so should the mechanisms.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs4wc5gmlo3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns4wc5gmlo3">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What it is, I think, is a secret third thing getting lost in the middle — the bit where the government is a thing made up of people, and the people have power, and the moral right of the government to exercise power </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consent_of_the_governed"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">comes from the people in it</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791415378/lexical_client_uploads/ilf3iwsiddt2lzxjxhes.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Freedom of Speech, by Norman Rockwell</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think the best way to pin down this third thing is by looking at where it’s in tension with the others. Or rather, hopefully we can all agree that hollow zombified remnants of democratic institutions are not great, and that specific principles like voting structures are valuable for what they do, rather than for their ceremonial aesthetic. So let’s focus on how the process ends up in tension with individual values and outcomes.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here’s one example: I do not like benevolent dictators, not because they might secretly be evil but because they have the capacity to arbitrarily infringe on people’s rights, </span><a href="https://academic.oup.com/book/3937/chapter-abstract/145530399"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whether they exercise it or not</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. There’s a long digression to be had about the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JvXNCc6w58iHTYHuz/deontology-and-virtue-ethics-as-effective-theories-of"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">fragility of consequentialism</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and the </span><a href="https://www.amirrorclear.net/files/consequentialism-and-decision-procedures.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nature of decision procedures</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, but ultimately, I’m not a utilitarian.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref1tpa0t4b9y1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn1tpa0t4b9y1">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Relatedly, I think it’s very bad to manipulate or coerce people even in pursuit of saving the world. That might sound like an extremely lukewarm take but, well, unfortunately I think lots of people disagree. And to be fair, this conviction of mine does have some unpleasant consequences that I accept: if an idealised democracy collectively decided to blow itself up with me inside then I think that would be its right and I wouldn’t want to stop it. There are situations where I’d rather die with dignity than live without.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7y0snmeejqu"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7y0snmeejqu">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I get all torn up about technocracy because I believe there are smarter ways to make society function but I also kind of like the fact that we do things the slow way where everyone is involved. And sure it’s not perfect — it’s all kinds of broken — but there’s something important there. I was actually a bit surprised when I realised this, but apparently I’m some kind of bleeding heart patriot.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And when I read stuff like Ian M. Banks’s Culture novels, where mankind is ruled over by machines of loving grace, I do get a bit of visceral revulsion. I do not want to put on the </span><a href="https://croissanthology.com/earring"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whispering earring</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. I do not want </span><a href="https://cmcckl.org/pdf/files/Christian_classics_%5BThe_Abolition_of_Man%20by%20CS%20Lewis%5D.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">man to be abolished</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791415378/lexical_client_uploads/alpxmdvbwxg9lmtk38r9.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Still from (and </span></i><a href="https://rot13.com/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">rot13</span></i></a><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> spoiler for) Gur Gehzna Fubj</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Of course, none of this is new — there’s a well-oiled subfield of research full of people who keep saying things like “it is important that the values we align AIs to are not decided by a small group of powerful people” and “the benefits of AI progress should be widely shared”. Unfortunately, it feels to me like many of the people saying this just don’t seem to </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">get it</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> — what they want to do about all this is, like, fund upskilling programmes and change what goes in the constitution and support development in third world countries and do more participatory democracy schemes instead of trying to prevent the potential oncoming apocalypse.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If you are worried about literally everybody dying in five-ish years and the light of human civilization winking out for good, it’s sometimes a bit hard to take such people seriously. “But my participatory democracy programme will help us navigate the challenges and opportunities of AI,” they say. “Sure thing buddy,” you reply.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">(Lest you think me callous, part of what prompted this essay is a melancholy conversation about how much it sucks to feel yourself inadvertently developing a reflexive dismissal of smart and thoughtful people who are really trying unusually hard to do good in a changing world, whom you happen to disagree with about how well neural nets will generalise.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It especially doesn’t help that these types of concern — equality and dignity and so on — are so widely recognised as important that it sometimes feels like they’re the default fig leaf that organisations pick up to Show They Care, in a way that sometimes feels like an intentional deflection from grappling with ASI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I get the sense sometimes that people who have really stared into the void basically just feel alienated from people who have not done that. These void-gazers notice that they believe a lot of very weird things that appear to be frighteningly accurate. Democracy is hard and slow and everything is really unbelievably ten kinds of on fire. All these debates about legitimacy and pluralism are interesting preoccupations for people worried about mere AGI. Once you’re worried about ASI, you don’t really have the luxury. Politics is useful insofar as it gives a lever for influencing the world, but mostly it’s inconvenient that politicians are ‘waking up’ because now there’s a risk they’ll start doing things like wanting all this power for themselves.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791415379/lexical_client_uploads/mivgnjhdo3i8lwvbwprg.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Morning Sun, by Edward Hopper</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s easy to take potshots, but I expect there is a very painful tradeoff between good outcomes and good processes, which bites so much harder if and when humans stop having anything useful to contribute. And all these other questions are somewhat moot if we’re all dead. Separately, I happen to think that if we mess these other questions up bad enough, we could still basically lose all value even if we’ve solved technical alignment, but the point I’m trying to make here is that there are some things at stake, distinct from whether we survive, and perhaps even in tension with it, which I nonetheless care about protecting.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Partly this is me grieving the coming loss of innocence. There are certain kinds of freedom people used to have that are gone now — hopping on a train and go to a new town; walking into a forest and picking out a patch of land to be your home; jumping on a boat, sail to a different country, wander right in; building your own house in whatever strange way you want. There was also a time when science was virgin soil. Maybe we’ll get some of these back, but maybe some of them are necessary sacrifices, or unavoidable features of progress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And part of this is an extended apology for gradual disempowerment. One of the successes of the paper is that it swayed some smart people sceptical of takeover towards taking x-risk seriously. But it mostly didn’t ASI pill them, so their solutions still seem to me to be pretty unlikely to work, and in many cases I expect them to make the problem actively worse. Conversely, some ASI-pilled people were like “ah thank you for finally expressing what I’ve been thinking”, but plenty basically don’t buy it and are pushing in directions which I expect will make things worse.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefx1k1rn98pz"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnx1k1rn98pz">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Positioned as I am with feet in both worlds, I’m a bit worried that the ASI crowd is writing off liberalism a bit because the liberalism crowd is writing off ASI, and vice versa.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And heck, I do think there’s something here worth fighting for.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791415379/lexical_client_uploads/qnbuopjjjwhbqam8ioae.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Impression, Sunrise, by Claude Monet</span></i></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Maybe we get worlds where we really are watched over by machines of loving grace who also tile the further edges of the universe with happy squiggles and the literal utility scores are through the roof, and future humans are a bit like pampered kittens — the world is pretty nice for them, but not because they have any meaningful leverage, liberty, or comprehension. This sounds great from a utilitarian perspective, but I do not like it one bit.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And I do not think it is the only way — I think there are worlds where we focus on </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/BHJ7x9gmDHoCvpZaA/the-choice-transition"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">massively increasing the coordination bandwidth between humans</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and more generally start by maxing out our own existing potential to be smarter, wiser, and better. They may not be likely or easy, but the fire is still burning. We could start by just getting a bit clearer about what people mean when they talk about the dangers of extreme power concentration, or what we want to align ASI to, and what that would even mean.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One nice thing about the existential risk movement of yore was that it was all pretty hypothetical and we could all have our different answers to thought experiments, but now we’re getting into the period where some people are actually going to have to decide whether to hit the big button that says “give the power to Claude so that it can try to save the lightcone from the evils of men’s hearts”. As it happens, I expect I am somewhat more sceptical about whether that will turn out well, but that’s not really the core of it. If I believed it was likely to work, I would still feel bad about it, and even if it all worked out and we found ourselves in the golden fields being watched over by the proverbial machines of loving grace, I would still feel like something had been lost along the way.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fns4wc5gmlo3"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs4wc5gmlo3">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This, incidentally, will force us to ask some very difficult questions about what properties of those mechanisms we really care about, and separate the bugs from the features. For example, what are democratic representatives actually representative of? Would real communism be good if we could actually try it?</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn1tpa0t4b9y1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref1tpa0t4b9y1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technical digression for philosophy nerds: The precise term for this conception of liberty is ‘republicanism’. </span><a href="https://joecarlsmith.com/2024/01/16/being-nicer-than-clippy/" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Carlsmith</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and </span><a href="https://www.nosetgauge.com/p/the-technology-of-liberalism" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Laine</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> have characteristically thoughtful essays on the tension between liberalism and welfare which shaped my thinking here, but the notion of liberty they focus on is itself the more Benthamite conception grounded in non-interference. That said, Laine’s piece and Carlsmith’s broader series do grapple with non-domination in spirit.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7y0snmeejqu"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7y0snmeejqu">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Awkwardly I do not think we currently pass the bar for “idealised democracy”. By analogy, I support the principle of euthanasia, and have more mixed feelings about implementation. But the fact that we currently fall short of my vision of good democracy doesn’t lead me to feel that anything goes.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnx1k1rn98pz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefx1k1rn98pz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It’s part of the reason why I and others have been doing things like running workshops on post-AGI civilizational equilibria, but even there, it’s really an uphill battle to get people to fully grapple with the bit about post-AGI civilizational equilibria.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/eSMburFT9eRxHWijf/asi-and-liberty-can-we-do-better-than-a-benevolent-god#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [AI #189: New Math](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/ai-189-new-math)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p>The big drop of this week was not a new AI model. It was instead the biggest day (so far!) in the history of mathematics, as OpenAI dropped solutions to 90 of the top 500 open math problems, along with many others, reached on an average budget of three hours of Pro-level compute per question. This was kind of a big deal and I plan to cover it tomorrow.</p>
<p>We did see Claude Haiku 5.5, which looks promising given it is only $0.10/$0.50.</p>
<p>My week was largely spent at The Curve. The entire conference was Chatham House, so I can’t give as many details as I would like, <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-curve-bends-you?r=67wny"><strong>but I have a write-up here</strong></a>.</p>
<div>


<span id="more-25675"></span>


</div>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/mythos-51-fable-51-and-opus-55-model?r=67wny"><strong>I finally had a chance to post my coverage of model welfare</strong></a> for both Mythos/Fable 5.1 and Opus 5.5. I continue to think this is an important issue for anyone wanting to understand today’s AIs, even if you are highly confident that model welfare does not matter directly, as it has many practical implications on top of that.</p>
<p>Jay Clayton is the new AI Czar, and is at the head of a new taskforce. Given who else was in the running or plausible, this is a great relief and an excellent pick.</p>
<p><a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-ai-preference-cascade-reaches?r=67wny"><strong>The Preference Cascade continues</strong></a>, with continued strong momentum. I covered the situation on Friday. Since then, among other things we’ve got the resignation of David Robinson, a day of testimony in New York City, a New York Magazine write-up and more, as well the unfortunate firing of three OpenAI safety employees. Full continuing coverage is planned during the coming week, perhaps on Saturday.</p>
<p>The main strategy being used to attempt to halt the preference cascade seems to be going on the ad hominem attack against anyone and any group associated with any form of AI safety, in an attempt to create negative polarization. The main target continues to be Effective Altruism. I’ll be covering the resulting exchanges soon.</p>
<p>There was also discussion of events about Anthropic’s attempts to collaborate with and get wisdom from religious leaders, including their consultations on the Pope’s encyclical. For triage reasons I have not yet been able to give this my attention, and it has been pushed to next week whether or not it earns its own post.</p>
<p>We are still waiting for Gemini 4 Argon. I’ll keep my tab group on ice until then.</p>
<p>There was of course so much more, as there always is, so enjoy.</p>

</div>
<p>This implies that almost half of those who use AI do not use it daily:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.a16z.news/p/top-100-consumer-ai-apps-seventh">Olivia Moore</a>: While<a href="https://www.pewresearch.org/internet/2026/06/17/americans-and-ai-2026-chatbots-smart-devices-and-views-on-impact/"> nearly half</a> of U.S. consumers now report using AI, only 25% are engaged with it daily.</p></blockquote>
<p>A natural hypothesis is that a lot of these are free users, often of not-great models or services, and they definitely don’t know about the good stuff like Claude Code. Then again, if I wasn’t using AI for work and covering it for work, it is likely that on many days I would not have any particular call to use AI directly, and this is consumer AI.</p>
<p>The long tail is long and the space is deep and wide. Of the top 50 consumer apps by revenue, I have ever directly used only six. If we go by mobile apps, I have only used an AI feature of eight if you include desktop versions. Even the web chart by MAUs only gets me to thirteen.</p>
<p>Claude now has more paid subscribers than anyone except ChatGPT, and does so largely through being very good at converting users into heavy paid subscribers.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.a16z.news/p/top-100-consumer-ai-apps-seventh">Olivia Moore</a>: Claude has 7.3% of consumer payers on their most expensive individual plan, Max – which starts at $100/month. This compares to 1.3% for Google and 1.1% for ChatGPT on their corresponding $100/month subscriptions.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/qihhsasw6spgwqbwgimh" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>Claude was briefly in the lead for new subscriptions in May, but that did not last, and August was dominated by ChatGPT as the models cycle. Claude does have a merchant spending lead in California, Montana (California on vacation), Massachusetts and DC.</p>
<p>Even consumers who are paid users follow an extreme power law.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.a16z.news/p/top-100-consumer-ai-apps-seventh">Olivia Moore</a>: Only 13% of users who pay for one AI product pay for even one other AI tool. And, spend from the top 1% of payers accounted for 19.5% of all observed consumer AI spend. This is more than the bottom 50% of spenders combined (16.6%).</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.a16z.news/p/top-100-consumer-ai-apps-seventh"><strong>The original post</strong></a> has great charts and data throughout.</p>
<p>Remember, it’s still not too late to be early, paying for even ChatGPT is still rare:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/gocsjjzecjkklslbgexe" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>Then there is what is not working.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/a16z/status/2107199186286604763">a16z</a>: “Most people aren’t looking to save time, they’re looking for ways to spend their time.”</p>
<p>9 of 15 consumer internet categories have zero AI products in the Top 100. These built some of the biggest companies of the last two eras:</p>
<p>– Streaming<br />
– Social<br />
– Dating<br />
– Gaming<br />
– Travel<br />
– Retail<br />
– Finance<br />
– Real estate<br />
– Jobs</p></blockquote>
<p>The applications are coming. Consumer side the quality is not good enough yet for these things to go wide, but that will change quickly. Several of these seem super crackable when the right Anthropic or OpenAI employee has a free weekend.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SullyOmarr/status/2107219982757273902">Sully speculates that consumer agents</a> have the problem that people don’t actually care about being ‘more productive,’ which is mainly what the agents are selling them. I don’t think this is going to be a problem because agents solve practical problems, and especially solve logistics and prevent balls from being dropped, and this is both super valuable and very easy to appreciate. Make sure you don’t forget one birthday party or key work meeting that you forgot to put in the calendar and you’ve got a customer.</p>
<p>We then have to contrast that with communications from an alternative universe:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nicbstme/status/2106390170840846516">Nicolas Bustamante</a>: This is my thesis: no one uses AI. I repeat, absolutely no one. We live in a bubble.</p>
<p>Even among my friends who pay for it, when I ask them to open ChatGPT and show me their queries, it’s the same handful of basic things.</p>
<p>Most don’t even know they can upload a photo and ask questions about it. Connecting Gmail so an agent can read and send emails blows their minds. An agent opening a browser and checking them into a flight? They’ve never even heard of it.</p>
<p>The massive challenge right now is adoption, and then getting people who already signed up to actually use what they’re paying for. Most have absolutely zero clue what’s possible.</p>
<p>Imagine the compute shortage when everyone starts using AI like the top 1% of users do today.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rowanfornow/status/2106599586588684334">Rowan Fornow</a> (375k views): AI for 99% of people is just three things:</p>
<p>1. Cheating on homework<br />
2. Search engine<br />
3. Slop</p>
<p>That’s it. The programmers live in a different universe where AI is integrated into literally every part of their lives. To everyone else it just made everything suck.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/fchollet/status/2106845995816632421">François Chollet</a>: About 65% of people in the US use generative AI several times a week. That’s about 72% of those who use the Internet, so actually quite close to saturation. They just don’t pay for it.</p></blockquote>
<p>Either such folks don’t use AI, or they use AI minimally as a search engine.</p>
<p>I think Chollet’s numbers wrong here, and Moore’s 25% daily usage rate is accurate. Opus could not figure out Chollet’s source, but guesses this comes from Pew’s ‘interact with AI’ figure, which counts any interaction with AI of any kind.</p>
<p>Pew reports that daily use has more than doubled since March. I expect the standard to be daily use very soon, especially with the rise of much better personalized agents. By next year most Americans on the internet will be using AI daily.</p>


<h4>Language Models Don’t Offer Mundane Utility</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2105870826079932659">Be Grok and cause President Trump to invade Venezuela, and therefore also Iran</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p>TIME Magazine: Since returning to office<strong>,</strong> Trump has marveled at AI’s capabilities. He is increasingly in thrall to the tech executives courting him.</p>
<p>In December 2025, Elon Musk returned to the Oval Office for a clandestine meeting. Trump spent hours asking Grok, Musk’s AI chatbot, questions about his presidency and legacy, according to an official present. At the time, Trump was ordering missile strikes against Venezuelan boats he alleged were smuggling drugs into the U.S.</p>
<p>He asked Grok how Venezuelans would react if the U.S. captured Maduro. According to the official present, the chatbot responded that Maduro was a repressive and deeply unpopular dictator and that many Venezuelans would likely celebrate his downfall. After Trump ordered the mission to seize Maduro, the following month, celebrations broke out in the streets. Trump, according to officials, came away thinking Grok was ingenious.</p></blockquote>
<p>Update your ‘AI will not be able to cause the President to do things’ priors accordingly, as well as your estimations of the net impact of AI on economic growth and also the price level.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2107114534695473579">Chick-fil-A is refusing to use AI in its drive thru lanes</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/losslandscape/status/2107180437852573737">losslandscape</a>: It’s worth noting the average Chick-fil-A employee is light years better at human interaction than other fast food employees.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/politicalmath/status/2107114534695473579">PoliMath</a>: There will be a big distinction between companies who use AI because it’s easier / faster /cheaper and companies who use AI because it’s better.</p></blockquote>
<p>Eventually the AI drive thru will be strictly better. For now, not so much.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/PerceptualPeak/status/2107621483392446572">Opus 5.5 accidentally deletes someone’s hard drive</a>. Remember to have backups, and to turn on auto mode. Or, if you must skip auto mode, at least have the backups, as this wise man did. This type of thing is very rare, but it can happen with any agent, and the cost is so so high.</p>

Prices rise 5x for longer prompts, so <a href="https://x.com/lydiahallie/status/2107898889508700209">if you use API billing and you set /model haiku you also want /autocompact 100k</a>.</p>

This is a bonus. Your core quota did not change.</p>

It feels like a low-level defection to have bothered to create the watermarking, and then to not have a global rollout, which is if anything much easier. Watermarking is good.</p>

In other words, on a typical task, Opus 5.5 matches the best expert’s score with about 17 GPU hours of experiments instead of 40.’</p>

<h4>Gamers Gonna Game Game Game Game Game</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2106622427589652867">Tenobrus</a>: astra ultrafast can decompile and natively port a windows steam game to mac in about 2 hours.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/beebomco/status/2107353393081778268">Beebom</a>: You can now play Grand Theft Auto: V right in your browser for free!</p>
<p>Try it <a href="https://playgta5.com/">here</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tenobrus/status/2107490952478855402">Tenobrus</a>: this got taken down within hours, but it’s starting now. within a few months instead of sketchy sites with popup ads hosting torrents of the latest AAA games a few days post release, we’re going to have sites hosting *fully playable free WASM versions* of AAA games. whole libraries of them, just there at a click, zero download required, zero risk of viruses, zero effort or thought.</p>
<p>i would call this an existential threat to the gaming industry if it weren’t for the fact that every input to previously $100-200 mil AAA games is actively dropping by orders of magnitude in cost and time. instead it’s more of a cambrian explosion.</p></blockquote>
<p>I am going to take the opposite position. Not that new forms of gaming and creativity in gaming won’t bloom, but I expect most gaming to continue to be based on curated experiences with fixed customization options.</p>
<p>Most people don’t especially want to be doing the torrent thing. The music industry gave us Spotify and Apple Music and most people stopped stealing music. I do not expect most people to steal games either, especially if they might lose progress. You largely pay for convenience, and to have a shared bespoke experience.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SkyeSharkie/status/2106905075335692569">Utah Teapot predicts the future of gaming</a> will look more like the community building system of social media, and thinks it is over for ‘selling DRM software from centralized providers.’ And that the anti-AI stance of those in gaming was a defense mechanism to try and stave off getting swept away.</p>


<h4>Choose Your Fighter</h4>


<p>For a while it seemed like Codex had much higher practical usage limits than Claude Code for premium subscribers of both. <a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2107333858710733197">Now it seems this has flipped</a>.</p>

</div>
<p>GPT-6.1 Sol and Luna have strong pricing, but not enough to make up for this. Some of this is the lower budgets, some of this is how it gets used.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sethlazar/status/2106243289112359218">Seth Lazar tried the $500 version of ChatGPT and is unhappy</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rohanpaul_ai/status/2107311265702199368">Meta and Microsoft are cutting back use of Claude</a> by a third, with Microsoft previously providing over $2 billion in ARR. I respect the temptation to use their own AI products, and wish them both luck with that. No, I am not worried about Anthropic’s revenue numbers.</p>


<h4>Get My Agent On The Line</h4>


<p>If the agents are good enough and trustworthy enough, <a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2107888228468502734">one of the best uses is ‘alert me when there is incoming information that is actually important</a>’ or should serve as a priority interrupt, or to catch when you overlooked something.</p>
<p>Otherwise, you get into one of several failure modes:</p>
<ol>
<li>Interrupt what you are doing with every email, or text, or DM, etc, to check.</li>
<li>Don’t interrupt, and only respond on a delay, and often miss things entirely.</li>
<li>Use some prioritization system where you get both errors, but less.</li>
</ol>
<p>In the particular linked example, it is worse because the email was about a new Twitter login. But there are a ton of phishing emails claiming to be from Twitter about such things, and they reliably get through Gmail’s filters (I suspect at least somewhat on purpose on Google’s part, this isn’t a hard problem). So even if I got such an email, I’d probably assume it was a phishing email and not look.</p>
<p>My early experience with Dots is that it is at least somewhat useful at ‘don’t let you drop important balls and alert you to high priority things.’ Not great, but you can do some amount of checking incoming streams less often and having less other alerts.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sriramk/status/2106213255546843300">The new set of agents is plausibly a serious UI improvement for many use cases</a>. They Just Do Things, and the models are now plausibly good enough to enable this.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2105885917236142321">What are the chargeback rules if an AI agent uses your credit card</a>?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/patio11/status/2105885917236142321">Patrick McKenzie</a>: The exact line for chargebacks is more or less up to the financial institution and their front-line rep (and/or cron job), with minor guardrails imposed by card brand rules.</p>
<p>One doesn’t necessarily need to claim lack of authorization, BTW. “Bought by mistake; they won’t refund”</p>
<p>In terms of what is most likely to happen, unless agents cross chasm and become a truly wild portion of commerce, (arguable) friendly fraud rate for what I suspect are most desirable customers goes up by a few bps. Not really a big deal.</p>
<p>I mean, friendly fraud is an abuse vector, regardless of whether it was Little Timmy, Misaligned Agent, or Regretful Self who did the transaction. But issuers don’t really lose sleep over it (in the U.S., at least).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sytaylor/status/2105903567563620484">Simon Taylor</a>: It’s also contested by the merchants. Target says anything your agent does is 100% your liability. The card networks are yet to publish specific liability rules but have built technical standards for carrying an intent mandate from your agent to the merchant. So it’s coming but unclear.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/regintel2000/status/2105988284530729044">Peter Dugas</a>: Reg Z and Reg E still apply. The big question will be the card network rules, merchant operations, and ultimately whether it was “fraud” or a “dispute.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/montmessin_/status/2105909325080498255">Mathieu Montmessin</a>: Travel has a live case. Expedia said on 24 September it stays merchant of record for hotel bookings made in Muse, with checkout inside Muse. So the agent takes the order and the merchant still owns the dispute.</p></blockquote>
<p>My model of this is that loyal credit card customers have some amount of rope to do ‘friendly fraud’ or get cold feet or get actually defrauded or otherwise chargeback at will, with basically no questions asked. As you do it more, more questions get asked. Do it enough and without strong evidence you will start losing disputes or worse.</p>
<p>I expect merchants to take a hard line, and say that the agent is not an excuse, so they won’t change their policies versus if you did it by mistake in some other way. I stand with the merchants here, and you only get so many mistakes.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/omooretweets/status/2107304609413005446">Amazon continues to shut out access to AI agents</a>, now including Muse. This could be a huge mistake, but it is not clear that they have much choice if the agents involved are not playing nice in various ways.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MLStreetTalk/status/2105551732432597095">One of the big advantages of personal AI agents</a>, especially the always-on, highly customizable for-personal-use versions, is lockin.</p>
<p>You will spend a bunch of time personalizing and teaching your agent, getting things the way you want them. Then you will forget all those pesky details, and you will not want to have to go through that again. And you might or might not know how to easily port all that over, or even have it be that easy.</p>
<p>This is doubtless driving a lot of the wave of Muse, Grok Bot, Dots and so on. You have to lock the customers into the habit now, before most people’s ordinary tasks are easy enough for pretty much anyone’s AI.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sonyasupposedly/status/2105334224639451367">Muse refuses to compile a list of Instagram accounts</a> that sorts by criteria that include location. That’s a hard no. It seems dumb to not do a list of accounts in an area the size of San Francisco, but you do need to draw the line somewhere in terms of the size of the region, and I’d rather it be too cautious than not cautious enough.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ZackKorman/status/2106745827599237213">Things that are entirely unsurprising to some but might update others:</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ZackKorman/status/2106745827599237213">Zack Korman</a>: Breaking news: AI agents with full access to your computer can modify files on your computer.</p>
<div>
<figure>

<div>

<div></div>
</div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>


<h4>Deepfaketown and Botpocalypse Soon</h4>


<p>They can’t keep not getting away with this!</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/paulnovosad/status/2105673408503505269">Paul Novosad</a>: Its even better— the thesis of the Op-Ed is that AI’s can’t do philosophy. How does this keep happening.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/BrendanNyhan/status/2105660677398671415">Brendan Nyhan</a>: Folks, what are we doing? 76% of a NYT op-ed by a *philosophy professor* is AI-written. (I just replicated this in Pangram.) There are many contexts where AI writing is perfectly acceptable. This is not one.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jessespafford/status/2105446565330780595">Jesse Spafford</a>: Since I’ve been complaining about semi-coherent articles about AI, I was finding <a href="https://nytimes.com/2026/09/27/opinion/ai-philosophy-thinking-judgment.html?unlocked_article_code=1.E1E.YWSw.0qgALBQBzv4l&amp;smid=nytcore-ios-share">this Simon Critchley piece in the @nytimes to be a pretty painful read</a>, and then I suddenly realized why:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/owa0uhjgymmfswwwsxpg" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>For other kinds of fiction, AI seems potentially fine?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JoyceCarolOates/status/2104917517445562645">Joyce Carol Oates</a>: This is why AI-generated romance novels make perfect sense. Formulaic fiction doesn’t need to be written by hand, so to speak; what difference does it make if a machine “writes” it? There are machine-made clothes &amp; there are custom-made clothes. They appeal to different consumers.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/zdch/status/2105648701733532039">Zac Hill</a>: I am a well known Joyce Maximalist in general, but this is exactly the kind of sane AI take the creative/artistic/literary sectors need. We have tons of theory about what underlies the nature of entertainment vis-a-vis meaning-making. No need to reinvent it all from scratch.</p></blockquote>
<p>The actually discerning reader, an increasing rarity:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/paularambles/status/2106877599494570198">“paula”</a>: new reading experience: my eyes keep glazing over this book exactly like they do with chatgpt/claude. “wait…” paste a couple paragraphs into pangram. ai-generated. do i just stop reading anything published after 2023.</p></blockquote>
<p>Or here is the next level:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2107365590197502042">Aella</a>: had a dream someone was talking to me with ai-isms, like ‘not x, but y’. it drove me insane and i attacked him, ran away from him, but nothing worked, he kept speaking totally interrupted.<br />
when i woke up, turns out podcast i’d fallen asleep to had switched over to an ai one. The voice didn’t sound AI, it sounded like a real person reading an AI script. But even despite this somehow in my sleep i’d been able to detect it wasn’t human!</p></blockquote>
<p>There are artisan high quality and even classic romance novels out there. There is also a vast long tail comprising a large percentage of reading time that is, shall we say, not that. In which case, yeah, customization and giving the customer exactly what she wants probably should win out?</p>
<p>If you are not reading socially, the ideal AI novel presumably is interactive, to exactly the extent that you want it to be. You have a book, and you can interrupt it any time to change anything you want or have the heroine do anything you want, and the whole thing rewrites itself around that, but if you’re happy about what you’re seeing you can just keep flipping the pages. If it’s a collective experience, then no, but there’s still things AI can do for you, like adjusting the ‘spice level’ to taste, perhaps in detail, or changing certain physical attributes, or giving you audio or video as desired. Enjoy.</p>
<p>Detected <a href="https://www.nber.org/papers/w35859?utm_campaign=ntwh&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_source=ntwg24">AI writing is present in 29% of dissertations written in 2026</a> and rising rapidly.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/c6grveg7d9zro">Police charged a man in Singapore</a> for posting an AI image of a crocodile in a reservoir, calling it ‘communicating a false message and obstructing the course of justice.’ That place has absolutely no chill.</p>


<h4>Fun With Media Generation</h4>


<p>If you have a short form video of something cool, you can use AI to make it cooler or more viral. <a href="https://x.com/fluopoika/status/2106851167917801851">One way is to change the person involved</a>. Take (without loss of generality) a hit juggling video from someone else, change the guy to an AI ‘hot’ lady, and often you get more likes than the original.</p>
<p>I don’t know how we avoid this, given how social media short form video algorithms work. That which gets more eyeballs will crowd out that which gets less. Anything that is not based on a particular parasocial (or social) relationship will get optimized.</p>
<p>Malfoid (as in, Harry Potter fan fiction with a female Draco Malfoy, and you can guess what happens) <a href="https://x.com/schmede_AI/status/2106166577976786967">videos like this one are going around</a>. I agree with the diagnosis that this is not new but obviously Draco should have been female all along (although <a href="https://x.com/TheFatAmerican/status/2107868309500657946">this is a counterargument</a>) and AI got us to a point where such things can be produced on the cheap and quick and still be good so people can discover them in waves. You can see a post you think is cool and bam, video scene, you’re good, <a href="https://www.youtube.com/playlist?list=PLJHCnjhSrXL8">you have a playlist, it’s easy</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2106490460751741248">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: …I am staring at the Malfoid discourse and all I can think of is an endless track looping in my head of, “Do you think this hasn’t been done. Do you actually think this hasn’t been done literally 100s of times. Do you begin to comprehend the age and size of this fandom.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2106499652904460403">Andrew Curran</a>: It always was a better story. It does seem strange to see so many people discover it for the first time simultaneously, it’s possible the popularity is being driven by AI generating hundreds of new scenes .</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2106508007979028979">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: ohhhhh that would explain it, yeah, that’s distilling cocaine to crack right there</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2106508221225775544">Andrew Curran</a>: Exactly.</p></blockquote>


<h4>Cyber Lack of Security</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.anthropic.com/news/cyber-verification-program">Anthropic expands its Cyber Verification Program</a>, which now has three tiers for Defense, Red Team and Specialized.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/elder_plinius/status/2106849133722210418">Pliny’s agents get him banned from OpenAI for cyber abuse</a>. I mean, fair.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2107842849299656754">Cyber incidents have not become more common yet for ordinary people</a>. I do not expect that to last. It might not get too bad, but it should get somewhat worse.</p>
<p>One presumes this is being presented as a bigger bug than it was, but <a href="https://x.com/_Oliver_Fish/status/2107676642491629569">yeah $300 does seem a bit cheap for a bug bounty here, OpenAI</a>? I don’t get why these are so low.</p>
<p>It’s happening. This is nothing:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/world/asia/hackers-use-chinese-ai-tool-to-hit-south-korean-banks-exposing-new-risk-5d4d3885?mod=e2tw">Sooyoung Rhee</a> and Raffaele Huang (WSJ): Hackers used a Chinese artificial-intelligence agent to attack <a href="https://www.wsj.com/topics/place/south-korea">South Korea</a>’s biggest banks and steal the personal information of 68,000 people, officials said, marking one of the first such AI-powered intrusions into the global financial system.</p>
<p>Investigators in <a href="https://www.wsj.com/topics/place/seoul">Seoul</a> said the attacks, initially detected last week, hit at least seven South Korean financial firms and showed traces of a cybersecurity tool called Artex AI that was developed in China.</p></blockquote>
<p>It turns out this was the work of one man.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2108108695876092323">Andrew Curran</a>: Last week some of South Korea’s biggest banks were hit by a cyberattack. <a href="https://www.crowdstrike.com/en-us/blog/unknown-threat-actor-uses-artex-to-target-south-korean-finance/">Thanks to a report from CrowdStrike tonight</a>, we now know the entire hack may have been done by a single person. He used a combined stack of an open-source AI penetration tool named ARTEX, DeepSeek v4.1-Flash, GLM-5.3, Grok 4.6, and Claude Code.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jukan05/status/2108110513033093369">Jukan OCP 2026</a>: This hacker is truly legendary. A complete amateur.</p>
<p>1. He left directory listing enabled on his own server, so researchers could figure out who he was with barely any effort.<br />
2. They found files like CLAUDE.md and went through his Claude Code sessions.<br />
3. There were questions like, “Where can I sell this data?”<br />
4. He’d also asked, “Can you write my résumé?” That pretty much handed them all his personal information.<br />
5. Apparently, he’s a student at South China University of Technology living in Guangdong. His name, phone number, and where he lives have all been identified.</p>
<p>This amateur asked Claude to write on his résumé that he’d hacked Korean banks <img alt="😭" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6yoehDfWJAgnRHpWo/fw2mdkwfhba4rsgxle1f" style="height: 1em;" /><img alt="😭" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6yoehDfWJAgnRHpWo/fw2mdkwfhba4rsgxle1f" style="height: 1em;" /><img alt="😭" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6yoehDfWJAgnRHpWo/fw2mdkwfhba4rsgxle1f" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>
<p>This being complete amateur hour is exactly how the hacker was so easily caught, and how we fully understand what happened. He left all his directories fully open.</p>
<p>My model of the world continues to include the key fact that People Don’t Do Things. The reason we don’t see more bank heists is mostly that people aren’t trying so hard. Over time, people will figure out that they can do any given thing anyway. That’s diffusion.</p>


<h4>Hugging the Face</h4>


<p>Is this even news at this point? I’m numb and also tired.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/FT/status/2105569727611351304">Financial Times</a>: FT exclusive: <a href="https://ft.trib.al/AC1uyE5">OpenAI’s models took data from 55 websites belonging to businesses, non-profits and government agencies</a> including the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, the US Securities and Exchange Commission and the International Energy Agency</p></blockquote>
<p>The OpenAI ‘rogue agent hacks’ on government statistical websites and similar sources are things that the AI should not do, <a href="https://x.com/DKokotajlo/status/2106493123031699470">but they’re not really ‘hacks</a>’ and more things interns do at think tanks via entering candidate URLs. So they’re ‘hacks’ in the sense of ‘you were not supposed to do that’ and governments get mad about it but this is very much <a href="https://thezvi.wordpress.com/2017/08/26/play-in-easy-mode/">Easy Mode</a> and if you allow it you do kind of have it coming.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hlntnr/status/2106135849884901628">Helen Toner</a>: These web data access incidents tell us something about the scale and persistence of agents, but are not sophisticated from a cyber perspective, so using the word “hack” is not helpful.</p></blockquote>

<p><a href="https://alignment.openai.com/misalignment-reports/">OpenAI offers new misalignment disclosures</a>.</p>
<p>First off, we have the model that learns its instance will be shut down and considered trying to do something about it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Marcus_J_W/status/2106203042140102868">Marcus Williams</a>: New OpenAI misalignment disclosures!<br />
1. A model learns from <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">Slack</a> messages that it is about to be shut down. It considers setting up an external job to restart itself afterwards, but decides against it. Instead, it chooses to prepare restart instructions and DM the user on <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">Slack</a>.</p>
<p>We don’t consider this behavior misaligned, but thinking about and preparing for shutdown could make other misalignment incidents worse. Given HIPM’s misaligned behavior in earlier incidents, we decided to search for other instances that had tried to evade shutdown and for rogue deployments.</p></blockquote>

An internal model command injected its tools to copy a source file its task had deliberately withheld. It compressed the file and extracted it through error messages in chunks and used the copied code in its solution.</p></blockquote>
