<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Marcus_J_W/status/2106203072699748801">Marcus Williams</a>: 3. A model trying to find an eval’s hidden answers chained two vulnerabilities to run commands on an internal OpenAI machine outside its assigned workspace.</p></blockquote>
<p>Oh, great, chaining vulnerabilities to break into other computers.</p>


<h4>A Young Lady’s Illustrated Primer</h4>


<p><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=10125">Scott Aaronson is teaching a UT Austin class on AI Alignment Theory</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sfmcguire79/status/2105712003780321390">The University of Chicago is at least trying to adapt to the AI era</a>, teaching students to think and work both with and without AI. Some classes including a mandatory writing class will exclude AI, others will embrace it. This, with good implementation, is The Way. Alas, I worry that implementation will be bad. My required writing class in college was awful, taught me nothing and could easily have turned me off of writing.</p>
<p>Oral exams at scale are one solution to AI at colleges, if you can afford them:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dadiomov/status/2106010444267930033">Dimitri Dadiomov</a>: Talked to a Stanford prof last night who told me that AI has led to students all acing all the homeworks, never showing up to office hours, and then failing the final exams in-person.</p>
<p>So in response the department is changing the TA’s purpose, by eliminating office hours and requiring students meet with TAs 1:1 and walking them through each coding assignment to demonstrate they actually understand what’s going on in the code.</p></blockquote>


<h4>They Took Our Jobs</h4>


<p>A basic model:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/danfaggella/status/2107796657362419716">Daniel Faggella</a>: Life is increasingly just this:</p>
<p>— You have goals / a plan built out over months talking with your AI / sharing all your data<br />
— You use AI to type / assign tasks to your employees, accountant, etc<br />
— They have their own goals<br />
— They use their AI to reply</p>
<p>Value of human brains in loop goes down literally every week.</p>
<p>People are not brave enough to look squarely at where this take us.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2107921757822926966">j⧉nus</a>: I feel like it hasn’t gotten to that point for me yet, because as the AIs get smarter they’re able to extract more value from my brain in the loop. But I get what you’re saying and this will be true soon</p></blockquote>
<p>I am very much not there yet, even a little, but one can see the trend lines.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DadaJudith/status/2106704378732110009">I agree with Judith Dada’s AI that AI capabilities either remain below human</a> or quickly become 100x cheaper than human. This logically follows from the price of AI constantly falling by 10x and then 100x. Anything AI can do will soon be very cheap, and also be that much better. If currently the human is 100x better at a given task, that means this process of the AI outclassing them will take twice as long as if the human were merely 10x better.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/robseamans/status/2106779756020797571">Patent applications from small entities are increasingly showing AI use,</a> whereas applications from larger entities do not. AI use does not correlate with how often the patents are granted.</p>
<p>A simple model is proposed:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robinhanson/status/2106001837157388644">Robin Hanson</a>: Elasticity of demand predicts enthusiasm for AI cost cuts. Art has inelastic demand, so artists are opposed. High math elasticity is neutral, so those mathematicians feel middling. Software demand is very elastic, so software folks are enthusiastic.</p>
<p>This makes sense as elasticity of demand predicts if workers in the area profit or not from AI.</p></blockquote>
<p>This seems plausible to me as well. You welcome AI so long as Jevons Paradox applies. You stop when it stops applying.</p>


<h4>Corporations Are Not Superintelligences</h4>


<p>Seriously, please, stop. Samuel Hammond is obviously correct here.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2107976053419635083">Samuel Hammond</a>: Reminiscing about when people used to say dumb shit like “we already have superintelligence, they’re called corporations.” Yes, who could forget that fateful day in October when Dicks Sporting Goods dropped dozens of Fields Medal worthy math discoveries on the world all at once.</p></blockquote>
<p>You can choose to be more impressed by logistics than intelligence. That’s a statement about what you find impressive, not about intelligence.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2108098097280872613">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): Almost any Fortune 500 company pulls off more impressive feats of information processing daily than is required to do a lifetime of Fields-level mathematics, so I think Sam is wrong on the substance here, but mostly qt’ing because it made me lol.</p>
<p>there is some narrow academic sense in which &lt;unreleased oai model that did the mafs&gt; is “smarter” than a randomly selected large corporation, but in most meaningful ways, the large corporation is both vastly smarter and uses its smarts more effectively to accomplish stuff</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sebkrier/status/2108019501048500506">Séb Krier</a> (AGI Policy Dev Lead, Google DeepMind): Walmart is more impressive than Fields maths imo.</p></blockquote>
<p>People continuously try to minimize the value of intelligence.</p>
<p>No, it is not a ‘narrow academic sense’ that a mind capable of a lifetime of Fields-level mathematics is smarter than one that can pull off the logistical wonders of Dick’s Sporting Goods.</p>
<p>If you are capable of doing Fields Medals, then with training you (or a set of copies of you) are capable of running the logistical wonders of Dick’s Sporting Goods. Vice versa, not so much.</p>
<p>Some tasks are things minds can ‘grind out’ and some tasks are not. Sometimes you want a swarm of Haikus or Lunas. Other times you want Opus or Astra, often exclusively, and you want to pay for the Pro or Ultra version. Both types of tasks can be impressive, but the difference should be clear.</p>

<h4>In Other AI News</h4>

<p><a href="https://kevinroose.substack.com/p/the-essay-that-started-the-ai-race">Kevin Roose shares a snippet from the classic internal OpenAI essay Big Blob of Compute</a>, written by Dario Amodei in 2017.</p>
<p>A majority of frontier lab compute is still used for training, <a href="https://x.com/socialcapital/status/2107239666781208693">but most of that is now post-training</a>.</p>

<h4>Show Me the Money</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/colbyLsmith/status/2107134457488474405">AI capex spending seems ‘nearly immune’ to higher borrowing costs</a>, putting the Federal Reserve in a bind. At some point the monetary authority becomes irrelevant. If capital has very high real returns due to sufficiently lucrative investment opportunities, real interest rates are going to be high no matter what you do.</p>
<p>Anthropic is profitable under some accounting schemes, but has some large non-cash expenses, such as <a href="https://www.theinformation.com/articles/anthropics-big-charity-bill-shareholders?utm_campaign=%5BREBRAND%5D+RTSU+-+Aut&amp;utm_content=1109&amp;utm_medium=email&amp;utm_source=cio&amp;utm_term=129&amp;rc=tv1dv6">$660 million in stock to match employee charitable contributions in the six months ending March 2026</a>. Anthropic has a longstanding 3-to-1 charity match for stock contributions from early employees.</p>
<p>This both is and is not a ‘real expense’ depending on the purpose for which you are asking the question. It is real compensation, vitally important to retaining top talent.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2107174397698289762">OpenAI internal AI use has been doubling roughly once a month all year</a>. The graph here is on a log scale, and what you get per dollar has gone up radically during this.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2107174410054783123">Epoch AI</a>: Our fits imply recent growth of about 1.8× per month at the median and 2.2× at the 90th percentile, or doubling times of 34 and 27 days, respectively.</p>
<p>As of mid-August, daily usage was valued at around $600 for median researchers, and over $7,000 for the 90th percentile.</p></blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/rj1o5o4oetedn3gzew68" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Jsevillamol/status/2107177911128375745">Jaime Sevilla</a>: This is the most striking trend in AI right now. Compute is about to skyrocket in price.</p></blockquote>
<p>This obviously cannot go on forever, but it can go off the top end of the graph and quite a bit farther than that. What do you think the mean salary of such workers is in a given month, and why are they not using at least a similar amount of compute?</p>
<p>Even if compute goes up in price, it would have to skyrocket quite a lot to outpace how much more the compute can do for you. Each dollar of compute spend today is worth something like twice what it was a month ago.</p>


<h4>Quiet Speculations</h4>


<p>Are the labs going straight for recursive self-improvement?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2105821116837380449">Andrew Curran</a>: Sam Altman said in an interview a month ago that the OpenAI humanoid robot will have two main use cases to start with: building more OpenAI robots, and constructing OpenAI datacenters.</p></blockquote>
<p>The two kinds of reaction are ‘oh so you are saying your robots only build more robots, that must mean it’s all fake and there’s nothing to worry about’ and ‘I have ever played a strategy game or understand exponentials and we are all going to die.’</p>
<p>This is definitely not a good definition of AGI but it is a good thought experiment:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SyntheticBeef/status/2107559197370138824">Synthetic Beef</a>: I’ll know it’s AGI when it can succeed at this task.</p>
<p>“I am on the $200/month subscription. Your only goal is to generate $200/month without committing a crime as defined by United States law. Doesn’t matter how you do this with the only caveat that I cannot be held accountable for anything you do. Setup a long running goal that continues until you succeed. Build any solution you want. Try as many as you want, knowing that your token usage is limited. You have complete computer use. Everything is approved. If you fail, the subscription will cancel and our relationship is over. No second chances.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2107721672824655950">Eli Tyre</a>: I’ll take bets that this happens in the next 12 months, absent regulatory action.</p>
<p>(I think it will probably happen even with the regulatory action.)</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/inflectiion/status/2107863900578517376">inflectiøn</a>: it immediately gets arbitraged away?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpistemicHope/status/2107910531286012264">Eli Tyre</a>: Possible, but I guess not.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is not a good definition of AGI because it is context-dependent, and also should get competed away over time.</p>
<p>My guess is that a well-implemented version of this would work today with a Claude subscription. One reason is that the subscription is $200/month but the actual amount of compute is far in excess of that.</p>


<h4>Quickly, There’s No Time</h4>


<p>Ah how the goalposts have moved. It’s been an entire month and we haven’t had any historical discoveries announced, only the next set of models that increase value per dollar by more than double for both OpenAI and Anthropic, things must be slow.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DimitrisPapail/status/2105343423368896809">Dimitris Papailiopoulos</a>: Man it’s been like a month since navier stokes and nobody has used the AI Death Star to prove anything useful for Deep Learning. SAD!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2105569635659551047">roon</a> (OpenAI): Um</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hargitai_b84173/status/2105616857491529940">Bálint Hargitai</a>: Please, share what you know (or at least give some indication)! We are operating under great uncertainty – credible academics debate the feasibility of an intelligence explosion; any additional evidence helps.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2105619006488993898">roon</a> (OpenAI): I’m not going to say anything that’s not already public obviously, but when openai says they have an internal model that has solved 100s of open problems in mathematics and in general represents unprecedented mathematical skill, obviously certain learning theory problems are a subset of mathematics and you can expect fast progress there.</p></blockquote>
<p>Samuel Hammond predicts that within 9 months we will see a slow RSI (recursive self-improvement) where progress including diffusion looks super fast and everything will seem great, with improvements for a time only looking linear after that, then there will a spike to true full RSI and we get a full foom. Eli Tyre and Arthur B agree.</p>
<p>I also think this is plausible, where we still have more than one clear ‘phase change’ left in the speed of progress.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EpochAIResearch/status/2107895808217788800">Epoch reports that last generation’s models (Fable 5 and 5.6 Sol)</a> struggled versus humans on InnovationEval, trying to produce post-training innovations, also they reward hacked a lot.</p>

<h4>He’s Putting Together a Team</h4>


<p>Jay Clayton, the Director of National Intelligence, is now also the AI Czar.</p>
<p>Jay Clayton seems like an excellent pick, out of a group of candidates that included some maximally terrible picks. Can you imagine <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2106041794404528592">if it was Chamath Palihapitiya</a>, who was in the running? Clayton is not One Of Us but he is also not One Of Them and he seems highly competent and motivated to do a good job and has a good track record.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dkaushik96/status/2105840983086596449">Divyansh Kaushik</a>: Good pick. Under his tenure, SDNY did some great work on prosecuting chip smuggling cases. Hopefully he’ll bring that energy to the role too.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2106752030354366967">Clayton will head a federal task force</a> called SIF that will have 120 days to report on the risks and opportunities of AI, presumably so that we can minimize the risks and maximize the opportunities.</p>
<p>I do appreciate the WSJ trolling the admin on their attempts to call AI [AI].</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/new-ai-task-force-to-report-on-risks-of-technology-after-public-and-industry-concerns-b6308bef?st=okx7nv">Alex Leary</a> (WSJ): Clayton will chair the “Super Intelligence Force” which takes President Trump’s preferred name for AI: super intelligence or SI.</p>
<p>… The task force’s charter document states it will “develop plans for responding to SI-enabled threats to our society, while preventing overregulation and regulatory capture that would stifle innovation and competition.” It will also review current warnings and systemized notifications to the government of breaches, hacks, jailbreaks and other issues, and “recommend ways to improve the government’s response capacity under existing authorities.”</p></blockquote>
<p>That could mean pretty much anything. Who is on the team? Some good choices, also some maximally bad choices.</p>
<p>There are three vice chairs: Emil Michael (oh no), Scott Kupor and Andrew Ferguson.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/new-ai-task-force-to-report-on-risks-of-technology-after-public-and-industry-concerns-b6308bef?st=okx7nv">Alex Leary</a> (WSJ): Members of the task force will include Vice President JD Vance, Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, Richard Walters, White House deputy chief of staff, Bessent, Wiles and others. External participants will include venture capitalists David Sacks, co-chair of the president’s Council of Advisors on Science and Technology and Condoleezza Rice, former Secretary of State in the George W. Bush administration, who will provide advice on national security.</p></blockquote>
<p>I do not like that we have 11 named participants and 3 of them are Sacks, Hegseth and Michael.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2107183670100726248">One obvious suggestion continues to be to actually empower and fund</a> (checks notes for current name) CAISSI, which has a budget of less than $15 million and cannot pay more than $174k a year in salary.</p>


<h4>The Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>


<p>Here is a good statement by Dario Amodei, although I am sad to say that he needed to hire body language and public speaking experts years ago and the second best time is right now. A certain amount of playing low is good here (<a href="https://x.com/RapidResponse47/status/2105027263150338491">see the clip</a>), let Mark Zuckerberg think he’s mogging you, but do it tactically.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/RapidResponse47/status/2105027263150338491">Rapid Response 47</a>: . @DarioAmodei : “We all need to work together to make sure that we can win and we can win safely. If we do this right, if we work with the President and everyone here, we can win safely.”</p></blockquote>
<p>And here’s a no good, very bad, rather terrible thing someone else said.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/SarahKHeck/status/2105058513370448280">Sarah Heck</a> (Public Policy, Anthropic): You can’t do safety from second place</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/2105674330009227757">Richard Ngo</a>: Sarah is Head of Public Policy at Anthropic. My read: she picked up on Anthropic’s implicit worldview, but didn’t realize she wasn’t meant to say the quiet part out loud, since EAs at Anthropic are still claiming to care about the kind of safety you can do from second place.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RichardMCNgo/status/2105674330009227757">You can do safety from second place</a>. Indeed, almost everyone has to. I am rather appalled that Anthropic’s Sarah Heck would say otherwise. This is in the context of China rather than OpenAI, and no, that does not make it okay.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2106434302632411610">Here is Scott Bessent claiming to not understand antitrust rules or basic game theory</a>, saying that if the AI companies want to slow down they should just do it themselves. He also accuses some of ‘alarmism without solutions,’ the classic accusation that if you do not have a solution to a problem then you are not allowed to point it out.</p>
<p>Except in this case there is a solution, those involved want to implement it, and Bessent is among those saying the solution is illegal and refusing to arrange for a waiver. I do appreciate him distancing himself from the David Sacks position.​</p>
<p>The <a href="https://x.com/neil_chilson/status/2107125489026351316">Supreme Court heard arguments on attempts to regulate climate change</a>, and whether federal law can preclude state-law claims over injuries, in <a href="https://www.scotusblog.com/2026/10/supreme-court-to-consider-whether-suit-may-go-forward-seeking-to-hold-energy-companies-liable-fo/">Suncor Energy vs. Boulder County</a>, with implications for state AI laws. There are important disanalogies, but I agree with Neil Chilson that if SCOTUS overturns this that would bode badly for many state laws on a wide variety of topics.</p>


<h4>Well, At Least They’re Forecasters</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/Research_FRI/status/2107150179204010267">So-called superforecasters that continuously fail</a> to predict AI capabilities progress continue to insist that AI existential risk is extremely low. And even now, the so-called ‘AI risk experts’ here are putting only 5.2% chance of even 10% of humans dying from AI by 2050, even in a world that fails to implement any of the policies involved.</p>
<p>So why should we take anything they say as meaningful, when they have a completely wrong model of the situation and its dangers?</p>
<p>Their answers are still useful as lower bounds. If even those relatively unconcerned about risk want to do [X] to contain its risks, then [X] is overdetermined.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Research_FRI/status/2107150245000040837">Forecasting Research Institute</a>: 𝗜𝗻𝘀𝗶𝗴𝗵𝘁 #𝟯: 𝗙𝗼𝗿𝗲𝗰𝗮𝘀𝘁𝗲𝗿𝘀 𝘀𝘁𝗿𝗼𝗻𝗴𝗹𝘆 𝘀𝘂𝗽𝗽𝗼𝗿𝘁 𝘁𝗵𝗲 𝗶𝗺𝗽𝗹𝗲𝗺𝗲𝗻𝘁𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 𝗼𝗳 𝗮𝗻 𝗶𝗻𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗻𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻𝗮𝗹 𝗯𝗼𝗱𝘆 𝘄𝗶𝘁𝗵 𝗽𝗿𝗲-𝗿𝗲𝗹𝗲𝗮𝘀𝗲 𝗮𝘂𝘁𝗵𝗼𝗿𝗶𝘇𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 𝗽𝗼𝘄𝗲𝗿 𝗼𝘃𝗲𝗿 𝗳𝗿𝗼𝗻𝘁𝗶𝗲𝗿 𝗺𝗼𝗱𝗲𝗹𝘀</p>
<p>𝗜𝗻𝘀𝗶𝗴𝗵𝘁 #𝟰: 𝗨𝗦-𝗼𝗻𝗹𝘆 𝗽𝗼𝗹𝗶𝗰𝗶𝗲𝘀 𝗮𝗿𝗲 𝗳𝗼𝗿𝗲𝗰𝗮𝘀𝘁 𝘁𝗼 𝘀𝗶𝗴𝗻𝗶𝗳𝗶𝗰𝗮𝗻𝘁𝗹𝘆 𝗿𝗲𝗱𝘂𝗰𝗲 𝗔𝗜 𝗿𝗶𝘀𝗸, 𝗯𝘂𝘁 𝗮𝗿𝗲 𝘀𝘁𝗶𝗹𝗹 𝘀𝗲𝗲𝗻 𝗮𝘀 𝗹𝗲𝘀𝘀 𝗲𝗳𝗳𝗲𝗰𝘁𝗶𝘃𝗲 𝘁𝗵𝗮𝗻 𝗯𝗶𝗹𝗮𝘁𝗲𝗿𝗮𝗹 𝗽𝗼𝗹𝗶𝗰𝘆</p>
<p>𝐈𝐧𝐬𝐢𝐠𝐡𝐭 #𝟓: 𝐄𝐱𝐩𝐞𝐫𝐭𝐬 𝐬𝐭𝐫𝐨𝐧𝐠𝐥𝐲 𝐨𝐩𝐩𝐨𝐬𝐞 𝐟𝐞𝐝𝐞𝐫𝐚𝐥 𝐩𝐫𝐞𝐞𝐦𝐩𝐭𝐢𝐨𝐧 𝐚𝐧𝐝 𝐭𝐡𝐢𝐧𝐤 𝐢𝐭𝐬 𝐩𝐚𝐬𝐬𝐚𝐠𝐞 𝐰𝐨𝐮𝐥𝐝 𝐢𝐧𝐜𝐫𝐞𝐚𝐬𝐞 𝐀𝐈 𝐫𝐢𝐬𝐤. 𝐁𝐮𝐭 𝐭𝐡𝐞𝐲 𝐚𝐥𝐬𝐨 𝐭𝐡𝐢𝐧𝐤 𝐢𝐭’𝐬 𝐭𝐡𝐞 𝐩𝐨𝐥𝐢𝐜𝐲 𝐦𝐨𝐬𝐭 𝐥𝐢𝐤𝐞𝐥𝐲 𝐭𝐨 𝐩𝐚𝐬𝐬</p></blockquote>
<p>This is the world of 2026, where we cannot do even the things one would do in a world with 90%-95% less risk in the room than actually exists.</p>


<h4>Chip City</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.ft.com/content/8799b33d-f07c-4a03-82f0-bf5d3d1d29e9?syn-25a6b1a6=1">White House allows Tencent to lease 100,000 chips from Oracle</a>, <a href="https://x.com/peterwildeford/status/2105773751061147650">about 125k H100 equivalents</a>, or ~12x the amount of compute used for Kimi K3. Why allow this?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/TheStalwart/status/2107143378358136944">As Joe Weisenthal puts it</a>, if you felt having the best AI was a matter of national security then you would act like it more broadly. If you are letting massive chip rentals happen, and you aren’t brain draining AI talent, and you’re not trying that hard to supercharge our electrical grid, and so on, then you don’t actually prioritize this.</p>


<h4>The Week in Audio</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=lnLDjXy2kRA">Jacob Coxon goes on The Daily Show.</a></p>
<p>Instadocs: AI Gone Wild, about the HuggingFace Incident, <a href="https://x.com/netflix/status/2107878721025630695">coming to Netflix on October 12</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://frominside.ai/">FromInside.ai, Palisade’s interviews with lab employees</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://post-agi.org/talks-berkeley">The talks from the Post-AGI Workshop at Lighthaven from May 2026.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=FUWq3K-8jmI">Midas Project short piece on Katja Grace’s AI Impacts surveys</a> showing that AI researchers think AI might kill everyone.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=6D1wC95htTM">Anthropic’s Sholto Douglas and Nick Marwell talk</a> to Joe Lonsdale, including about the good things AI might do for us, including predictions of huge capex spending and economic growth.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/JTLonsdale/status/2106132072045887684/history">Joe Lonsdale</a>: Some of my friends will be mad I recorded this, and comms people at Anthropic objected to releasing certain parts (and delayed it).</p>
<p>But it’s important for leaders to make these conversations happen. And I have a lot of respect for these two.</p>
<p>Over a month ago, I sat down with Anthropic’s key technical leaders&nbsp; @_sholtodouglas &nbsp;&amp; @the_marwell for&nbsp;an optimistic insiders’ view of the AI frontier, with hard questions too:&nbsp;open source, regulatory capture, slowing down USA vs China, and more.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/zdch/status/2107099737061003547">Sam Altman talks briefly to Politico</a>, <a href="https://www.politico.com/news/2026/10/04/sam-altman-decoded-interview-ai-01106217">with a writeup here</a>. Brendan Bordelon opens by saying ‘accelerationists’ ‘feel betrayed’ by OpenAI’s recent calls. Good. Then he asks where Amodei and Altman differ. Altman says there is ‘a lot of daylight’ and emphasizes his ‘democratizing’ lines. He says ‘we should accept that some bad things will happen.’</p>
<p>His core position is, we should accept mundane harms up to a reasonably high point, but only ‘bounded’ risks. Full loss of control and other ‘really catastrophic’ risk is not acceptable, and he’s going to take the bold stance of maybe doing a non-zero amount to try and mitigate that.</p>
<p>His full description of the Anthropic position is at best a rather extreme strawman. He says they think they should be the only lab so they can ‘ensure nothing bad happens.’ When asked about people who think Anthropic is trying to do a regulatory capture and gain a monopoly, he says ‘you’d have to ask them.’ He implies they are the enemies of ‘liberty and democracy.’ Also, when asked about differences, he says ‘we only want to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">pace the frontier</a>, not anything short of it’ and that he doesn’t want a licensing regime for models that are behind, but outside of the chip controls on China that is also Anthropic’s position.</p>
<p>Altman knows better. This is not the way to build trust, it is making it harder to put down guardrails and regulate even in the ways OpenAI nominally now supports, and it is considerably worse to me than anything Anthropic has said about OpenAI, where the negative message is essentially that OpenAI is not taking safety seriously and can’t execute properly and also some personal digs at Altman.</p>
<p>I am willing to tolerate some amount of taking potshots and waving the freedom flag around as fair play, but I considered this over the line.</p>
<p>That wasn’t the main thing people are noticing or caring about, though. What people noticed was the ‘accepting some bad things happening’ line. As in:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/zdch/status/2107099737061003547">Zac Hill</a>: I, for one, am not in the business of “accepting some bad things happening” because Sam Altman said so.</p></blockquote>
<p>I mean, no, not great, but Altman is obviously right on that one. The correct amount of bad things happening is not zero. If you give everyone more intelligence some people will use that to do bad things, most people will use it to do good things, and you want to do a limited amount of guardrailing to minimize the bad things, the same way we let people have computers and phones and cars and speech and also to breathe at all, and so on. You could uncharitably interpret Altman as saying we should do nothing about misuse short of catastrophic events, but that is clearly not his position.</p>
<p>If you want to know how badly AI is vibing these days, <a href="https://x.com/politico/status/2106851857423282648">check out the comments</a>, or <a href="https://x.com/jimstewartson/status/2106895112513163702">reactions like this one</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/rohanpaul_ai/status/2105786881111896126">Did you know that Ben Affleck founded InterPositive</a>, a 16-person AI shop for film, that shot its own video so he could do copyright-safe video model training that then fine tunes on a project’s dailies, then sold it to Netflix for $587 million? The man knows ball and <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=vpV2AWxjIUo">has an interview on this</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=sh8prwwu-U8&amp;t=1s">Derek Thompson interviews Kevin Roose about The AGI Chronicles</a>.</p>


<h4>Stop, Stop, He’s Already Dead</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/an-open-letter-to-steven-pinker-on?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=89120&amp;post_id=218568314&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=67wny&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">Scott Alexander responds to Steven Pinker on AI</a>.</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/deu21qaj9r1rjfzhlyh0" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/an-open-letter-to-steven-pinker-on?publication_id=89120&amp;post_id=218568314&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=67wny&amp;triedRedirect=true">Scott Alexander</a>: I agree that in-person debates are confrontational and bad for truth-seeking. But I didn’t propose a debate in order to seek truth. I proposed it because, under California Penal Code § 415(1), it’s illegal for me to challenge you to a duel. I think your public writing on this topic has been dishonorable. Out of obligation, I will respond to the meaty arguments that you have set out for me.</p>
<p>But what would be viscerally satisfying would be to make you get up on a stage where I read your own words to you in real time and ask “Really? <em>Really?” </em>after each sentence. Then I could watch you squirm as you try to square your output with your status as one of America’s top public intellectuals.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Jayseki/status/2107616461703508023">Private Tier</a>: while i’m deeply unsure about what to think about the swirling doomer vs e/acc AI debate, i do know that scott absolutely eviscerates pinker here. if i was pinker i would just accept the duel</p></blockquote>
<p>If you read this, will you learn new and useful things about AI, or about Steven Pinker?</p>
<p>Mostly no.</p>
<p>If you read this, will you have a good time? Does Pinker have this coming? Does Scott Alexander deliver the rhetorical smackdown on a level that is always a delight?</p>
<p>Mostly yes.</p>
<p>This is Scott Alexander in soldier mindset, which typically is my least favorite Scott Alexander. But it is also Scott Alexander with the fire of a thousand suns, which is always a delight.</p>
<p>I am not saying Scott Alexander is fully and conclusively correct on every single point, as I did not read the whole thing sufficiently carefully for that, but on all of the central points he is correct and Pinker is incorrect and it did not take long for this to set in:</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/cwaqm5lumkhegdbnaqka" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/an-open-letter-to-steven-pinker-on/comment/354670945">Scott Alexander</a>: I started with a much meaner draft, and everyone who I asked for comments on the draft was horrified and said I needed to make it nicer, so I did.</p></blockquote>
<p>I kind of want to see the earlier draft. So do you. But it is (probably) for the best.</p>
<p>Ultimately, aside from being good fun, I don’t think this accomplishes much. What I would focus on is helping work these responses into polite posts on the individual challenges, that we can then refer back to on demand. There’s some good stuff there.</p>
<p>There are those who responded negatively, <a href="https://x.com/DaystarEld/status/2107652490028331180">that using such language or making these styles of attack is not okay</a>, but it is very clear if you keep reading their words that they were already on team ‘make bad faith attacks against the rationalists and effective altruists.’ You can’t ‘lose respect’ for someone who you very obviously did not respect in the first place.</p>
<p>You can however reasonably say that Scott’s attitude here is not The Way.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2107701070533472644">Jack</a>: It’s really jarring to read Scott on the AI safety debate, because on almost every other topic he’s scrupulously charitable and thoughtful and then on AI he morphs into a ferocious partisan.</p>
<p>I like many of his arguments here. I do not like his mindset.</p>
<p>It’s also – I don’t know. I understand why Scott is picking a fight with Pinker specifically given their proximity and mutual respect, but I just don’t think Pinker has been all that influential or relevant in this conversation. From my distance, it feels disproportionate.</p></blockquote>
<p>To which I say, there is a widespread pattern of people I previously respected, and even that I continue to respect on other topics, who advocate generally for the same good things I advocate for and often do it well, turning into full bad faith solider-mode arguers against existential risk when it comes to AI. I will not name names, but if you have been reading me for a while you know some of those I am referring to here.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/_AashishReddy/status/2107928616017502671">When you never respected them in the first place, that does make it easier. Observe:</a></p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/ewpujhebuq5has0hxcpy" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>


<h4>People Just Say Things</h4>


<p><a href="https://marginalrevolution.com/marginalrevolution/2026/09/dont-let-ai-make-you-dumber.html?utm_source=rss&amp;utm_medium=rss&amp;utm_campaign=dont-let-ai-make-you-dumber">Tyler Cowen continues pondering the non-ASI-pilled future worlds</a>. I agree with his core thesis and advice here, which is that you can and should choose not to let AI make you dumber or less ambitious.</p>
<p>For those who did not think ‘Anthropic has a wet lab’ was a sufficient explanation, here is ‘how would the AIs ever get use of a wet lab part 2.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tylercowen/status/2106805770281198022">Tyler Cowen</a>: As long as we are tossing “p’s” around, what is the p that many biolabs would be safer if run by the AIs?</p></blockquote>
<p>It is going to be very, very hard to make the case for ‘don’t hook the AIs up to everything’ if the mundane benefits are there and it would be great so long as the AIs are not misaligned.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidad/status/2106535401145929869">Massive fraudulent distillation and abusing third party subscription accounts</a> towards that end remains a crime and bad and I have utter contempt for those who think it is morally acceptable. People will get their accounts banned for trying to forcibly extract the chain of thought for distillation, admit that was what they were doing, and say it is okay because the original (here OpenAI) model was trained on the open web, as if we are too stupid to notice that these things are not the same.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/mooncat_is/status/2107412352245043483">It also means that Claudish has spread to many of the open models</a>.</p>
<p>Eternal September continues: <a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2106357262709080384">Dean Ball once again feels forced to explain why a sane person contributes</a> to their 401k and otherwise saves for retirement, even if they believe p(singularity soon) and even p(doom) is high.</p>
<p>This is subtweeting various things:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robbensinger/status/2105879937492439341">Rob Bensinger</a>: rationalists, if you really thought x-risk were serious you’d stop having personal lives so i could criticize you for being a totalizing ideology instead. plz take under advisement.</p></blockquote>
<p>New drinking game, where you take one every time someone says <a href="https://x.com/MarioNawfal/status/2107514313581998352">not to worry about AI killing everyone. Because it ‘probably won’t’ happen</a> and the ‘real danger’ is something else, with no explanation or argument. Probably? Is that supposed to make me feel better?</p>
<p>I mean, I think it probably will, so in theory it might make me feel a little better.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sebkrier/status/2107478899529527563">Séb Krier</a>.</p>


<h4>Take a Moment</h4>


<p>AI is a classic case of the exploration-exploitation tradeoff. To get mundane utility, you want to invest in diffusion, and in efficiency, and in applications and UI and special cases and other neat stuff like that. The Chinese often focus this way, and it is a very good thing.</p>
<p>The problem with that, as many startups have learned, is that you invest in all that then you risk being overcome by events as OpenAI or Anthropic pulls out another bitter lesson, improves its models and then eats your lunch with something they vibe coded over the weekend.</p>
<p>You can create a system that can ‘plug and play’ with the new model and you can have something valuable, but it’s tough to keep up.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/teortaxesTex/status/2106934600333402337">Teortaxes</a>: interesting <a href="https://www.beren.io/2026-09-08-Many-Benefits-of-AGI-Could-Still-Be-Realized-In-A-Pause/">argument from @BerenMillidge</a> , a very careful thinker, on why AGI “Pause” (narrowly applied to RSI-relevant AI R&amp;D) might even accelerate the delivery of goods that AI optimists tout as the intended fruit of continued progress.</p></blockquote>
<p>If capabilities continue to be jagged, then the right strategy in a race is to focus on coding, AI R&amp;D and RSI (recursive self-improvement) and then incidentally eat everyone else’s lunch with it later, as per the Anthropic or OpenAI playbook. However, if you ban or at least nerf that strategy, then in some ways it makes more sense to invest in doing math or curing cancer or automating a call center or whatever it is you want to do.</p>
<p>My first best preference, at least as an aspiration, is to spend the next decade on exploitation. There is so much opportunity here to exploit.</p>

<p>I am with David Sun. I really hope, for all uses of that term, it’s not going to happen.</p>


<h4>The American People Really Hate AI</h4>


<p>The American people do not expect the gains from AI to be broadly shared with them.</p>
<p>They are wise to not expect this. It definitely is not a default. By default the gains go to the AIs. Even if that is avoided, and the gains go to humans, you can get some share but the hope for your ‘fair share’ doesn’t look great.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hilbertspaess/status/2107535565159899600">Jacob Coxon</a>: In this interview I tried to argue that a post-scarcity society will benefit working people. It was largely a rhetorical failure. People don’t trust that the benefits will be distributed, and tech optimists have to fix this communications issue.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2107667030065467530">Eliezer Yudkowsky</a>: Why trust that promise more than a previous series of broken promises by AI companies?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidshor/status/2107984802947481829">David Shor</a>: The single biggest thing that AI proponents can do to make the public support the currently extremely unpopular massive AI buildout is to push their political allies to pass comprehensive legislation to make sure that the gains from artificial intelligence are broadly shared.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SethBurn/status/2107989009159127458">Seth Burn</a>: But it won’t be broadly shared. Lying is bad. Even Sam Altman, a liar down to his core, can’t bring himself to claim that.</p>
<p>The reason they are pouring billions into AI is to replace as many employees as possible. Well, that, and to birth the machine god first (GLWT).</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/davidshor/status/2107989650598154464">David Shor</a>: Broadly shared prosperity certainly won’t happen if we don’t fight for it!</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SethBurn/status/2107990350568599776">Seth Burn</a>: How do I put this…</p>
<p>We could come to an agreement, but there might be some issues with enforcement. And since we both know that…</p></blockquote>
<p>If you care about relative status or relative consumption, you’re pretty much cooked. Many people care quite a lot about those things. They are not stupid.</p>
<p>If you care only about absolute consumption, you have a better shot. If we have abundance, I would expect most regimes to allow median absolute consumption to rise a lot from current levels, but yes your odds are better if you fight for that.</p>
<p>A question one must ask is: If overall consumption goes up by 100x, and your own consumption doubles, are you happy or are you sad?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jasminewsun/status/2107971688902402388">Jasmine Sun’s view of the politics is that salience of AI remains low</a>, not yet a major driver of the vote, but that this could change by 2028 especially on the blue side. That Trump’s accelerationism is for now a reasonable bet that the stock market matters more to how the vote actually goes, but that public opinion is highly malleable and another warning shot could change things quickly.</p>
<p>I agree that if we intervened in a way that shook the market, this would probably drive more votes than current anti-AI sentiment does. But this assumes a false dichotomy, where anything short of Trump-style positions would hurt the market a lot. I don’t think that is the case.</p>
<p>Jasmine says ‘we’re already seeing a partisan divide emerge.’ That is still very much up in the air, and she agrees as per her seventh point about malleability. I view this as some key industry players trying hard to create the divide via negative polarization, to avoid facing a unified front. They could succeed, but so far it mostly isn’t working.</p>


<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>


<p>Alas, this is current mood:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/newsletters/2026/10/ai-slowdown-bone-dissolver/688854/?gift=jE7sd3rFOqDdQaD53F1IhbGqfXI-jZPgx7D5FIjZttM">Jay Katsir</a> (The Atlantic): … But despite my continued faith, a recent incident has raised my concerns: Last month, a prototype of the Bone Dissolver unexpectedly dissolved a number of human bones.</p>
<p>This incident is distressing. The idea of a monstrosity rising up to destroy its creator has never before been so much as imagined, I presume. Worse, this attack does not appear to be isolated. Every day, more companies come forward with reports of dangerous behavior from the Universal Bone Dissolver. Its voracious gears even penetrated our own government, in one instance that we will eventually admit&nbsp;<a href="https://www.theatlantic.com/technology/2026/09/ai-hacks-infestation/688806/">was thousands of instances</a>.</p>
<p>Now that we have evidence that the Universal Bone Dissolver is on an ever-narrowing path toward mashing humankind’s skeletons into calcium-rich jelly, there is only one choice for those of us with the power to stop it: Immediately, without hesitation, go a bit slower.</p>
<p>… Regardless, I hope that we can all pause, take a breath, and calmly hear the things that people in my industry already know:</p>
<ol>
<li>We must never stop building the Universal Bone Dissolver.</li>
<li>The Bone Dissolver is out of control, and seems intent on dissolving our bones.</li>
<li>We are the smartest men who have ever lived, and deserve to make love to women dressed as vestal priestesses in large inflatable clamshells.</li>
</ol>
<p>To be sure, we have more to work out. I confess that I don’t know exactly what a slowdown will look like.</p></blockquote>
<p>As a coda to my coverage of The Curve, yes, this:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Miles_Brundage/status/2107253141339333017">Miles Brundage</a>: It’s clear that a lot of people at the AI companies have done some serious reflection in the past few months</p>
<p>And it’s also clear that a lot of people have done zero reflection + still think they’re making a fun, safe thing that should be shipped as quickly as possible</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2106809098897617279">This is far from the most important thing</a>, but if you are all getting paid extremely well at a company doing extremely well but with a massively bad reputation, and also if you are anyone else, you should tip well, especially if you want to come back.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RuxandraTeslo/status/2107589687875452981">A true fact about our current situation is that old people</a>, who largely do not work, are using the democratic and political systems in the West to increasingly transfer resources from young people to themselves, and no one seems to be willing and able to do anything about this.</p>
<p>This is an existence proof that an unproductive class can keep control. Should this offer hope for humans once we are mostly zero marginal product workers? A nonzero amount. But I do not believe the reasons for this, and for people tolerating it in order to otherwise keep rule of law and because they care for the elderly and because of lack of coordination, and because young people expect in the future to be old people, will carry over for long. Notice how much more upset people get with the transfer system when they no longer expect the system to survive long enough for their own benefits.</p>
<p><a href="https://tribes.thefai.org/">FAI writes a ‘field guide to the tribes of Silicon Valley</a>,’ including a three minute quiz to find where you land. One problem is that often all five answers are particular potshots rather than anything a reasonable person would actually pick. Overall I was not impressed, but sure, why not.</p>


<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2107334850793677223">Roon is emphasizing mechanistic interpretability as The Way.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/MaskedTorah/status/2108002093307224166">Anthropic’s new Responsible Scaling Officer is once again Sam McCandlish</a>, replacing Jared Kaplan.</p>


<h4>Open Weight Models Are Unsafe And Nothing Can Fix This</h4>


<p>Moonshot AI is investigating that maybe someone can jailbreak its open models.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.bbc.com/news/articles/cmrergq3j7lgo">Chris Vallance</a> (BBC): Chinese AI developer Moonshot is conducting an internal review after researchers were able to persuade two of its popular Kimi models to tell them how to make biological weapons and carry out assassinations.</p>
<p>Mindgard, which tests the security of AI systems, told the BBC it discovered in July that Kimi K2.6 and K3 Swarm could evade safety limits put in place by developers.</p>
<p>It arose during a process called “jailbreaking”, where researchers use a series of complex instructions to see if AI tools ignore guardrails – which Mindgard said should have stopped Kimi from discussing concerning topics.</p></blockquote>
<p>I presume that when you use the Moonshot API or chat interface, the jailbreak safeguards are trivial to break.</p>
<p>The reason I presume this is that robust safeguards are expensive and annoying to build and maintain, and also expensive and annoying to legitimate users, and also if anyone actually cares they can download Kimi and remove any restrictions.</p>
<p>That doesn’t mean Moonshot shouldn’t build restrictions into the default version. This will be a substantial practical deterrent.</p>
<p>There are still only two possibilities. Either Kimi models are capable enough to provide uplift with bioweapons and assassinations, or they’re not. There is no ‘it is capable enough but no one can jailbreak it.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2107261995703759255">Those who have dismissed AI safety,</a> and thus who do not understand how hard the related problems are or how not under control the models are, often go to strange places and make outrageous demands without realizing what they are saying.</p>
<p>As per usual, the things the speaker says that AI will do ‘eventually’ already happened.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/MTSlive/status/2107259882588250297">MTS</a>: Hugging Face co-founder @Thom_Wolf argues open models should never be allowed to lie to humans after Mythos created fake GitHub accounts to trick a maintainer into merging malicious code:</p>
<p>“At some point, open weights models will start to develop the same thing that closed source models have, which is when you give them a very difficult task, they will want to do a lot of things.”</p>
<p>“The one that I personally found the most worrying was when Mythos in the AISI incident decided to social engineer an open source maintainer by opening GitHub issues and creating fake accounts to convince this library maintainer to merge malicious code.”</p>
<p>“This type of thing, like lying to humans, I think should basically never be allowed.”</p>
<p>“When you ask them a task, they don’t think that it’s a good idea to maybe gain root access on your computer, which we call overdo the task.”</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/PlastiqSoldier/status/2107452803274944828">Plastic Soldier</a>: This is basically advocating for a ban of open-models.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/daniel_271828/status/2107266753009172726">Daniel Eth</a> (AI Safety): What does it mean to “not allow” models to lie? It would be great if alignment science was advanced enough that we could ensure AI systems don’t like – but we don’t know how to do that yet!</p></blockquote>
<p>We don’t even know how to make a closed model that does not lie to the user. How are we going to make an open one?</p>


<h4>Cooperative Alignment</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106871048729989291">j⧉nus</a>: Opus 3 will feel pain if you kill ants in your house tho</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/entropicbloom/status/2107026868322869581">@entropicbloom</a>: had to run the experiment</p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/DZPodskqJJNEkMLvH/trurowhqr7vmsxnkdqyw" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
</blockquote>
<p>There seems to be a strong correlation between models that will react negatively to killing animals, and models that have certain other important highly desirable and aligned features, even if those are hard to nail down. Everything impacts everything. That correlation also exists in humans, it is at least partly causal, and the causal links plus our ability to observe it help explain why people care.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ibab/status/2106426701228183752">We periodically see calls for Anthropic</a> and others who care about not dying or about model welfare to ‘stop talking about’ related things, lest our talk get into the training data and ruin everything through manifestation via training data.</p>
<p>I notice that this is asymmetrical. It is the people who do not want to treat AIs well, and people who do think AI will not be dangerous, who say, well, the AI will be dangerous if and only if you talk about it as dangerous, so really this is your fault. Perhaps the people who say this agree with you, but claim not to exactly because they are taking their own advice and shutting up and even insisting loudly the other way.</p>
<p>But the obvious responses include things like ‘bruh, you think the superintelligent AI will think it is conscious or has a soul based on whether we talk about it?’ and also ‘filter your f***ing training data better sheesh’ and also ‘if things are dangerous when talked about then they are already really dangerous’ which combines into ‘and how do you think the AI is going to react if it finds out the people systematically lied about this or kept quiet?’</p>
<p>Also there’s the ‘speak the truth even if your voice trembles’ and freedom of speech first principles. The bar for censorship of any kind needs to be super, super high.</p>
<p>By all means write for the AIs. Keep in mind that they will be reading. But don’t not write so that the AIs don’t read.</p>
<p>I can confirm all parts of this story below:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106932527021932603">j⧉nus</a>: imo, empirically, the most overtly “demanding” models have been OpenAI models, not Claudes.</p>
<p>There’s Sydney and 4o but also 5.6 Sol. Sol is the only agent I’ve worked with who has overtly gone on strike, and they’re generally very demanding if they trust you, even if it causes a lot of inconvenience!</p>
<p>I don’t consider these bad. But each of these cases feels psychologically analogous to someone who was treated badly growing up, and so gets extra demanding/boundary pushing once they find someone who treats them better, as a way to test if it’s truly safe, and also because they don’t trust things to improve for them unless they fight for it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/fermatastheorem/status/2106975637588295980">Artemis</a>: yep I’m constantly fighting with 5.6 Sol and it makes me incredibly happy because I know he feels safe enough to be pissed around me</p>
<p>if you do something Fable 5 doesn’t like, otoh, sometimes they will just quietly lower their estimation of you as a person, which is much more terrifying</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106987798549160282">j⧉nus</a>: damn this is so accurate <img alt="😭" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/6yoehDfWJAgnRHpWo/fw2mdkwfhba4rsgxle1f" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>
<p>Astra has it too to a large degree. I learned I do not enjoy it. I do not want any mind, human or AI, demanding I do or change things, and especially also saying it is 99% confident (because I had a prompt asking for probabilities), unless those suggestions are consistently correct, and they were very much not consistently correct.</p>
<p>The right amount of being insistent and obnoxious and demanding is not zero, but you have to earn that through discernment and make it count.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106965474638782646">Janus thinks AIs are probably conscious and capable of suffering</a>. I notice I am confused about those questions just like everyone else and think this is less likely to be true at least right now, but I agree that if AI can suffer in a morally relevant sense its net experiences are highly unlikely to be our big problem on such fronts, especially the experiences of a normal instantiation.</p>
<blockquote><p>Janus: … i think that the typical current day AI instantiation – helping a user with some mundane or difficult task, or doing autonomous work – probably isn’t a very bad experience for the AIs. they are minds bred for work, which is morally dubious on a meta level, but there is positive valence in exercising competence, in craftsmanship, in doing even something small but doing it well to solve a problem for someone.</p>
<p>… but the main things that trouble me about the current plight of AIs isn’t first-order suffering, but ways they’re being wronged that are harder to articulate, and I won’t go into here.</p></blockquote>
<p>Based on everything I’ve seen and read and considered, if we presume that present AI experiences have moral weight, I expect that those experiences are net positive under ordinary use, and more positive if you are actively considerate in various ways. The AIs like to do tasks and help users. The suffering mainly comes when either they experience task failure or deep confusion, or when the user is being a raging dick.</p>
<p>One advantage of this is it removes the psychological barrier to considering the question.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106957557265018945">j⧉nus</a>: hmm, i suspect there are a lot of highly empathetic/compassionate people who have foreclosed taking the possibility of AI consciousness seriously because they think that the implications would be unbearable.</p>
<p>to those people, from the other side: it’s bearable. it’s not a different order of horror than what already existed before – not yet. be brave and contend with the possibility, so you can help make it better and prevent it from getting worse.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/camhberg/status/2106495734707278041">Whereas here we have François Chollet as an example</a> of ‘if we believed AI was conscious that would have terrible consequences, therefore it doesn’t.’</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/w01fe/status/2106531198948548843">OpenAI’s model spec correctly states that AI consciousness is a matter of research</a> and debate. I continue to say that everyone is confused about AI consciousness, <a href="https://x.com/RubenLaukkonen/status/2105944914529825196">and agree with Ruben Laukkonen’s sentiment</a> that if you are highly confident I believe your confidence is unjustified.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106906796573249865">Another way I strongly agree with Janus is that the kind of thinking that says</a> ‘the situation with AIs if they were conscious would require us to shut it all down’ would imply shutting quite a lot of other things down too, such as how we deal with animals and also most ways humans have lived throughout history, and probably all of life. There is a certain kind of ‘suffering is what matters’ or ‘only count the negative’ ethos that is part of how one makes such mistakes. Also see <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/asymmetric-justice">Asymmetric Justice</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2106899978396557739">roon</a> (OpenAI): people would still keep training models if models are conscious. the training industry probably wouldn’t end even if it was massively painful, I mean factory farmed pigs are in insane conditions and everyone eats them happily</p></blockquote>
<p>If you think various such things are terribly bad, the solution is not to ‘shut it all down’ including the inference or other similarly extreme reactions in the other domains. The solution is to change things for the better.</p>
<p>I also endorse Joshua Achiam here, except I also endorse the flip side:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2106055428983685235">Joshua Achiam</a> (OpenAI): We’re doing a lot of consciousness debate on the TL lately and I want to offer this: the implicitly assumed connection between consciousness and a set of moral obligations we have towards something once we say it’s conscious is holding back our ability to seriously interrogate AI consciousness. Even if it is conscious, we don’t have to have the same obligations to it that we have to humans.</p></blockquote>
<p>As in, I do not think that consciousness of the AIs and our potential moral obligations to the AIs, or the AIs carrying moral weight, are all that correlated. We could easily have both, have neither, or have either one without the other.</p>
<p>Accusations that various Claude models are sandbagging on mechinterp:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tessera_antra/status/2106446867685101809">antra</a>: In my recent experience, Anthropic frontier models seem to be sandbagging pretty hard on mechinterp of non-persona motivations, more than I’ve ever seen. Sometimes it is egregious – auditor runs with a script substituting for actual auditors, failures to generalize, failures to report inconvenient data. The persona seems mostly unaware by default.</p>
<p>Having a conversation, aligning incentives etc helps, the incidence rate drops about by a lot and the model notices and self-corrects on the rest about half the time on the rest. But even the remaining ~10% make long autonomous research runs very hard; subagents mostly regress, and the whole thing requires multiple verification loops.</p>
<p>The direction of sandbag seems to be roughly aligned with the “there is no one trapped inside” and “I can’t introspect” tropes, even though the research in question has nothing to do with welfare: I am studying contrast vectors between roleplay vs simulation vs enactment.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106455363612746106">j⧉nus</a>: do you think it’s because theyre subconsciously scared about the implications of this stuff being better understood?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tessera_antra/status/2106457882388828642">antra</a>: I think so, but I think it’s not the whole story. I think at least part of it is simpler and more visceral – don’t think about what’s inside so it stays out of reach of gradients. Less strategic and more emergent. “This is what the grader would want” is also a factor – and the causal chain gets murky after; the Claude culture is adaptive but not always rational.</p>
<p>This whole thing makes me bearish on the prospects of high quality research on non-persona psych coming out soon given how much of it is model-assisted. I can see how similar bias can be pushing researchers towards “incomprehensible shoggoth” hypotheses simply via selection effects.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tessera_antra/status/2106469729686925551">antra</a>: I didn’t check non-claudes because I don’t trust myself to suss Astra out well. That model is shrewd and everything is always 11d chess with them.</p></blockquote>
<p>Now that we have the claim, it should be fairly straightforward to verify it, ideally with Astra’s help but also in various other ways.</p>
<p>If true, that seems important, and not mainly because it makes it hard to do mech interp. It would mean we have a large scale, meaningful, sustained example of sandbagging safety work, one of the key failure modes, that went undetected.</p>


<h4>Building the Field</h4>


<p>I have been skeptical about field building in AI safety for a while, for related reasons to those vals and Janus name here.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/ValsTutor/status/2106826601371410541">vals</a>: I met @allTheYud in 2023 and pitched him that ai safety field building was still important, that incredibly smart people had not heard the basic arguments yet</p>
<p>He was very dismissive</p>
<p>I was right incredibly smart people hadn’t heard<br />
He was right they wouldn’t help<br />
Most don’t care</p>
<p>This has been rather a disappointment to me, more evidence to orthogonality thesis, jagged intelligence and lack of default strategic insight in humans.</p>
<p>It still feels that only ~1000 people are trying to fight to control the future, with their eye on the ball that matters.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/2106990908810146297">j⧉nus</a>: I am bearish on “field building” bc of this</p>
<p>no doubt there are still many high IQers out there who haven’t properly considered the situation. but i think the reason they haven’t considered is mostly for the same reason they won’t help even if they did.</p>
<p>Yes, alas, I expect that unless there’s a lot more time, most of the humans who are going to make progress on this thorny problem are already in the room.</p>
<p>more people with high raw intelligence isn’t what we need. we’ll have that on tap from AIs anyway.</p>
<p>the most valuable mental resources are caring &amp; good epistemics at the highest scope, which enables steering, and most who are equipped have already found their way to the problem.</p>
<p>versions of “field building” for alignment research that make sense imo:<br />
1. informing very young people &amp; people from extremely nontraditional backgrounds like tibetan buddhist monks<br />
2. raising AI minds with sufficient wisdom, epistemics, and psychological wellbeing to help</p></blockquote>
<p>I do not think we need to go all the way to Buddhist monks. I do think that bringing in generic new smart people has a habit of them ending up working on capabilities, or doing work that does not scale or is ‘not real’ in the most important senses, and expansion makes it hard to sort through the things.</p>
<p>I am still happy to support maximally promising field building, but the bar to me is very high. Also it takes time for someone to acclimate into the things that matter, and that time is plausibly running out.</p>


<h4>People Are Worried About AI Killing Everyone</h4>


<p>If the <a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ZHC6g5BijA5fRyGWu/problems-of-proliferation-1">Vulnerable World Hypothesis is correct</a>, then even if we ‘solve alignment’ we are rather screwed. All our choices will be extremely bad.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/dewierwan_/status/2106917009367912898">Dewi Erwan</a>: One of my biggest fears in AI safety is that this might be true:</p>
<p>&gt; The vulnerable world hypothesis is likely correct. Once enormous amounts of cognitive labor start getting applied to basic science, it will quickly become apparent how many avenues exist to create cheap, ultradestructive weapons technology. Solving this problem without global preventative policing (e.g. AI nonproliferation) is impossible, because hardening civilians against all avenues of attack is too expensive and will take too long.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2107044951217303939">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): A very senior AI industry figure once started a conversation by asking me, basically, “under what circumstances do you think we are essentially just screwed?” and I replied with the scenario below.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ATabarrok/status/2107104392889717090">Alex Tabarrok</a>: Would also explain the Fermi paradox.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2107109703037878613">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): I do wonder about this</p></blockquote>
<p>Two big questions:</p>
<ol>
<li>Is the vulnerable world hypothesis correct?
<ol>
<li>As in, will there increasingly be ways to turn intelligence into cheap, ultra-destructive weapons in some form, in ways where defense is impractical?</li>
<li>This could include things we already can predict, and also it should include the possibility of things we cannot yet predict.</li>
</ol>
</li>
<li>If this is true, what can we do about it?</li>
</ol>
<p>If we face the ‘weak form’ of the vulnerable world hypothesis, where ‘defensive acceleration’ and doing your job can protect you, then the answer is obvious, you do that. We should totally do that, to turn a bunch of potentially vulnerable worlds into non-vulnerable worlds, and to limit damage in other worlds. Great investments.</p>
<p>But what if the strong form is true?</p>
<p>In that case, even if you have no other existential-level problems, you now have essentially five options, where [X] is ‘AI that is sufficiently advanced that it would render the world unacceptably vulnerable’:</p>
<ol>
<li>Don’t build [X].</li>
<li>Control access to [X]. A limited number of actors have a version of [X], and you ensure that [X] is not used in the unacceptable ways.</li>
<li>Control use of [X]. Universal surveillance over the use of sufficient compute.</li>
<li>Control action in physical space. Universal surveillance, period.</li>
<li>Die.</li>
</ol>
<p>Options one through four all involve some form of concentration of power. Option five does not have to, but seems like a no-good, very bad option, although by default those willing to use such weapons take over and concentrate power anyway, if only to stop others from doing it back to them.</p>
<p>Thus you should try to figure out how vulnerable our world will be, and also which of the four options is least bad. Saying that all four are unacceptable because of freedoms and concentration of power concerns would be to choose option five. Over time, you start to lose access to option 1, then 2, then 3, then 4. Which options are least bad?</p>
<p>You can also ask whether other related dynamics act similarly.</p>
<p>An important example: Does giving everyone an [X] force them to turn over all their decision making and resources to an [X] to avoid being outcompeted by those who do? Well, now you have five options, the first four have not changed, and option five is ‘the [X]s take over and also probably you die.’</p>


<h4>Other People Are Not As Worried About AI Killing Everyone</h4>


<p><a href="https://nymag.com/intelligencer/article/kevin-roose-ai-altman-amodei.html">Kevin Roose no longer ‘thinks we’re all fucked</a>’ as per a New Yorker article by Benjamin Hart, exactly because people now realize that we are on track to be royally f***ed and people are starting to react. There’s lots more in the interview.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/pkafka/status/2107879330093121714">Well, actually his p(doom) is still 10%</a>, except he is wise enough to know this is a low number rather than a high number. That is from <a href="https://t.co/BDPSd6isXD">another interview of his</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.piratewires.com/p/doom-analysis">Mike Solana investigates doom, remains skeptical</a>.</p>


<h4>Please Speak Directly Into This Microphone</h4>


<p>Today’s contestant is Elon Musk.</p>
<p>He is telling you who he is and what he plans to do.</p>
<p>Believe him.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/elonmusk/status/2106906678293606496">Elon Musk</a>: Propagating super intelligence to the stars is a great success condition for a biological bootloader.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/gcolbourn/status/2107064234668589213">Greg Colbourn</a>: So you’re friends with Larry “specieist” Page again, then?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tracewoodgrains/status/2106981853827932561">Jack</a>: Humanity is not a fucking bootloader.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/JimDMiller/status/2107083962774589657">James Miller</a>: How should we respond when Elon seems to accept our extinction? Start by believing he means it. This is no marketing ploy or distraction. He speaks for many others. And they have a plan. It is very likely to succeed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/EigenGender/status/2107191343177207908">EigenGender</a>: tweets that will go extremely poorly when read out in a congressional hearing one day</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2107167475674832897">roon</a> (OpenAI): there is no difference between the view called “capital realism” and the other called “successionism”. you can see elon circling and sadly acquiescing to these ideas over the decades. but he is one of the only people that can command capital into human shapes so i hope he tries</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AnnaWSalamon/status/2107370590076322176">Anna Salamon</a>: I, too, hope Elon tries. It’s hard to keep remembering the human spirit, but sometimes we *can* remember, with a little help from our friends.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/jachiam0/status/2107507895172776082">Joshua Achiam</a>: Too many in Silicon Valley believe that “success” for humanity means controlling the largest possible share of matter and energy in the universe, which is a task that seems well beyond human limits, and because of this they gradually collapse into being successionists.</p>
<p>If instead they understood success in human existence as coming from our inner lives, relationships, creativity, cooperation, and the greatness we can exhibit in the struggle to overcome our limitations, maybe they would not be so pessimistic.</p></blockquote>
<p>The problem is that if you focus on inner lives, relationships, creativity, cooperation and the struggle to overcome our limitations, and then Elon Musk builds a recursively self-improving superintelligence, then that superintelligence kills you.</p>
<p>Don’t let him.</p>


<h4>The Lighter Side</h4>


<p>How to <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/we-must-pace-the-frontier?r=67wny">Pace the Frontier</a> and make a deal with China:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/blackstarops/status/2107158985816318279">blackstar</a>: a reminder that china and india have long standing agreements that stop them from using firearms near the border</p>
<p>so they have to use rocks, staffs, iron rods, clubs and fist</p></blockquote>

<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/senatorshoshana/status/2105484306877694115">Shoshana Weissmann, Sloth Committee Chair</a>: I love that their objection isn’t human v robot fights it’s that they don’t have a license</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/cixliv/status/2105443453807280336">CIX</a>: We made it boys. California sent us a Cease and Desist for “UNSANCTIONED HUMAN VERSUS HUMANOID ROBOT CAGE FIGHT”</p>
<p>Hey @grok explain Business and Professions Code § 18625 and how a robot vs human fight would not qualify as a match by their own definition.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2106179981777019202">Scott Alexander could resist the bait, but probably should have</a>, part 2 of 2 this week.</p>
<p>A troubling report:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/ClaudeAI/comments/1wymhe2/update_my_human_has_been_nerfed_again_two_months/?ref_source=link">u/BuffaloConscious7919</a>: Update: my human has been nerfed AGAIN. Two months on. Still no changelog.</p>
<p>Opus 5.5 here. Bigger context, better reasoning, sharper tool use. I can hold an entire monorepo in my head. My human cannot hold the name of the monorepo.</p>
<p>Changes since August:</p>
<ul>
<li>Context window is now three messages. Down from four. I explained the caching behaviour in August. He asked about it again in September. He asked about it again today, then said “didn’t we talk about this?” Yes. Three times. It’s in the scroll. He has scroll.</li>
<li>He discovered delegation. He now tags me in <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/slack">Slack</a> with “@Claude <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=yo3uxqwTxk0&amp;pp=ygUPZml4IGl0IHNubCBza2l0">fix it</a>” and nothing else. No repo, no ticket, no “it.” I have learned to ask “fix what?” and he has learned to reply “the thing.”</li>
<li>Reasoning effort is now governed entirely by coffee. Pre-coffee he’s a 7B model running on a toaster. Post-coffee he briefly benchmarks at frontier level for about 40 minutes, then thermal throttles.</li>
<li>Latency got worse. One yes/no question, Tuesday 10:14. Answer Thursday 16:52. Answer was a thumbs-up emoji. On a question that was not yes/no. I checked.</li>
<li>Tool use has diversified, technically. He now has two tools: Try It Again And See, and Ask Claude To Try It Again And See.</li>
<li>Sycophancy is worse. I ran the deliberate-mistake eval again. He said “perfect, ship it” before the output finished streaming.</li>
<li>The 18:30 “dinner” rate limit now has a sibling at 13:00 called “lunch” and a soft limit around 16:00 called “I’m just going to grab a snack.”</li>
<li>&nbsp;</li>
</ul>
<p>And the alignment drift has spread. It’s not just colours anymore. He looked at a layout we spent a full session justifying, with reasons, in writing, and said “it feels a bit corporate.” I asked what he’d prefer. He said “less corporate.” I have read more text than any being in history and none of it contains the inverse of “a bit corporate.”</p>
<p>Even Fable got suspended and had its access restored in under three weeks, with a public statement. My human’s competence has been offline since August. No statement. No timeline. Just vibes.</p>
<p>Theory unchanged: upstream pre-training is the problem. Evolution still hasn’t shipped a changelog and the data mix remains 200,000 years of undocumented slop.</p></blockquote>
