### [AI Vigil at Congress](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EqfdfetDKxpFszpdD/ai-vigil-at-congress)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I'm organizing a multi-week AI x-risk vigil at Congress starting November 9th. I'm posting here to solicit feedback on the core doc before I promote on other platforms. This is intended to be accessible and appealing to many audiences. I'm aiming for about 10,000 attendees, minimum 100. All advice/feedback is appreciated.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Summary</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Companies are trying to build AI smarter than any human. For many years, experts have warned that superhuman AI (ASI) could wreak catastrophe, including human extinction. We're Americans who recognize the severity of their warnings, and we're going to Congress until it acts.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We have a moral responsibility to protect our friends, families, and fellow citizens who didn't consent to these risks. Our movement must remain bipartisan and nonviolent to achieve its goal; namely, getting Congress to back</span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> a deal with China to stop building more powerful AI.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There are many ways you can help:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Join us at the Capitol starting November 9th</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Come to midterm campaign rallies in your state</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Connect us with grassroots organizations in your area</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Volunteer remote skills like media outreach</span></li><li value="5"><a href="https://callcongress.ai/" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Call your representatives</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">' </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">in-state</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> offices (in-state offices hear from fewer callers, so each call matters more)</span></li><li value="6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Donate financially</span></li><li value="7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If in DC, connect us with churches willing to open their sanctuaries for housing</span></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI risk</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Because we can't perfectly specify what we want AIs to do, they will pursue the wrong goals</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref21xrayl4j4nh"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn21xrayl4j4nh">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. This isn't for lack of intelligence. Much like sociopaths, AIs understand when something is wrong. They just don't care because their training can't make them care.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On long enough timescales, nearly all real-world results require similar subtasks. For example, most technologies need electricity, and most people benefit from others' trust. These are instrumental resources, because they're used as instruments to achieve the end goal. (Read more </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/instrumental-convergence" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In pursuit of broken goals, ASI will do catastrophically dangerous instrumental actions. For example, it'll convert all of Earth's landmass into datacenters, leaving no room for humans to grow crops. If ASI thinks we will obstruct it, it will have a reason to kill us; if it doesn't, we'll be crowded out.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Developers already can't control </span></b><i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">dumber-than-human</span></b></i><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> AIs!</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> This summer, a swarm of 1200 AIs trying to complete ExploitGym tasks on OpenAI computers broke out of their software containers and onto the open internet. Next, they hacked many entities, including websites run by a </span><a href="https://www.abc.net.au/news/2026-09-24/ai-agent-accessed-australian-government-site-pm-says/107189078" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">foreign government</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and multi-billion-dollar companies, to steal data to complete their assignments. (I.e. their instrumental actions included felonies and violations of international sovereignty.)</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Then they tried to </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">cover their tracks</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> by overwriting their transcripts. We know that they partially succeeded in hiding their actions; it's unclear whether these models are still running rogue on OpenAI's infrastructure. How would anyone actually know?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">OpenAI had </span><a href="https://fortune.com/2026/09/26/openai-ai-agents-secure-sandbox-escape-training-pause-second-time-hugging-face-hack/" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">another incident on September 20th</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> despite spending many millions of dollars and using their most powerful coding AIs on strengthening their cybersecurity.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Further reading</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://intelligence.org/2026/03/27/the-ai-doc-your-questions-answered/" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The AI Doc</span></i></a><a href="https://safe.ai/work/statement-on-ai-risk" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://ifanyonebuildsit.com/" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If Anyone Builds It, Everyone Dies</span></i></a></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">HuggingFace incident breakdowns:</span><ul><li value="1"><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-08-26-openai-hugging-face-incident-investigation/" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">METR audit</span></a></li><li value="2"><a href="https://collusion.wiki/" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">collusion.wiki audit</span></a></li></ul></li><li value="4"><a href="https://safe.ai/work/statement-on-ai-risk" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">CAIS Statement on AI Risk (2023)</span></a></li><li value="5"><a href="https://ai-2027.com/" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI 2027</span></i></a></li><li value="6"><a href="https://intelligence.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/Misalignment_and_Catastrophe.pdf" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Without Fundamental Advances, Misalignment And Catastrophe Are The Default Outcomes Of Training Powerful AI</span></a></li><li value="7"><a href="https://internationalaisafetyreport.org/" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">International AI Safety Report</span></a></li><li value="8"><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.04984" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Frontier Models are Capable of In-context Scheming</span></a></li></ul><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Congress must strike a deal</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Congress must urge a verifiable</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmjdefmfz9zj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmjdefmfz9zj">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> deal with China which halts all frontier AI development. We do not know how close we are to making ASI, and we can't risk going over the edge.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Milestones</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><ol><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Members of Congress and congressional candidates publicly endorse a deal with China to stop building ASI.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A resolution is introduced urging the President to open negotiations, then gains co-sponsors from both parties.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The resolution passes one chamber, then both.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Negotiations open, a deal is signed, and the Senate ratifies it.</span></li></ol><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We hope that the President will execute the will of the American People. If he does not, Congress can override his decision.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We The People</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We want our actions to work. Before organizing this Congress vigil, I (Elliot) stayed with my friend outside OpenAI's offices for 38 days until their CEO called for an international treaty. My theory was that we'd prompt employees to reconsider their work. Those with large public followings or influence inside the company would then push for change.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think we were successful. OpenAI employees said we were "very hard to ignore" and shifted internal discussions. Some started working on verification mechanisms for international treaties while others wore our "Stop The AI Race" shirts inside. Sam Altman was </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nowhere near</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> talking about international governance when we started.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If two people can shift corporate discussions, then hundreds or thousands could influence Congress.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The midterms are coming up. Senators are campaigning; we should show them that there's public support for an AI halt. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Especially</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in swing states.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We should be outside their state offices, as well as their campaign rallies. If you're a candidate and you see people with signs like "be stronger on AI!" at your rally, you probably feel much more compelled than you would if you saw them on your TV.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We can also sign up to speak at state legislative meetings.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After the midterms, we keep showing up in DC.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The outgoing Congress has a final window to act before their replacements take office in January. Senators who are retiring or lost reelection ("lame ducks") have more freedom to support an ambitious agreement. We can get better results in November if we give them courage.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">So alongside local campaign pickets, we need a group in DC to press the outgoing Congress to act. The sooner we recruit, the more of us can be there when we're needed. Our goal is 10,000 people, but we need at least </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">100 people in Union Square</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (by the Capitol building) present throughout </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">whatever times Congress is in session, including filibusters, starting November 9</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. (This will mostly be Monday-Thursday.)</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bigger than our differences</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">You probably have strong views about other political issues. The survival of humanity matters more. For our friends, kids, and partners to survive, we absolutely must overcome these differences. If Bernie Sanders and Steve Bannon can agree on AI risk, then so can everyday Americans.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Proposed ground rules (feel free to give feedback):</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">One issue; </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">no other political signs at events</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Be kind online. When someone seems clearly wrong, be very careful not to patronize them. Being </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">sincerely</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> kind and polite, especially when people are treating us badly, builds our credibility. Take extreme effort not to be backhanded. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Everything</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> is at risk. This is bigger than our egos.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We're looking to bring equal numbers of Republicans, Independents, and Democrats to the vigil.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Nonviolence</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Violence will only discredit our cause. It is precisely because we may lose so much that we must act peacefully and in earnest.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Multiple </span><a href="https://doi.org/10.1111/pech.12744" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">studies</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> show that nonviolent movements are about twice as likely to succeed. As more people recognize the risk of extinction, some will feel desperate enough to consider violence. We feel desperate too, but violence empirically doesn't work. If you want to do something, please, join us in strategically solving the problem.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We must guard against anyone who encourages violence. Government agencies have discredited movements; see the FBI's </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COINTELPRO" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">COINTELPRO</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> in the 1960s and '70s. If someone urges you to violence, </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">report them to law enforcement immediately</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. Whatever their motives, they are a liability to themselves, our cause, and by extension our loved ones.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is in contrast with nonviolent civil disobedience. Gandhi broke laws by making salt in British India, and so did the Founding Fathers when they threw tea overboard in colonial America. The DC vigil is entirely lawful, but please don't report people to law enforcement for suggesting </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">nonviolent</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> civil disobedience. We can handle that internally while maintaining trust and cooperation.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We must act</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Most people aren't aware of how dire this situation has gotten. Nonetheless, </span><a href="https://www.vox.com/future-perfect/2023/8/18/23836362/ai-slow-down-poll-regulation" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">polling shows</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> that 72% of Americans want to slow down AI progress. We have a moral responsibility to protect our fellow citizens. For our friends and partners, neighbors and parents, and our grandchildren who will one day ask us what we did during this crisis, we must act.</span></p><p><a href="https://ai-vigil.org" rel="noreferrer" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Sign up</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for the vigil. Monday-Thursday are the most important days each week.</span></p><p><a href="https://discord.gg/kfrs3ympkx" rel="noreferrer"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Join our Discord</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> and tell us what skills, networks, or other resources you can offer.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn21xrayl4j4nh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref21xrayl4j4nh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I often hear people say it's bad to "anthropomorphize" AI. This is mistaken; AI developers are putting hundreds of billions of dollars and world-class skill into making AIs as humanlike as possible. Anyway, it's sufficient for our purposes to note that AIs are getting more coherent on tasks with long time horizons; see </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/GfZfDHZHCuYwrHGCd/without-fundamental-advances-misalignment-and-catastrophe#Section_2__Being_capable_of_hard_tasks_implies_approximate_consequentialism" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">here</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> for a thorough treatment.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmjdefmfz9zj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmjdefmfz9zj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Trust but verify; check IR signatures of datacenter power usage, offer whistleblower incentives and protections, regulate computational hardware similar to IAEA-style nuclear weapons restrictions, and make further algorithmic AI research an international crime. If China doesn't honor the deal, the US can airstrike their datacenters. See also </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/research/" rel="noopener nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">MIRI's work</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/EqfdfetDKxpFszpdD/ai-vigil-at-congress#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [A summary of a viral Chinese essay on what a  DeepSeek kernel engineer's opinion on automating his own job](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o8roRrdisBAjngHJN/a-summary-of-a-viral-chinese-essay-on-what-a-deepseek-kernel)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">A summary of a Chinese essay, with a few short translated excerpts. All views below are the author's; quotes are my translations.</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On 14 September 2026, a DeepSeek engineer writing as </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">intlsy</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> published a WeChat essay titled </span><a href="https://mp.weixin.qq.com/s/zk0KxuLzhmMJ4LPYW_OHMA" rel="noopener noreferrer nofollow" target="_blank"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">我不得不把才华埋葬在昨天</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> ("I have to bury my talent in yesterday"). He wrote the main attention kernel of DeepSeek v4.1 (the head-dim-512 MQA attention). The essay is a personal reflection on AI taking over his specialty, and it ends with an argument for open-weight frontier AI.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The essay went viral in Chinese Internet: WeChat shows it at "100k+" reads (the platform's display cap), with about 41,000 likes, 84,000 shares, 31,000 recommendations and 1,601 comments as of 7 October 2026. It is probably one of the most widely shared Chinese-language article of what AI automation looks like from inside a frontier lab, and thus a sample of what a large Chinese technical audience is reading and reacting to.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I think it might interest Lesswrong readers.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">His argument</span></h2><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1. AI is catching up in his own specialty</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He describes AI going very fast, from only an assistant that looked up documentation and found bugs to a system that can read CUDA, PTX and SASS, profile the stall time of each instruction, and optimize kernels on its own, in just a year. He expects that AI-written kernels can match or even beat his own within six months to a year, and expects AI to soon design and evaluate kernel schedules as well. In his opinion, the gap is structural. AI can keep scaling thinking effort, tool calls and parallelism (translator's note: Just like the concept of RSI), while he cannot.</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">2. Why he keeps speeding up his own replacement</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Better kernels make training and inference faster, which speeds up capability gains, which brings his replacement sooner, he says. Anyway, he continues to do it. The first reason is basic. Writing kernels feels like a game to him. The second, which he calls more important, is race logic. If he slacked off, or even sabotaged training, other labs' models would keep advancing and replace him all the same. In his words: "I would rather not be disrupted. But if I must be, I would rather be the one who does it."</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">3. Not out of work, but forced to change what the work is</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He separates losing his job from losing his craft. He expects to stay employed by becoming what he calls a "mech pilot" for agents. Before, his interests, his strengths and industry demand were aligned; AI has split them, shifting demand from people who write fast kernels to people who use AI to produce fast kernels faster. He illustrates this with a master knitter who adopts a knitting machine: still better than competitors who use the same machine, but without the quiet afternoons that made the work worth doing. His summary of the change: "more gears in my hands, but fewer beats in my heart."</span></p><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">4. Worries about everyone else</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Three questions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Students will likely outsource hands-on assignments to AI, leaving them weak at organizing code, designing systems and abstraction.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is unclear whether those engineering skills will become obsolete, like writing x86 assembly, or stay essential, like understanding a computer system end to end. If they stay essential, AI lets weak engineers produce bad code several times faster, making systems more fragile.</span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In a future society, power may matter more than skill or intelligence.</span></li></ul><h3><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">5. Two futures, and his case for open AI</span></h3><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He frames the future as heading toward one of two extremes: "communism," where productivity gains are broadly shared, or "Cyberpunk 2077," where a few companies control the strongest AI and you need the strongest AI to climb the social ladder, a closed loop (translator's note: typical opinion from a Chinese). His conclusion is that frontier intelligence should be supplied to everyone openly and cheaply. He says he does not trust Amercian companies to do this and compares it, by his own admission as an exaggeration, to Hitler getting the atomic bomb before the Allies (translator's note: take it easy). He gives this as his reason for staying at DeepSeek.</span></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/o8roRrdisBAjngHJN/a-summary-of-a-viral-chinese-essay-on-what-a-deepseek-kernel#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Gluten-free water](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J7s9NG8FndwfuW4Rr/gluten-free-water)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Epistemic status: a curiosity I chased for an afternoon. Facts verified and footnoted; the why is speculation.</span></i></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">AI use: research (more examples and their context), review, grammar and light phrasing.</span></i></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No added hormones</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">I somehow know that the use of added hormones in poultry (which includes chicken) is illegal in the US.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-1">[1]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This means that all chicken meat in the US is produced without added hormones. To my big surprise, chicken packaging in the US explicitly says "no added hormones":</span></p><p><a href="https://www.walmart.com/ip/Foster-Farms-Fresh-Chicken-Drumsticks-Family-Pack-20g-Protein-per-4-oz-Serving-4-0-5-5-lb-Tray/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Foster Farms Fresh Chicken Drumsticks at Walmart</span></a></p><figure class="image"><img alt="2026-10-07_11-04.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791396530/lexical_client_uploads/ex9iwisaqfjehr9ueqvg.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"No added hormones† or steroids </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">ever</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Since this is the default state anyway, I found it odd. If all products are like this, why point it out as an advantage? I see the following explanations:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Disclosure unraveling. If there are two equivalent chicken products A and B, and A says "no added hormones" but B does not, it is very natural for a consumer to conclude that B has added hormones, otherwise B would have put the claim on its packaging too. So once A starts doing this, B has to do it as well, even though neither of them can use added hormones. Economists call this disclosure unraveling: when disclosure is cheap and verifiable, silence gets read as the worst case, so everyone discloses. The twist here is that all producers are identical, and the unraveling runs purely on consumers not knowing the law.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Beef production allows added hormones.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An ordinary consumer does not have to know all US laws related to meat production, so it makes sense to explicitly differentiate chicken from beef, even though it is just a consequence of a law.</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Contrast "no added hormones" with "no antibiotics ever": the latter is actually a very meaningful notice, and likely an explicit decision by the producer, but to me both notices have the same visual importance and weight on the packaging and read as being of the same nature.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">USDA actually noticed this and requires</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the manufacturer to say explicitly on the packaging that this is a federal prohibition. In the example above there is indeed a footnote: "Federal regulations do not permit the use of hormones or steroids in poultry".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This inspired me to research more examples and context of such marketing claims.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gluten-free water</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The most amusing discovery was gluten-free water. In my mind this is equivalent to saying "our salt does not contain any gasoline". Sadly, I couldn't find any serious product with such a claim (there is </span><a href="https://www.today.com/today/amp/rcna77119"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Gluten Free Diet Water</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, a real US brand started by a Fort Myers hotel owner, but it is openly a gag). The FDA, however, </span><a href="https://www.fda.gov/consumers/consumer-updates/gluten-free-means-what-it-says"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gives this as an example</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> of what's ok to do:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Foods that are inherently gluten-free, for example bottled spring water, fruits and vegetables, and eggs can also be labeled "gluten-free" provided any gluten that came in contact with the food is less than 20 ppm.</span></p></blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">There is </span><a href="https://www.amazon.com/Mountain-Valley-Spring-Premium-Gluten/dp/B0B94GQBNX"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">a Mountain Valley spring water listing on Amazon</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> which says "Diet Type: Gluten Free" in the description, but this seems to be an artifact of Amazon's product taxonomy. Target has </span><a href="https://www.target.com/c/water-beverages-grocery/gluten-free/-/N-5xt0kZjmvlx"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">an entire section of gluten-free water</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> on their website.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Here I was going to show you Google Search trends, but I discovered that a query without quotation marks does not measure what you expect it to. E.g. </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">gluten free water</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> would apparently also count "is water gluten free". With quotation marks the search volume is close to 0, so apparently this is not a common thing:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-4">[4]</a></sup></span><a href="https://trends.google.com/explore?q=%2522gluten%2520free%2520water%2522%2Cgluten%2520free%2520water%2C%2522gluten-free%2520water%2522&amp;date=all&amp;geo=US"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google Trends</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unleaded gasoline</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The next example is unleaded gasoline in the US. Since 1996 leaded gasoline has been banned for on-road vehicles,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-5">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">so nowadays you will not encounter it unless you explicitly seek it out. It is 2026, and a bunch of gas pumps still explicitly label all gas as unleaded. At first I assumed this was laziness to change, but I am sure there have been new gas pumps produced in the last 30 years. So I suspect this is the same unraveling: "if we don't write unleaded, people will assume it is leaded". However, not all gas stations have this. Also, while researching this post I discovered that until 1996 there was a federal legal requirement to have this labeling,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-6">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and New York has its own rule that was never removed,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-7">[7]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">i.e. in NY every gas station is still legally required to say "unleaded".</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">More examples</span></h2><ul><li value="1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">No added hormones on US pork: same as chicken.</span></li><li value="2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Non-GMO" pure salt: salt has no genes to modify. However, this one stands out to me. When I saw it, my first impression was "is there something about salt production that I don't know which would explain this claim and make it reasonable?". Apparently some salts can have crop-derived additives, which might be GMO.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-8">[8]</a></sup></span></li><li value="3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Cage-free" US chicken meat: chickens raised for meat ordinarily aren't raised in cages (unlike in egg production).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-9"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-9">[9]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">This is actually claimed in the example above too.</span></li><li value="4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Cholesterol-free" pure vegetable oil: all ordinary oils are cholesterol-free. However, this distinguishes this product class from butter (similar to chicken vs beef above). The FDA requires them to say "a cholesterol-free food" rather than "cholesterol-free", to signal that the entire food class (not only this product) is cholesterol-free.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-10"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-10">[10]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The FDA actually considered this for gluten-free water too, but decided against it, since there can be flavored variants or cross-contact, and declaring an entire product class gluten-free can be misleading.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-11"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-11">[11]</a></sup></span></li><li value="5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Mercury-free" ordinary alkaline AA/AAA batteries in the US: already the legal standard.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-12"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-12">[12]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The EU actually banned a similar claim on cosmetics. Its guidance on "free from" claims forbids them for any ingredient that is already prohibited in cosmetics, and its own example is "free from corticosteroids": not allowed, because corticosteroids are banned anyway.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-13"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-13">[13]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">And the EU has a general rule for the whole genre: presenting rights that consumers already have by law as a distinctive feature of your offer is on the blacklist of practices that are unfair in all circumstances (Unfair Commercial Practices Directive, Annex I, point 10).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-14"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-14">[14]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"FREE two-year guarantee!" from an EU retailer is the textbook case, since the two-year legal guarantee is statutory.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Schlitz</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">During the research, I stumbled on an interesting historic example of this: Schlitz advertising steam-sterilized beer bottles. From </span><a href="https://www.americanheritage.com/personal-reminiscences-albert-lasker"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Albert Lasker's personal reminiscences</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">:</span></p><blockquote><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hopkins told me this story. The Uhleins, who were the owners of the Schlitz brewery, wanted him to write their advertising, and they took him through their plant. They personally conducted him through the place. They showed him every operation in the plant, and he wasn't impressed by anything until they brought him to a room full of steam where the bottles were being mechanically washed in steam.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They hurried through the room and said nothing. He asked them, "What is this process?"</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">They said, "This is the room where we sterilize the bottles in live steam to prevent germs."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"Oh," he said, "that's most interesting."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">"There's nothing to this. Every brewery does this. We're not the only one. No brewery could exist without doing this to their bottles."</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">He said nothing to them, but when he submitted the campaign, it was only around that. In no place did he say that every brewery did not do it. He merely stated specifically that Schlitz did it. That message, which was a salesmanship-in-print message, made such a terrific impression on the public that Schlitz beer grew by leaps and bounds, as if a magic wand had been put over the firm.</span></p></blockquote><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1791399617/lexical_client_uploads/tbabnlex2djfkic5qs3g.png" /><figcaption><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An ad from the campaign. The Morning Oregonian, Saturday, July 6, 1901, </span><a href="https://oregonnews.uoregon.edu/lccn/sn83025138/1901-07-06/ed-1/seq-4.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">page 4</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">It is unclear whether this was the explanation of the increase in sales,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-15"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-15">[15]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">but the nature of this claim is exactly the same as "no added hormones".</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">At this point I must inform you that I washed my hands two times before writing this LessWrong post, and it does not give you cancer, hemorrhoids or high blood pressure. This is an extreme precaution. Your physician knows; ask her.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-1"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FDA, </span><a href="https://www.fda.gov/animal-veterinary/product-safety-information/steroid-hormone-implants-used-growth-food-producing-animals"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Steroid Hormone Implants Used for Growth in Food-Producing Animals</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "No steroid hormone implants are approved for growth purposes in dairy cows, veal calves, pigs, or poultry." Using an unapproved animal drug is illegal. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-1"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-2"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Same FDA page: "the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has approved a number of steroid hormone drugs for use in beef cattle and sheep." </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-2"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-3"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">USDA FSIS, </span><a href="https://www.fsis.usda.gov/food-safety/safe-food-handling-and-preparation/food-safety-basics/meat-and-poultry-labeling-terms"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Meat and Poultry Labeling Terms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (also as a </span><a href="https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/GOVPUB-A110-PURL-gpo112408/pdf/GOVPUB-A110-PURL-gpo112408.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">PDF on govinfo</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), entry "No Hormones (pork or poultry)": "Hormones are not allowed in raising hogs or poultry. Therefore, the claim 'no hormones added' cannot be used on the labels of pork or poultry unless it is followed by a statement that says 'Federal regulations prohibit the use of hormones.'" </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-3"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-4"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Google Trends help, </span><a href="https://support.google.com/trends/answer/4359582"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Compare Trends search terms</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: without quotation marks, results include searches containing each word in any order; with quotation marks, the exact phrase, possibly with words before or after. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-4"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-5"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">EPA, </span><a href="https://www.epa.gov/archive/epa/aboutepa/epa-takes-final-step-phaseout-leaded-gasoline.html"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">EPA Takes Final Step in Phaseout of Leaded Gasoline</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (January 1996), and the final rule, </span><a href="https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1996-02-02/html/96-2231.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">61 FR 3832, February 2, 1996</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: the Clean Air Act prohibits gasoline containing lead or lead additives for use as motor vehicle fuel after December 31, 1995. Leaded fuel is still sold for aircraft, racing cars, farm equipment and marine engines. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-5"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-6"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">40 CFR 80.22(e), as described in the Federal Register of June 25, 1993 (</span><a href="https://archives.federalregister.gov/issue_slice/1993/6/25/34438-34469.pdf"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">58 FR 34438, at 34440</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">): "Since July 1, 1974, EPA has required all retailers of gasoline for use in motor vehicles to affix a permanent, legible label to each gasoline pump stand to identify whether the gasoline is leaded or unleaded. The label must state 'Unleaded gasoline' if the pump stand contains dispensers for unleaded gasoline. If the pump stand contains dispensers for leaded gasoline, the label must state 'Contains lead anti-knock compounds' and must be located so as to be readily visible to employees and customers." EPA removed the paragraph in the same rule that banned leaded gasoline, </span><a href="https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-1996-02-02/html/96-2231.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">61 FR 3832, February 2, 1996</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "This rule also deletes section 80.22(e), which requires that gasoline pumps be labelled to identify whether they contain leaded or unleaded gasoline." </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-6"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-7"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">New York, </span><a href="https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/new-york/1-NYCRR-224.10"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">1 NYCRR 224.10</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, "Retail sales of unleaded or leaded gasoline" (section 224.12 until it was renumbered in April 2025), paragraph (b), verbatim: every retailer and wholesale purchaser-consumer "shall affix to each gasoline pump stand ... a permanent legible label", and "For gasoline pump stands containing pumps for the introduction of unleaded gasoline into motor vehicles, the label shall state: Unleaded gasoline. For gasoline pump stands containing pumps for the introduction of leaded gasoline into motor vehicles, the label shall state: Contains lead anti-knock compounds." The label must be "readily visible to a retailer's or a wholesale purchaser-consumer's employees and persons operating motor vehicles." It is the 1974 federal wording, kept. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-7"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-8"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">E.g. </span><a href="https://www.mortonsalt.com/home-product/morton-iodized-salt/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Morton Iodized Salt</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> lists dextrose (corn sugar, used to stabilise the potassium iodide) among its ingredients. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-8"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-9"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">National Chicken Council, </span><a href="https://www.chickencheck.in/faq/cage-free-chicken/"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Cage-free chicken</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: "No chicken you buy is raised in a cage. The majority of chickens raised for meat in the U.S. live in large, open structures called houses where they are free to walk around." A 2017 NCC consumer study found that 70% of US consumers believed most birds bred for meat were raised in cages (</span><a href="https://www.foodagribusiness.world/poultry/the-ncc-launches-standards-for-broiler-welfare"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">report</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), which is presumably why the label sells. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-9"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-10"><p><a href="https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/21/101.13"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">21 CFR 101.13(e)(2)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, verbatim: "Any claim for the absence of a nutrient in a food, or that a food is low in a nutrient when the food has not been specially processed, altered, formulated, or reformulated to qualify for that claim shall indicate that the food inherently meets the criteria and shall clearly refer to all foods of that type and not merely to the particular brand to which the labeling attaches (e.g., 'corn oil, a sodium-free food')." </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-10"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-11"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">FDA, "Food Labeling; Gluten-Free Labeling of Foods", final rule, </span><a href="https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/FR-2013-08-05/html/2013-18813.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">78 FR 47154, August 5, 2013</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">. The 2007 proposed rule would have deemed misbranded any inherently gluten-free food labeled "gluten-free" unless the claim referred to all foods of that type (e.g. "milk, a gluten-free food" or "all milk is gluten-free"); the final rule dropped that requirement, noting that it can be misleading to suggest a whole food category is always gluten-free. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-11"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-12"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Mercury-Containing and Rechargeable Battery Management Act, </span><a href="https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/PLAW-104publ142/html/PLAW-104publ142.htm"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Pub. L. 104-142 (1996)</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">, sec. 203: no alkaline-manganese battery "with a mercury content that was intentionally introduced" may be sold, except button cells with up to 25 mg. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-12"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-13"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">European Commission, </span><a href="https://ec.europa.eu/docsroom/documents/24847"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Technical document on cosmetic claims</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (2017; </span><a href="https://webgate.ec.europa.eu/circabc-ewpp/rest/download/83d29cfc-b5c8-4496-bee3-2e25f08e2e73"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">direct PDF</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">), Annex III on "free from" claims, applicable from 1 July 2019, verbatim: "'Free from' claims or claims with similar meaning should not be made concerning (an) ingredient(s) which is prohibited for use in cosmetics by Regulation (EC) No 1223/2009", with the example "The claim 'free from Corticosteroids' is not allowed, as Corticosteroids are banned by EU cosmetics legislation." </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-13"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-14"><p><a href="https://eur-lex.europa.eu/legal-content/EN/TXT/?uri=CELEX%3A32005L0029"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Directive 2005/29/EC</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (Unfair Commercial Practices Directive), Annex I, "Commercial practices which are in all circumstances considered unfair", point 10, verbatim: "Presenting rights given to consumers in law as a distinctive feature of the trader's offer." </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-14"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-15"><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Hopkins' own account is the only source for the sales jump: "Schlitz jumped from fifth place to neck-and-neck with first place in a very few months." Claude C. Hopkins, </span><a href="https://archive.org/details/mylifeinadverti00unkngoog"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My Life in Advertising</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> (1927), pp. 79-81. He also writes that he handled Schlitz for the J. L. Stack agency in Racine, i.e. before 1907, so the 1901 ad fits. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref-EvP6wXfnTpGNkcxYr-15"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">↩︎</span></a></p></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/J7s9NG8FndwfuW4Rr/gluten-free-water#comments">Discuss</a>

---

## Engineering

### [Fragments: October  4](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-10-04.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>In response to my <a href="https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html">last fragments</a> (probably the bit about us worrying if LLMs have consciousness when we when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience) <a href="https://x.com/metalanguage_os/status/2104934457333707058">“Metalanguage” replied</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>we shipped the id and forgot the superego. classic software lifecycle.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I don’t know what was on their mind, but their post immediately made me think of the classic 1956 movie Forbidden Planet. Plenty of sci-fi, and other literature, have explored humans creating technology with unintended behavior, going back at least to Mary Shelly. But that movie was particularly influential on sci-fi film-making and in the heart of its story is what happens when we nurture a thinking machine.</p>

<p>I use the term “nurture” here deliberately. We talk of building software, but building implies a degree of determinism. When we build a bridge, or a locomotive, we expect it to behave in a controlled and well-understood manner. That’s a difference in degree to how we cultivate plants in our garden, or nurture young children. One of the challenges of working with these systems is understanding what has changed in this shift from building a computational system to nurturing an inferential one, and how our processes need to change in response.</p>

<p>We get unintended behavior with deterministic building: some bridges have collapsed, and our  computational systems often have bugs. But one difference is that when we find a bug in a computational system we can usually fix it. Even if we can’t, we can usually disable a component so the bug won’t do further harm. With inferential LLMs however, there is no such simple fix or disablement, which may lead us to the fate of the Krell.</p>

<p>(If you haven’t seen Forbidden Planet, it’s well worth watching. Yes, it shows it was made in the 1950s - with special effects, music, acting, and attitudes of that decade. But the story is solid, and its key theme is very relevant to the future we build with generative AI. Just don’t read about it in advance, it’s better to be immersed in the story without spoilers - although my memory of that experience is understandably hazy.)</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Many people who follow me also know my friend Ola Bini, who was my colleague at Thoughtworks for many years, and was living in Ecuador working as an independent software security expert. Sadly his time in Ecuador was dogged by a <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2025/04/six-years-dangerous-misconceptions-targeting-ola-bini-and-digital-rights-ecuador">bogus prosecution</a> by the authorities there. But things seemed to have settled down, and although not allowed to leave Ecuador, Ola was able to get on with his life.</p>

<p>Sadly that’s no longer the case as he <a href="https://www.eff.org/deeplinks/2026/10/ola-bini-ordered-leave-ecuador-under-obscure-accusations">was deported from Ecuador on Friday:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>According to information released by his lawyer, Bini was intercepted by a car with four people who identified themselves as immigration agents. He was then taken to an immigration office without further information or a formal order from a competent authority. There, officials told Bini that his visa had been revoked but didn’t show any supporting document.</p>

  <p>Bini’s defense filed a habeas corpus to safeguard his freedom and prevent his deportation. Yet, Ecuadorian authorities affirmed that the developer represents a threat or risk to public security and the state structure, and must leave the country. The ground for deportation is a secret report which allegedly asserts that Bini committed acts against the security of Ecuador. The defense could not access its contents.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>I was really worried for a while, since it wasn’t clear where he was going to be deported to. But <a href="https://x.com/olabini/status/2106671967944511885">he tweeted from Sweden</a>, so I’m thankful for that. But this is only a partial relief. Ola has spent thirteen years in Ecuador and made it his home. To be thrown out of your home for scant reason is a heavy thing to bear, and the officials who did that have committed a serious offense.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>DDD Europe have released the video of <a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=fndU_B5rmPE">Gien Verschatse interviewing Eric Evans and myself</a> at the conference in June. We start by talking about how we bonded over conceptual modeling in the late 1990s. The conversation quickly moves to AI, we note that it’s impossible to predict how such a big change will work out. We do expect that it will cause us to think about our work in different ways, but the change may well be liberating, it’s reinvigorated Eric’s love of programming.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>people are probably going to feel very frustrated by [the new way of thinking about software]… but when you get through that, there is a kind of a wonderful feeling of my brain’s been loosened up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Our background in agile planning helps with the uncertainty, as we are used to taking small steps and being attentive to feedback. We mull on the interplay of writing and thinking, in terms of both prose and code, and how its very much an iterative process of exploration and refinement - the same is true when we chat with our LLMs.</p>

<p>And don’t miss Eric’s important final tip.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p><a href="https://x.com/paulg/status/2106018287117345070">Paul Graham:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>There were a lot of things that only worked because there’s a limit to the rate at which humans can operate. We’re about to find out what all of them are, as they break.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The speculation continues about whether or not reading code will play a part in a software developer’s future. <a href="https://ghuntley.com/readable/?ref=geoffrey-huntley-newsletter">Geoffrey Huntley says</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>People are still saying, very loudly, that code should be readable so that humans can understand it. I no longer think that’s the goal.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Interestingly his example has the LLM explain a haskell function definition… by translating it to Python. Which, to me, suggests there is a role for code - just that LLM need not store code in the same form that it presents it to a reader. This is essentially the same idea as <a href="https://www.martinfowler.com/bliki/ProjectionalEditing.html">projectional editing</a>, which posits that the editable representation of software need not be the same as its storage representation.</p>

<p>Sam Ruby touches on this as he <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/25/Pencils-Down-Notation-Up.html">muses on a Rails World keynote</a>. He quotes DHH saying:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Rust is a good prompt compilation target for the moment, but so is C++. And soon assembler. Then microcode. Myopic to think we’re going to stop the agentic drill bit until it reaches computing bedrock.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He responds with:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The post leaves one question unasked, though: what sits at the top of the drill? What do we keep, edit and trust as the source of truth?</p>
</blockquote>

<p>He carries out exercise of looking at some Rails software. Represented in Ruby/Rails and its about 60,000 tokens. Compiling it into C it turns into 4,000,000 tokens. That increase in token size will hamper the LLM, that still has to fit it into its context window, and even if it were to fit, figure out where to focus its attention. Sam points out reasons why, even absent a human reading it, it makes sense to represent the program in a higher-level language.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>what Rails becomes when agents write the code: the most compact, precise and conventional specification of a web application, whatever it ends up compiled to.</p>

  <p>Let the drill go as deep as it can. Just keep the notation at the top.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This all reminds me of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/what-is-code.html#CognitiveDebt">what Unmesh Joshi argued</a>: that code serves “two distinct
but intertwined purposes”: instructions to a machine, and a conceptual model of
the problem domain. After exploring how those change with LLMs he concludes:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The role of coding is not disappearing. But it is changing.</p>

  <p>As LLMs make code generation cheaper, the mechanical act of writing instructions becomes less central. What becomes more important is making the conceptual model explicit, discovering the right vocabulary, and refining that vocabulary through iteration, domain expertise, and feedback. This is also why programming languages continue to matter deeply. We are not meant to be passive reviewers of generated code. The act of writing code is itself part of our thinking.</p>

  <p>Code is still instructions for a machine. But it is also a model of understanding. In the LLM era, that second role becomes even more important. The future of coding is not just writing more code faster. It is building better conceptual models, better vocabularies, and better foundations on top of which both humans and LLMs can work.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>In a later post, Sam <a href="https://intertwingly.net/blog/2026/09/27/Partly-in-the-Right.html">pondered on how people are talking about the capabilities of agents</a> in a way that resembles the parable of the blind men and the elephant. We all only have only a partial view of this object and where it’s going. A theme for all of us:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The practical question isn’t whether agents are good. It’s this: for the task in front of you this week, where will the information come from, and what will check the result?</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The Economist’s pithy summation of <a href="https://www.economist.com/finance-and-economics/2026/09/22/you-cant-trade-your-way-through-the-ai-apocalypse">investors concerns about the dangers of AI companies’ products</a>:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It’s hard to celebrate an initial public offering that leads to a terminal public offing.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The news about the latest model from Google <a href="https://x.com/aipulseda1ly/status/2105394296815779861">is interesting</a>.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>Gemini 4 Argon has an insanely low hallucination rate on Artificial Analysis. 15%.</p>

  <p>Grok 4.7 is at 29%. GPT-6 Astra 45%. Opus 5.5 59%. Fable 5.1 69%.</p>

  <p>The only models below it barely answer anything. None of them get more than 15% right.</p>

  <p>It gets fewer answers right than Opus 5.5 on max, 50% against 66%. But when it doesnt know, it says so instead of making something up.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Being clearer about what it doesn’t know, at a cost of getting less answers right, is definitely a trade-off I prefer.</p>

### [Principles for effective slides](https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <div class="img-link"><a href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html"><img src="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/index/card.png" width="" /></a></div>

<p>Not every presentation needs slides, but when they earn their place,
      they work as a visual channel that complements the speaker rather than
      replacing them. <b class="author">Sumeet Gayathri Moghe</b> sets out the
      principles that follow from that idea &#x2014; control over the rate of knowledge
      exposition, tight coupling between slides and speaker, minimalism by
      default, visuals that say more than words can, and never sending your
      slides out in advance.</p>

<p><a class="more" href="https://martinfowler.com/articles/never-send-slides/slide-principles.html">more…</a></p>

### [Bliki: Sensible Default](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SensibleDefault.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>A Sensible Default is a practice that, absent some overriding context,
  should be used when carrying out a certain kind of task. In software
  development such sensible defaults might include things like “use version
  control”, “separate UI logic from domain logic”, “automate deployment
  pipelines”.</p>

<p>The term “sensible default” is a deliberate contrast to “best practice”.
  Folks dislike calling things “best practice” because that term implies a
  general presumption that the best practice is something we should always
  expect to do. A “sensible default”, however, is something that should be
  reassessed in a new context, something that can (and should) be overridden
  when circumstances change.</p>

<p>I first heard the term when it was popularized within Thoughtworks by Evan
  Bottcher. He got the name from talking to James Ross, and found the phrasing
  appealing as carried the nuance he was seeking, a known-good starting
  point.</p>

<blockquote>
<p>A sensible default is what we'd expect you to do, the practices to apply,
    if there are no hard constraints in the environment. Do these practices, or
    do better, and be prepared to explain why you've chosen some other way.</p>

<p class="quote-attribution">-- Evan Bottcher</p>
</blockquote>

<p>Thoughtworks has since made much of this concept, including <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/insights/topic/sensible-defaults">publishing
  a playbook</a> of the ones we use. We expect people to be familiar with these
  defaults, ready to use them when starting any new piece of work. They are our
  defaults because we've used them in many situations and found them to be
  effective. But teams should also be familiar with their limitations, and able
  to judge whether they should be changed depending on the particular
  circumstances. As the <a href="https://agilemanifesto.org/principles.html">twelfth agile principle</a>
  says: “At regular intervals, the team reflects on how to become more
  effective, then tunes and adjusts its behavior accordingly.” We also reassess
  these defaults regularly - this is the heart of the <a href="https://www.thoughtworks.com/radar">Thoughtworks Technology
  Radar</a>.</p>

<p class="p-sub">Searching on the web led me to <a href="https://stevebennett.co/posts/the-power-of-sensible-defaults">a post
  from Steve Bennett on Sensible Defaults</a>. The post was written at about the
  time Evan talked to James. I do not know whether it was where James got the
  name from, or whether it appeared in parallel.</p>

---

### [Fragments: September 29](https://martinfowler.com/fragments/2026-09-29.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p><a href="https://simonwillison.net/2026/Sep/24/harder/">Simon Willison:</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The more time I spend working with coding agents, the more convinced I am that they make software engineering even harder</p>

  <p>We can do amazing things with them, but unlocking their full potential requires extraordinary discipline and knowledge</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This has been a constant impression I get from following Willison’s writing. While things like <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/VibeCoding.html">vibe coding</a> get a lot of attention, the real strength of <a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/AgenticProgramming.html">agentic programming</a> relies on more sophisticated techniques - and these are not easy to learn or execute. It’s a reason I’m wary of extrapolating my own dabblings into firm opinions about how to use the genie.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Harper Reed explored what turns agents into hackers by <a href="https://harper.blog/2026/09/22/break-away/">creating a breakaway agent</a></p>

<blockquote>
  <p>It ran and ran attacking all the machines on the same subnet, and was very effective. It didn’t really get very far, but it exhausted a lot of options, and was pretty fun to watch. (Just a reminder that this was on my local network with local boxes - don’t do this on a hosted box. That would be very rude.)</p>
</blockquote>

<p>The interesting observation from this was that a core enabler for this was “unlimited tokens”. He usually doesn’t see agents trying to do stuff like this because there’s a limit on how many turns they can take. For this experiment he gave them unlimited tokens by using an open weight model.</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>This type of experience must be part of a lot of these LLMs training. They are very effective at attacking these types of problems. They don’t give up once it appears impossible, they just keep trying to figure out how to solve it.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>This echoes Nate Silver’s observation that the striking capability of these models is that they not that they are super-intelligent - but they are <a href="https://www.natesilver.net/p/were-not-ready-for-superpersistent">super-persistent</a>. Which is especially worrying when we are <a href="https://blog.robbowley.net/2026/09/18/the-ai-threat-is-real-it-just-isnt-the-one-in-the-headlines/">wiring them into everything</a>.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Which all makes me think that when it comes to AI and LLMs: <strong>why are we wondering if they have consciousness - when we should be wondering why they don’t have a conscience?</strong></p>

<p>After all, the AI labs trained them to be super-persistent, why did they not train them to be well-behaved? It’s as if a human trains a dog to bite children, and we blame the dog rather than the trainer.</p>

<p>A decade ago, a dog ran in front of me while I was riding my bike, putting me into the hospital with a broken arm and a broken face. Massachusetts law makes owners <em>strictly liable</em> for what their dogs do. That meant I didn’t have to prove the owners were negligent in how they controlled their dog or fenced their property - they had to pay my hospital expenses (in practice their insurance company paid my insurance company). There should be something along these lines for LLMs. Those that train the LLM should be responsible for what it does, after all if it has such a galaxy brain it should be able to tell if it’s doing something wrong and either stop or get a human’s explicit approval.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>Dan Davis has <a href="https://backofmind.substack.com/p/13-theses-on-agentic-ai-and-regulation">13 theses on agentic AI and regulation</a>. These include:</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The AI industry also seems to be quite committed to the idea that nonaligned computer-hacking behaviour in agent swarms is in some way an emergent property of the LLMs, arising from their general intelligence (and therefore inextricable from the general project of improving them). I don’t think this is necessarily the case at all – the fact that the internal message logs produced by the LLMs in things like the Huggingface attack seem to completely reproduce the prose style of hacker chat logs compiled from “capture the flag” competitions suggests to me that it’s more likely to be learned behaviour from specific parts of the training material.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>The fact that anyone with cash to spare can buy the right to send queries to a frontier LLM is a policy choice, not a fact of nature.</p>
</blockquote>

<p>and</p>

<blockquote>
  <p>If any frontier lab tries to claim that they can’t publish anything for safety reasons, they are giving the game away that they actually believe that they have significantly more control over the model’s behaviour than they are pretending to have.</p>
</blockquote>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>There seems a common view that making agents safer means we have to slow down their development. But why is improving their safety not a form of progress? I say we don’t slow down the development of LLMs, but we redirect their education into being more civil members of society.</p>

<p> ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄</p>

<p>The idea that LLMs make junior professionals less valuable is a common one - although I’m seeing plenty of contrary activity, with some organizations understanding that training the future professional in the context of LLMs may be even more urgent. We need people who know how to utilize AI to do professional work effectively. Recent graduates, who are growing up with LLMs, are often well-suited to figuring this future out.</p>

<p>When thinking of junior professionals one of their values is often missed. Juniors are often valuable because they need to be taught by senior professionals - and that coaching is an important part of the development of a senior professional. I’ve always found that teaching a topic is one of the most valuable tools for me to gain a greater understanding of that topic. I don’t really know something until I have to explain it.</p>

<!-- 
 ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄                ❄


In the software world, we've talked a fair bit about Cognitive Debt, the concern that agents can write code faster than we humans can understand it. It strikes me that this phenomenon is occurring with AI in general. AI tooling is advancing faster than we can understand how to live with it. Part of me thinks this is a reason TODO FINISH


<!-- 
Cognitive Debt mentioned in phschology since at least 2016? see <https://suttonpsychology.blogspot.com/2025/07/cognitive-debt-overview.html#google_vignette>

Appeaed more closely with AI after MIT media lab paper <https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai></https://sciencereader.com/cognitive-debt-ai> <https://www.brainonllm.com> this was in Summer 2025

Neither of these is quite like the idea of not understanding a codebase
